# Feeding Difficulties in the Baby and Younger Child

Michelle L.N. Tan • Denise L.M. Goh • Marion M. Aw

# DEFINITION OF FEEDING DIFFICULTY

• Feeding difficulty refers to

  ◦ Any problem that negatively affects the process through which parents/caregivers provide food to their infant/child.
  ◦ Any problem that negatively affects the process through which an individual accesses or consumes food.

• It includes:

  ◦ Picky eating: This is a loosely used term to describe features such as having a small appetite (eating small or perceived small quantities of food), being selective about the types of foods eaten, and fussy mealtime behaviors.
  ◦ Neophobia: Reluctance to try/eat new foods.
  ◦ Feeding disorder: Presence of medical issues causing the child to undereat/overeat/eat the wrong foods.

# SYMPTOMS OF FEEDING DIFFICULTY

The symptoms can include

• Small appetite
• Refusal to feed/food aversion
• Taking a long time to feed
• Narrow food spectrum eaten
• Swallowing difficulties/choking/gagging
• Discomfort or pain with feeding
• Vomiting
• Abdominal pain
• Noisy breathing
• Recurrent pneumonias
• Nutritional deficiencies
• Failure to thrive
• Overweight

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Organic Causes

• Condition that causes loss of appetite or food aversion

  ◦ Hypothyroidism
  ◦ Malignancy

283

- Autoimmune, inflammatory condition
- Renal failure
- Some types of inborn errors of metabolism (IEMs), e.g., protein aversion in urea cycle defect, organic acidemias
- Many chronic illnesses

- Condition that affects ability to swallow

- Abnormal craniofacial structures or esophagus, e.g., cleft lip/palate, Pierre-Robin sequence, choana atresia
- Swallowing dysfunction, e.g., cerebral palsy, muscular dystrophy, myopathy

- Condition that causes of respiratory distress, e.g., heart failure, lower respiratory tract diseases

- Condition that causes recurrent vomiting

- Gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD)
- Hiatal hernia
- Malrotation with intermittent volvulus
- Pyloric stenosis
- Raised intracranial pressure
- Some IEMs

- Condition that causes abdominal pain, abnormal transit, abnormal digestion/absorption (abnormality in gastrointestinal [GI] structure, motility, or function)

- GI tract malformation, e.g., tracheoesophageal fistula, hiatal hernia
- Gastroesophageal reflux (GER), GERD
- Gastritis
- Cow's milk protein allergy (CMPA)
- Inflammatory bowel disease
- Coeliac disease

- Condition that requires more than the usual calories for age

- Metabolic disorder, e.g., diabetes mellitus

- Condition that affects normal dietary principles, e.g., restricting certain nutrient(s)

- Liver failure diet, renal failure diet
- Some IEM diet
- Fluid restricted diet

## 2. Behavioral Causes

- Food fixation (selective, extreme dietary limitation)
- Sensory food aversions/anticipatory gagging
- Post-traumatic feeding disorder (following nasogastric tube feeding, or force feeding)

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

- Is there really a feeding difficulty?
- What is the cause?

- What is the consequence?
- Is there any red flag?

# 1. History Taking: Is There Really a Feeding Difficulty?

- Determine what the parents mean by feeding difficulty.
- The following symptoms are suggestive of feeding difficulty:

- Small/loss of appetite
- Refusal to feed
- Taking a long time to feed
- Narrow food spectrum/food aversion
- Swallowing difficulties/choking/gagging
- Discomfort or pain with feeding
- Vomiting
- Abdominal pain
- Noisy breathing
- Recurrent pneumonias
- Nutritional deficiencies
- Failure to thrive
- Overweight

# 2. History Taking: What Is the Cause? What Is the Consequence? Is There Any Red Flag?

- Information about serial height and head circumference measurements/growth.
- Information about serial weight, weight loss/gain, BMI – time course, minimum and maximum.
- Take more history about the signs and symptoms of feeding difficulty.

- Chronology of symptoms
- Associated symptoms (see Table 27-1 for possible causes based on the symptoms)
- Other illness
- Any perceived trigger or preceding event

- Take an antenatal history

- including in utero growth, intrauterine infection, teratogenic exposures such as alcohol/illicit drugs, maternal food intake, maternal health, maternal disease.

- Take a history of the child's diet and feeding history.

- Diet since birth
- Changes of milk formula
- Introduction of solids (when, what)
- Introduction of texture and food variety
- Current diet: quantity and type of food eaten, food aversions
- Meal times: length of meal times, mealtime routines, strategies used at meal times, and environment and behavior around mealtimes

- Determine if the current nutritional intake is adequate.

- Assess for consequences of malnutrition – e.g., pallor, fatigue, mood changes, syncope, hair loss, cold intolerance, sleep disturbances, amenorrhea.
- Assess hydration status.
- Systems review looking for organic causes of feeding difficulty (see Table 27-1 for possible causes based on the symptoms).
- Social history:

- Determine who the main caregiver is.
- Any stressor or emotional issues affecting the child, and the child's interaction with siblings/family/caregiver(s).

- Family history – feeding disorder in parents or siblings, family dynamics, and other relevant family stressors.

### 3. Physical Examination: What is the cause? What Is the Consequence? Are There Any red Flags?

- A very thorough head-to-toe examination is required to answer these questions: What is the cause? What is the consequence? Are there any red flags?
- Anthropometry – height, weight, and BMI on age-appropriate charts. Try to obtain serial data points and review the trends.
- Head-to-toe examination looking especially for

- Any dysmorphic feature/craniofacial abnormality
- Drooling
- Features of atopy
- Abnormal neurological examination
- Abnormal abdominal examination
- Any sign of systemic disease

- Assessment of developmental milestones

### 4. Observation of a Meal if Possible

- Interaction during feeding should be assessed where possible.
- Features of positive interaction:

- Presence of good eye contact
- Reciprocal vocalization
- Praise, touch

- Features of negative interaction:

- Forced feeding, bribery, threatening parenting style
- Disruptive behavior by the child, e.g., throwing of food, turning head away from food

5. Algorithm

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- <75% ideal body weight
- Severe dehydration
- Hemodynamic instability
- Respiratory distress
- Recurrent pneumonias
- Bilious or bloody vomitus

# CLINICAL SCENARIO

Janet is a 5-month-old infant. She is the first child of a nonconsanguineous union. Her mother has been concerned about Janet's feeding since she was 3-4 months of age. Janet was born at 37 weeks gestation with a birth weight of 3 kg (50th percentile). Her birth length was 49 cm (50th percentile). Other than neonatal jaundice requiring a day of phototherapy, she had no other significant medical issues.

Janet had been bottle-fed expressed breast milk and formula milk since birth. She had fed well in the first 2 months of life and had put on weight quickly. Subsequently, her mother felt that Janet appeared less interested in feeding, and despite being "older," the volume of milk that she was drinking each day was still about the same.

Janet's maternal grandmother has been helping to care for her since she was 3 months old. She was concerned that Janet was small and not putting on sufficient weight. She would patiently persuade Janet to complete all her milk feeds. Janet would get distressed and cry or vomit when this happened. Janet's mother noted that Janet has regurgitated or vomited easily since birth. However, as Janet had initially fed well, she had not been too concerned about it. However, with Janet's more recent crying and vomiting, her mother was wondering if there was an underlying medical problem.

On physical examination, Janet's current weight is 6.7 kg (50th percentile) and length is 66.5 cm (75th-90th percentile). Examination did not reveal any abnormality. Her growth chart is as below (see growth charts below)

In terms of social history, both parents are working full-time. Janet's current main caregiver is her grandmother.

This is the dietary history (see table below). After the calculation of her caloric and protein intake, Janet is receiving what she requires for her growth.

|  0700 | Full strength formula | 120 mL  |

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

What is the clinical problem?
What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

What is the clinical problem?

An infant who seems to have feeding difficulties

What is your diagnosis?

GER but not affecting growth
Caregiver anxiety that is possibly affecting the child

List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

Patient vomits easily, especially after feeds.
Vomiting occurs more often after being persuaded to complete a feed.
Growth chart shows that she is tracking along the percentile. In fact, she has crossed upwards.
No abnormality was detected during physical examination.
Grandmother is overly anxious, and Janet may have been force-fed on several occasions. This may contribute to feeding aversion.
Grandmother's anxiety is affecting the mother.

List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

No particular finding in this case goes against this diagnosis.

If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Other than getting distressed and crying during feeds, no other history provided about the presence/absence of choking, coughing, noisy breathing with feeding
No information about whether she seems to be in pain/arching when feeding
No information about how long she takes to complete her milk feeds
No information about whether the formula is cow's milk or soy milk, etc.
No information about how rice cereals are being fed
No information about her stools and stooling pattern
No information about any atopic feature or family history of atopy
No information about whether Janet had IEM screen after birth

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

  - Cow's milk allergy, eosinophilic esophagitis (EE)

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

  - CMPA can overlap with GER and a history of atopy may suggest increased risk for CMPA.

  - EE is a rare condition but can occasionally present in infants.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

  - Nothing much goes against it except that they are rare compared to GERD.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

  - Information about the type of infant formula, whether mother is taking cow's milk

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

  - GER but not affecting growth

  - Caregiver anxiety that is possibly affecting the child

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

  - None

- What is your plan for this patient?

  - Reassure caregivers that Janet is showing normal tracking of growth, which means she is receiving adequate calories through her current feeds. The perceived "inadequate" volume of milk is sufficient.

  - GER is common and if the child is putting on weight, then no further tests or medication is needed.

  - Weaning should be encouraged and the introduction of solids would help with the GER.

  - Help caregivers with their anxiety.

# Fever (Acute)

# DEFINITION OF NORMAL TEMPERATURE AND ACUTE FEVER

- Normal body temperature

  - Varies with age

    - In infants and young children, the mean normal rectal temperature is 37.5°C. This is due to their higher metabolic rate and greater body-surface-area-to-weight ratio.
    - In older children and young adults, the mean normal rectal temperature is 37.0°C.

  - Follows a circadian rhythm; temperature tends to be lower in the morning and higher in the late afternoon or evening.

- Measured temperature varies depending on the site of measurement and the level of activity:

  - Rectal temperature is considered the standard of reference for core temperature. Taking a rectal temperature is contraindicated in children with neutropenia.
  - Oral temperature is the preferred method in children who are old enough to cooperate. It is usually 0.6°C lower than the rectal temperature and is affected by mouth breathing and ingestion of hot or cold liquids.
  - Axillary temperatures are usually measured in young children who cannot cooperate with the use of oral thermometers (usually for 5 years or younger children). It is about 1°C lower than the rectal temperature and about 0.5°C lower than oral readings, but this can vary widely.
  - Infrared tympanic thermometers measure the heat produced by the tympanic membrane. These thermometers are inaccurate and poorly sensitive at all ages; but these are commonly used because of their ease and parental acceptability.
  - Infrared forehead thermometers measure the heat produced by the temporal arteries. These thermometers are inaccurate and are affected by sweating and vascular changes.

- Definition of fever

  - Fever is defined as an elevated body temperature above normal range.
  - However, there is no fixed defined temperature threshold for fever.
  - A core rectal temperature >38.0°C is usually considered as "fever," but different physicians may use different cut-offs.
  - The temperature threshold for defining fever may also depend on the underlying condition; e.g., for patients with neutropenia, the threshold may be lower than 38°C.

295

- Hyperpyrexia is usually defined as fever that is >40°C.
- Acute fever is defined as fever for <14 days.

o An acute fever may be the first presentation of a patient who has recurrent/prolonged fever (see Chapter 29).

# SYMPTOMS OF ACUTE FEVER

- Tactile warmth: the child may feel warm/hot to touch all over the body, or only feel warm centrally in the trunk and cold peripherally in the limbs.
- Feeling cold or shivering (chills).
- Feeling hot.
- Sweating.
- There may be nonspecific symptoms related to fever such as feeling uncomfortable, feeling tired, feeling lethargic, excessive crying, irritability, loss of appetite, mild abdominal discomfort, and headache.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

# The pathophysiology of fever

- Physiological elevation of the brain's temperature set point in response to triggers

o Infections, e.g., virus, bacterial, parasite, fungal
o Inflammatory conditions not due to infection, e.g., autoimmune or autoinflammatory (e.g., Kawasaki infection)
o Malignancies, e.g., acute leukemia
o Drugs

- Failure of the body's physiological mechanism to manage the body's temperature adequately

o Too much heat production
o Inadequate heat loss

- Factitious

# 1. Causes of Physiological Elevation of the Brain's Temperature Set Point in Response to Triggers

- Causes

o Infections, e.g., virus, bacterial, parasite, fungal
o Inflammatory conditions not due to infection, e.g., autoimmune or autoinflammatory (e.g., Kawasaki infection)
o Malignancies, e.g., acute leukemia
o Drugs

- Pathophysiology

o Infectious agents stimulate phagocytic cells in blood or tissue to release endogenous pyrogenic cytokines such as interleukin (IL)-1, IL-6, tumor necrosis factor (TNF), and interferon (IFN)-alpha into the blood. These act on the brain's thermoregulatory center in the anterior

hypothalamus to induce prostaglandin (PG) production, especially PG-E2. PG-E2 acts on the thermoregulatory center to raise the hypothalamic set point for body temperature.

• This activates peripheral mechanisms that stimulate the sympathetic chain and terminal adrenergic efferent nerves to raise the body temperature to the new higher set point; increasing heat production by increasing metabolic rate and increasing muscle tone and activity (shivering), and decreasing heat loss via reducing skin perfusion (peripheral vasoconstriction). Autonomic (decreased sweating) and endocrine (decreased vasopressin to reduce amount of body fluid heated) pathways are also activated.

## 2. Causes of Failure of the Body's Physiological Mechanism to Manage the Body's Temperature Adequately

• Too much heat production

  • General anesthesia induced malignant hyperthermia
  • Drug fever

• Inadequate heat loss

  • Conditions associated with decreased ability to sweat, e.g., heat stroke, rare genetic conditions

## 3. Factitious

• Causes

  • The individual is purposefully creating elevated temperature readings for himself/herself (Munchausen syndrome) or for the child (Munchausen by proxy).

• Manifestations

  • Temperature readings that are incredulous, or a suspicious overall clinical picture

# COMMON DIAGNOSES ASSOCIATED WITH ACUTE FEVER

• The most common diagnosis is infection, with viral infections being more common than bacterial infections.

## 1. Acute Fever with Localizing Symptoms or Signs

• Viral infections

  • Upper respiratory tract infection (URTI), e.g., herpangina, pharyngitis, tonsillitis, croup
  • Lower respiratory tract infection (LRTI), e.g., bronchiolitis, viral pneumonia
  • Acute otitis media
  • Dental abscess
  • Gastroenteritis (GE), e.g., rotavirus
  • Viral hepatitis

- Viral illness with exanthema (e.g., chicken pox, hand-foot and mouth disease, herpangina, exanthem subitum, measles, rubella)
- Viral myositis
- Viral myocarditis
- Viral encephalomeningitis

# • Bacterial infections

- Bacterial tonsillitis, tonsillar abscess, retropharyngeal abscess, epiglottitis, tracheitis
- Streptococcus pharyngitis, scarlet fever
- Sinusitis
- Acute otitis media
- Pneumonia
- Gastroenteritis, e.g., Salmonella enteritidis
- Cholangitis
- Appendicitis
- Peritonitis
- Meningitis
- Urinary tract infection (UTI), e.g., cystitis, pyelonephritis
- Bone and joint infection, e.g., osteomyelitis, septic arthritis
- Skin and soft tissue infection, e.g., cellulitis, lymphadenitis, abscess, folliculitis, impetigo, necrotizing fasciitis
- Bacterial endocarditis

# • Other infections

- Malaria, amoebiasis
- Fungal

• Malignancies, e.g., leukemia, lymphoma

• Autoimmune/inflammatory conditions, e.g., Kawasaki disease, Steven-Johnson syndrome

• Heat-related illness, e.g., heat stroke

# 2. Acute Fever with No Localizing Symptom or Sign

- Early undifferentiated viral illness
- Generalized viremia, e.g., dengue fever, Epstein-Barr virus (EBV), Cytomegalovirus (CMV)
- Occult bacteremia/sepsis
- Typhoid/enteric fever
- Malaria
- Deep-seated abscess
- Vaccine-related fever
- Drug-related fever
- Inflammatory/autoimmune conditions, e.g., Kawasaki disease, acute rheumatic fever
- Trauma (injuries, post surgery)
- Heat-related illness, e.g., heat stroke
- Factitious illness

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If indicated, do primary assessment, secondary assessment (see Chapter 36 Ill-Looking Baby or Child)

- If not indicated, proceed with history and physical examination

# 2. Take a History to Determine whether There Is Really a Fever

- Review the temperature readings.
- Measure the temperature.

- Neonate: preferably rectal but usually axillary is used

- 1 month to 5 years: axillary

- Older than 5 years old: oral

- Temperature values that are incredulous (e.g., >42°C): suspect factitious illness; carry out supervised temperature taking to see if the temperatures are truly real.

- Take a history of the duration and frequency of fever, method of measurement, and use of antipyretic medication and clinical response.

- Most benign self-limiting viral infections do not cause a fever longer than 5–7 days.
- Fever ≥5 days should prompt consideration and workup for a serious bacterial infection/other causes.

# 3. Take a History to Look for Localizing Symptoms and Signs

- There are many possible localizing symptoms and signs.
- The list below shows some of the possible associated symptoms and their possible causes:
- Age of child

- The younger the febrile child, the higher the incidence of serious bacterial infection.

- Febrile neonates ≤28 days old are considered a separate high-risk group as they have a higher incidence of serious bacterial infection (12%–32%).

- Fever and CNS-related symptoms

- Altered conscious state: shock, sepsis, hypoglycemia, meningitis, encephalitis, seizure
- Seizures: febrile fit, meningitis, encephalitis, hypoglycemia
- Headache: any febrile illness, meningitis, encephalitis, sinusitis

1

# • Fever and HEENT-related symptoms

o Red eyes: viral infections, Kawasaki disease
o Eye discharge: viral conjunctivitis, bacterial conjunctivitis
o Eye swelling: periorbital cellulitis, orbital cellulitis, infectious mononucleosis (EBV, CMV)
o Face pain: sinusitis, cellulitis, abscess
o Runny nose: usually viral URTI
o Colored nasal discharge: usually viral URTI, can also be seen in sinusitis
o Ear pain: otitis externa, otitis media
o Ear discharge: otitis externa, otitis media with perforated ear drum
o Gum pain: abscess, gingivitis
o Toothache: dental abscess
o Mouth ulcers: herpangina, hand, foot, and mouth disease, Steven-Johnson syndrome
o Throat pain: viral pharyngitis, bacterial pharyngitis, infectious mononucleosis (EBV, CMV)
o Difficulty swallowing: viral pharyngitis, bacterial pharyngitis, infectious mononucleosis (EBV, CMV), epiglottitis, retropharyngeal abscess, tonsillar abscess

# • Fever and respiratory-related symptoms

o Cough: viral URTI, bacterial URTI, viral LRTI, bacterial LRTI
o Breathlessness: some URTI (e.g., severe croup, epiglottis), bacterial LRTI, viral LRTI, myocarditis, shock
o Chest pain: LRTI, empyema

# • Fever and gastrointestinal-related symptoms

o Abdominal pain: viral GE, bacterial GE, hepatitis, appendicitis, peritonitis, shock
o Vomiting: viral GE, bacterial GE, hepatitis, appendicitis, peritonitis, meningitis, encephalitis
o Bilious vomiting: appendicitis, peritonitis
o Diarrhea: viral GE, bacterial GE, hepatitis, appendicitis, peritonitis
o Bloody diarrhea: bacterial GE
o Jaundice: hepatitis, cholangitis, viral-induced hemolysis

# • Fever and musculoskeletal-related symptoms

o Neck pain, neck stiffness: meningitis
o Limb pain, limb swelling: myalgia, osteomyelitis, leukemia, necrotizing fasciitis, myositis
o Joint pain: viral arthralgia, septic arthritis, reactive arthritis, acute rheumatic fever
o Joint swelling: septic arthritis, reactive arthritis, acute rheumatic fever
o Back pain: viral illness, osteomyelitis, UTI, myalgia
o Muscle pain: myositis, viral-induced myalgia

• Fever and skin-related symptoms

• Flushing of skin: shock, viral infection (e.g., dengue)
• Rash: meningococcal meningitis, viral exanthema, Kawasaki disease, scarlet fever, acute rheumatic fever, EBV, CMV
• Peeling skin: scarlet fever, Kawasaki disease
• Bruising: DIC, dengue hemorrhagic shock, meningococcemia

• Fever and dysuria, frequency, foul smelling urine, hematuria: UTI

• Fever and loin pain: UTI

• Fever and swelling: lymphadenopathy, lymphadenitis, abscess, Kawasaki disease, EBV, CMV, leukemia, lymphoma

# 4. Take a History to Look for Any Risk Factors

• Immunosuppressed/immunodeficient (see Chapter 32 Frequent Infections)
• Any structural condition or obstruction that will predispose to infection, e.g., vesico-ureteric reflux (VUR), biliary atresia
• Incomplete immunization
• Travel history
• Contact history
• Recent surgery
• Prosthetic device in situ, e.g., ventriculoperitoneal shunt, artificial heart valves, stents
• Medical device in situ, e.g., urinary catheters, intravenous lines
• Any other chronic illness, e.g., prematurity, low birth weight, malnutrition

# 5. Take a History about Investigation and Treatment Done So Far

• Test results
• Medications given, especially antibiotic exposure, which may alter the course of bacterial infections, or affect subsequent investigations (like cultures)
• What makes the fever better? What makes the fever worse?

# 6. Conduct a Head-to-Toe Physical Examination

• If there are localizing symptoms, you can start with the examination of that system but you must go on and conduct a complete head-to-toe physical examination to ensure that no other signs are missed.

• Head-to-toe examination

• Skin: examine for skin changes (e.g., papules, macules, pustules, vesicles, bullae, petechiae, purpura, erythema, mottling, pallor, cyanosis, flushing, swelling, tenderness).
• Eyes: look for conjunctivitis, discharge, pallor, jaundice.
• Fundi: look for Roth spots, papilledema, retinal changes.
• Sinuses (frontal and maxillary): percuss for tenderness.
• Nose: look for congestion and discharge (clear or purulent).
• Oropharynx and gingiva: look for enlarged tonsils, erythema, pustules, exudate, ulcers.

- **Tympanic membranes**: examine for erythema, dullness, bulging, effusion, discharge, signs of inflammation/infection.
- **Neck**: assess for neck stiffness, feel for lymph node enlargement, any other swelling, any tenderness.
- **Axilla**: feel for lymph node enlargement, any other swelling, any tenderness
- **Chest**: examine the heart, examine the lungs.
- **Abdomen**: feel for tenderness, guarding, mass, organ enlargement, etc.
- **Groin**: feel for lymph node enlargement, any other swelling, any tenderness.
- **Anus**: assess for tenderness, swelling, ulcers.
- **All joints** (including spine): assess for signs of inflammation (swelling, redness, warmth, pain, limitation of movement).
- **Hands and feet**: assess for splinter hemorrhages, Janeway lesions, blisters, rash, swelling, redness.
- **Conduct a full neurologic examination.**

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

# Red flags

- Age < 1 month
- Lethargy, listlessness
- Toxic looking
- Irritable, inconsolable
- Altered mental state
- Hemodynamic instability
- Neck stiffness
- Respiratory distress, breathlessness
- Petechiae or purpura (often associated with sepsis with or without disseminated intravascular coagulation)
- Immunosuppressed/immunodeficient
- Condition that predisposes to infection, e.g., VUR, biliary atresia,
- Recent surgery
- Prosthetic device in situ, e.g., VP shunt, artificial heart valves, stents
- Medical device in situ, e.g., urinary catheters, intravenous lines
- Inability to sweat

# The presence of any red flags

- should prompt consideration for further investigation for the cause of the fever
- should prompt consideration for starting empirical treatment for serious bacterial infections even before confirmatory testing results are out

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 7-week-old baby presents with a 1-day history of fever to a maximum temperature of 38.7°C. This was associated with poor feeding; her 3 hourly breastfeeds were reduced in duration from 10–15 min to 5–10 min each time. She had one episode of watery green nonbloody stool, but

subsequent stools (three episodes) were normal in consistency and frequency. She was passing urine as per normal. She had no vomits, lethargy, cough, cold, or rash. She was crying more than usual but was easily consolable by parents.

She was born at 37 weeks gestation with a birth weight of 2.9 kg by emergency Caesarean section for failure to progress. Mother had spontaneous labor with no prolonged rupture of membranes. Mother was a Group B Streptococcus carrier and received adequate antibiotic prophylaxis during labor. Her antenatal history was unremarkable.

At delivery, baby was born in good condition and required no resuscitation at birth. She was discharged at day 3 of life. Since discharge, growth and development have been normal. Immunizations were up to date. She lives at home with both parents and a 14-month-old sister. Her sister was unwell a week ago with fever and a runny nose. Her mother was unwell 3 days ago with fever and a runny nose. There is no travel history.

On examination, her temperature is 38.2°C, heart rate 135/min, respiratory rate 35/min, blood pressure 85/50 mmHg, and oxygen saturation 98% in room air. Her weight is 4.1 kg. She is pink and well perfused. Capillary refill time is <2 seconds. She has no sunken eyes or reduced skin turgor. She is active and alert with normal tone and movement. She handles well. Anterior fontanelle is soft. There is no rash or jaundice. There is no respiratory distress. Heart sounds are normal with no murmurs. On auscultation of the lungs, there is good and equal air entry bilaterally with vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft, not distended, and nontender. The liver is palpable 1 cm below the right costal margin. There are no masses felt. Bowel sounds are normal. Femoral pulses are easily palpable bilaterally. Moro reflex is symmetrical.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

○ Fever with nonspecific symptoms of poor feeding, increased crying, and a single episode of diarrhea

• What is your diagnosis?

○ Possible bacterial infection, e.g., UTI or occult bacteremia

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

○ Presence of significant fever

○ Young infant between 1 and 3 months old

○ Acute illness

○ Crying

○ Poor feeding

○ No specific localizing signs or symptoms to indicate a clinically apparent focus

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

○ No findings to exclude this diagnosis

○ No other risk factors, e.g., prematurity, congenital anomalies that would increase the risk of serious bacterial infection

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

○ Foul-smelling urine may not always be present in a febrile UTI.

○ Abnormal urine microscopy (pyuria), positive urine culture if UTI.

○ Positive blood culture if there is occult bacteremia.

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

○ Early viral upper respiratory tract infection

○ Early gastroenteritis

• For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

○ Viral upper respiratory tract infection – positive contact history with close household contacts with recent fever and rhinorrhea; alert, active, not toxic

○ Gastroenteritis – watery diarrhea

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

○ Viral upper respiratory tract infection – absence of nasal congestion, rhinorrhea, cough so far

○ Gastroenteritis – a single episode of watery stool is a nonspecific finding

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ Development of localizing symptoms with time (nasal congestion, rhinorrhea, cough, increased diarrhea or vomits)

○ Positive viral immunofluorescence or PCR result from a nasopharyngeal swab/aspirate (e.g., respiratory syncytial virus, influenza)

○ Positive stool viral PCR (e.g., rotavirus)

○ Mild lymphopenia or neutropenia

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Possible UTI or occult bacteremia

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ Reduced feeding, which may lead to dehydration

○ Increased crying, which may indicate a very ill baby

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Admit the child for observation of fever and feeding.

- Carry out partial septic screen – full blood count, blood culture, urine microscopy and culture. C reactive protein may also be done.
- If initial blood tests and urine microscopy are normal, to hold off antibiotic treatment and observe.
- If an elevated white blood cell count, elevated CRP or abnormal urine microscopy (pyuria), to complete a full septic workup with a lumbar puncture for CSF protein, glucose, lactate, cell count (microscopy) and culture, and start empiric antibiotic treatment.
- Can consider doing viral immunofluorescence or PCR test from a nasopharyngeal swab/aspirate to detect common respiratory viruses if available.
- Can consider doing a stool culture and/or viral PCR test.

# Fever (Recurrent, Persistent, or Prolonged)

Chan Si Min • Elizabeth Y. Ang • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF RECURRENT, PERSISTENT, PROLONGED FEVER

- See Chapter 28 for definition of the following terms:

- Normal body temperature
- Measured temperature (varies depending on the level of activity and site of measurement)
- Definition of fever
- Acute fever (defined as fever <14 days)

- Fever can also be frequent, persistent/prolonged, recurrent, or periodic.

- Frequent fever/recurrent fever

- "Frequent fever" refers to the situation where a person is perceived to have more episodes of fever than the average person in that age group. There is usually at least one fever-free day between these fever episodes.
- There is no strict definition as to how frequent is too frequent.
- The term "recurrent fever" is also sometimes used when a patient has multiple episodes of fever.

- Persistent or prolonged fever

- There is no strict definition as to what is persistent or prolonged fever.
- Some of the common working definitions include

- Having fever for 8 days or more

- A single illness in which the duration of fever exceeds that expected for the clinical diagnosis (e.g., >10 days for viral upper respiratory tract infections; >3 weeks for infectious mononucleosis)

- The above are descriptions of the patterns/categories of fever; they are not diagnoses. They are meant to help the doctor narrow down the list of differential diagnoses so that clinicians can make better choices on investigations and referrals.

# SYMPTOMS OF RECURRENT, PERSISTENT, PROLONGED FEVER

- Tactile warmth: the child may feel warm/hot to touch all over the body, or only feel warm centrally in the trunk and cold peripherally in the limbs.
- Feeling cold or shivering (chills).
- Feeling hot.

306

- Sweating.
- There may be nonspecific symptoms related to fever such as feeling uncomfortable, feeling tired, feeling lethargic, excessive crying, irritability, loss of appetite, mild abdominal discomfort, and headache.
- The patient may also be seeing you in between the febrile episodes as the patient/parent is worried about the history of fever.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

# The pathophysiology of fever

- Physiological elevation of the brain's temperature set point in response to triggers

- Infections (e.g., virus, bacterial, parasite)
- Inflammatory conditions not due to infection, e.g., be autoimmune or autoinflammatory (e.g., Kawasaki infection)
- Malignancies (e.g., acute leukemia)
- Drugs

- Failure of the body's physiological mechanism to manage the body's temperature adequately

- Too much heat production
- Inadequate heat loss

- Factitious

# 1. Causes of Physiological Elevation of the Brain's Temperature Set Point in Response to Triggers

- Infections

- Viral: Epstein-Barr virus infection, cytomegalovirus infection, human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infection.
- Bacterial: urinary tract infection, otitis media, gingivitis, sinusitis, infective endocarditis, osteomyelitis, abscess, streptococcal throat infection, tuberculosis, cat-scratch disease, typhoid fever, brucellosis, and borreliosis.
- Parasitic: malaria.

- Malignancies

- Leukemia
- Lymphoma
- Neuroblastoma

- Drug fever

- Inflammatory conditions not due to infection

- Autoimmune: Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)
- Others: Kawasaki disease, inflammatory bowel disease, Kikuchi disease
- Auto-inflammatory:

- Juvenile idiopathic arthritis systemic type (JIA, systemic type)
- Periodic fever syndromes

☐ These refer to a heterogeneous group of auto-inflammatory disorders where the defect is in the innate immune system, causing unexplained periods of abnormal and exaggerated inflammation.
☐ Does not involve autoantibodies.
☐ Fever is the main feature, and the associated symptoms for each fever episode are similar and predictable within each type of periodic fever syndrome.
☐ The duration of fever can be days to weeks, with intervening intervals of weeks to months of complete well-being. Episodes can have either "clockwork" or irregular periodicity.
☐ Causes are

- Unknown: periodic fever, aphthous stomatitis, pharyngitis, adenitis (PFAPA), and cyclical neutropenia

- Single gene disorder: familial Mediterranean fever (FMF), hyperimmunoglobulin D syndrome (HIDS), cyropyrin associated periodic syndromes (CAPS) (include neonatal onset multisystem inflammatory disease [NOMID], Muckle–Wells syndrome), TNF receptor-associated periodic syndrome (TRAPS), etc.

## 2. Causes of Failure of the Body's Physiological Mechanism to Manage the Body's Temperature Adequately

- Too much heat production

  - Hyperthyroidism
  - Drug fever

- Inadequate heat loss

  - Drug fever
  - Conditions associated with decreased ability to sweat

    - Fever tends to come on when the environment is hot and/or when significant exercise is done.
    - Sweating is sparse or absent.
    - May have evidence of epidermal dysplasia (sparse hair, sparse eyebrows, sparse eyelashes, dry skin).

## 3. Fictitious

- Causes

  - The individual is purposefully creating elevated temperature readings for himself/herself (Munchausen syndrome) or for the child (Munchausen by proxy).

- Manifestations

  - Temperature readings that are incredulous
  - May be associated with other features suggestive of non-accidental injuries (see Chapter 37)

# 1. Common Diagnoses Associated with Frequent Fevers/ Recurrent Fever

- The most common cause of frequent fevers is frequent infections or inadequately treated infection

- Viral infections: upper respiratory tract infections, gastroenteritis, lower respiratory tract infections
- Bacterial: urinary tract infection, otitis media, gingivitis, sinusitis, infective endocarditis, osteomyelitis, intra-abdominal abscess, streptococcal throat infection, tuberculosis, cat-scratch disease, enteric fever, brucellosis, borreliosis
- Parasite: malaria
- Conditions that may predispose to frequent and/or recurrent infections (see Chapter 32)

- Environment factors
- Structural abnormality and/or obstruction
- Other systemic condition apart from immunodeficiency
- Immunodeficiency (primary or secondary)

- Inflammatory conditions not due to infection

- Autoimmune: SLE
- Auto-inflammatory: JIA (systemic type), periodic fever syndromes
- Others: inflammatory bowel disease

- Malignancies

- Leukemia
- Lymphoma
- Neuroblastoma

- Hyperthyroidism
- Drug fever
- Decreased ability to sweat
- Factitious

# 2. Common Diagnoses Associated with Persistent or Prolonged Fever

- Infection is by far the most common cause of prolonged fever

- Viral: Epstein-Barr virus infection, cytomegalovirus infection, HIV infection
- Bacterial: gingivitis, sinusitis, streptococcal throat infection, infective endocarditis, osteomyelitis, intra-abdominal abscess, tuberculosis, cat-scratch disease, enteric fever, brucellosis, borreliosis
- Parasite: malaria
- Conditions that may predispose to frequent and/or recurrent infections (see Chapter 32)

- Environmental factors
- Structural abnormality and/or obstruction
- Other systemic condition apart from immunodeficiency
- Immunodeficiency (primary or secondary)

• Inflammatory conditions that are not due to infection

• Autoimmune: SLE
• Autoinflammatory: JIA (systemic type), periodic fever syndromes
• Others: Kawasaki, inflammatory bowel disease

• Malignancy

• Leukemia
• Lymphoma
• Neuroblastoma

• Hyperthyroidism

• Drug fever

• Decreased ability to sweat

• Factitious

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Initial Impression

• Initial impression

• Assess appearance
• Assess work of breathing
• Assess circulation to skin (color)

• The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

• If indicated, do primary assessment, secondary assessment (see Chapter 36 III-Looking Baby or child)

• If not indicated, proceed with history and physical examination

# 2. Take a History to Determine whether There Is Really a Fever

• Ask if temperature was measured; and if so, determine which method was used and what the readings were.
• You may also need to take a temperature using your own equipment so as to verify that it is not an equipment fault.
• If no temperature was taken, you should take a temperature of the patient to verify the presence of fever.
• Most history reporting about high-grade fever is likely to be accurate, i.e. fever was present.
• History about having low-grade fever diagnosed based on "felt warm to the touch" or feeling warm may or may not be accurate.

# 3. History Taking: How Many Fever Episodes Were There? What Were the Details of Each Episode?

• How many fever episodes were there? One or more than one?
• If there was more than one episode, for each episode ask

• How long was each episode?

• What is the pattern of the fever?

■ Did the temperature ever touch the normal range during the fever?
■ Is it only at night? (This may be seen more commonly with TB or lymphoma.)

■ Bear in mind that the use of antipyretics will change the pattern.

• What were the associated symptoms?
• What made it better? What made it worse?

• Obtain information about investigation, treatment, response to treatment, etc.
• Travel history
• Contact history
• How long was the afebrile period between each fever episode?
• Did the fevers recur regularly or irregularly? Are the fever episodes predictable?

If it occurs regularly, think about things like malaria (fever separated by days) or periodic fever syndromes (fever separated by weeks).

• What was the progression of the fever frequency? Getting more frequent? Less frequent?

# 4. History Taking: Look for Any Predisposition to Frequent Infections

• Factors predisposing to frequent infections include

• Environmental factors
• Structural abnormality and/or obstruction
• Foreign body in situ
• Other systemic condition apart from immunodeficiency
• Immunodeficiency (primary or secondary)
• See Chapter 32

• Environment factors

• Poor air quality
• High air humidity
• Poor water sanitation
• Poor water hygiene
• Contaminated food supply
• Poor food hygiene
• Unhygienic food utensils (e.g., baby bottles, pots, bowls, cups)
• Poor hand hygiene
• Crowded living conditions
• High population density
• Day care/kindergarten/school going age
• Siblings/household contacts
• Epidemiology of infective agents (e.g., malaria in the tropics, seasonal influenza infection)
• Health care policies, e.g., immunization program

• Structural abnormality and/or obstruction

• CNS: congenital cranial and spinal cord defects, congenital inner ear malformations, cerebrospinal fluid fistula after skull fracture → frequent meningitis
• Respiratory: bronchial cyst, cystic fibrosis, immotile cilia syndrome, bronchial compression due to an external mass → frequent lower respiratory tract infections

- Urological: vesico-ureteric reflux, posterior urethral valve, ureteropelvic junction obstruction → frequent urinary tract infections
- Upper airway: Eustachian tube dysfunction, tonsillar and/or adenoidal hypertrophy → frequent otitis media
- Skin: dermatitis → frequent staphylococcal skin infections

# - Foreign body in situ

- Prosthetic device in situ, e.g., ventriculoperitoneal (VP) shunt, artificial heart valve, stent
- Medical device in situ, e.g., urinary catheter, intravenous line
- Other foreign bodies

# - Other systemic condition apart from immunodeficiency

- Diabetes mellitus
- Staphylococcus aureus carrier
- Any form of dermatitis
- Swallowing dysfunction
- Gastroesophageal reflux disease
- Allergic rhinitis
- Tonsillar and adenoid hypertrophy/enlargement
- Cystic fibrosis
- Immotile cilia syndrome
- Implants/foreign body in situ

# - Immunodeficiency

- Secondary immunodeficiency
- Primary immunodeficiency

# 5. History Taking: Do a Systems Review, Get Information about Immunization Status, Take a Family History

- Do a systems review to detect any other symptoms
- Ask for a family history: evidence of similar episodes may point towards a genetic cause but may also point towards a shared environmental cause.
- Immunization status: determine what was given, any reactions to the vaccination.

# 6. Carry Out a Thorough Physical Examination

- See if there are physical findings that help reach the diagnosis.
- Evaluate for complications (sepsis, shock, organ damage, growth failure, developmental delay, psychosocial effects, stress on caregiver, stress on family).

# 7. Algorithm

- It is likely that you will need to look at the grouping/pattern of the various physical findings to determine the cause.
- Examples (not a totally exhaustive list) are shown in the table (Table 29-1)
- Please also refer to Chapter 32 to determine whether or not an underlying predisposition to frequent infection needs to be excluded.

1
