# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Lethargy, listlessness
- Toxic looking
- Irritable, Inconsolable
- Altered mental state
- Hemodynamic instability
- Respiratory distress, breathlessness
- Petechiae or purpura
- Severe anemia
- Immunosuppressed/immunodeficient
- Fever >5 days
- More than 10 self-limited viral illnesses per year for the first 2–3 years of life
- Recurrent bacterial infections or unusual infections
- Prosthetic device in situ, e.g., ventriculoperitoneal shunt, artificial heart valves, stents
- Medical device in situ, e.g., urinary catheters, intravenous lines
- Poor growth, failure to thrive, loss of weight
- History and signs suggestive of thyroid storm, family history of early childhood deaths, recurrent fevers, kidney failure (to suggest amyloidosis, which is a complication of some periodic fever syndromes)

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

Ethan has been brought by his mother to see you as she is concerned about him having fever frequently. He is a 2-year-old boy who was previously well. His mother reported that he has had two episodes of fever in the last 1 month.

One month ago, he received his second dose of measles–mumps–rubella–varicella zoster (MMRV) vaccination. A day after the vaccination, he developed a low-grade fever of up to 38°C for 3 days. This was not associated with any localizing symptom. Two weeks ago, he began having fever daily. The fever could reach 38.5°C. When the fever was up, Ethan would be less active and less interested in playing. There have been no symptoms of rhinorrhea, coughing, vomiting, diarrhea, or smelly urine. He did not have frequent fevers or infections.

His mother also reported that he had more bruises over the last 2 weeks. The bruises were noted to be on his forehead, back, thighs, and legs, as well as arms. Most of the bruises were mostly related to trauma. (Ethan is a very active boy.) However, he did have some unexplained small bruises on his back and his left post-auricular area. These bruises were nontender and would fade away by themselves. His parents pinpointed some red spots over Ethan's legs about 2 weeks back, which resolved on their own. Starting Friday last week, he started developing darker and larger patches of bruises. The patient is cared for by the family helper at home. The parents do not suspect that the helper is hurting the patient because she has always been very protective of the children.

He also had some gum bleeding the night before possibly from being hit by his older brother during play. This resolved after some local pressure. Otherwise, there have been no other symptoms of bleeding such as epistaxis, hematuria, bloody or malenic stools, sudden joint swellings, or muscle swellings. This is the first time Ethan is having such problems. He had not been so prone to bruising before. He has never had problems with clotting. Last year, he fell and had a cut on his chin. This did not bleed excessively and has healed completely.

He was not looking pale. He was still playing and eating well and was gaining good weight.

There is no report of neck or abdominal swelling. There has not been limping or complaining of any bone pain. His sleep has not been disturbed.

The rest of the systemic review showed no abnormalities. There is no travel history. He did take paracetamol during the episodes of fever. He is not on any long-term medication, traditional medicine, or health supplement. He has no known drug allergies.

On examination, height is 10th percentile, weight 50th percentile, and head circumference 50th percentile. The temperature was 38.6°C, heart rate was 112/min, respiratory rate was 28/min, and SpO₂ was 96% on room air. The child was alert, cheerful, well thrived, and well hydrated. The anterior fontanelle was normotensive. There was pallor. There was no jaundice, clubbing, or respiratory distress. Bilateral tympanic membranes were intact and normal. There were no oral ulcers. Heart and lung examination was normal. There was 8-cm firm hepatomegaly and 5-cm firm splenomegaly. Otherwise, the abdomen was soft, nontender, and with no ascites. Bilateral testes were descended, equal in size, and not tender. There were multiple matted firm nontender lymph nodes at pre-auricular, post-auricular, occipital, cervical, supraclavicular, axillary, and inguinal regions. Largest lymph node size was 1 cm. GCS was 15. There was no neck rigidity. Tone was normal. Power was normal. Reflexes 2+ in all four limbs with downgoing plantars. Gait was steady. There were no joint swellings or abnormal bone contours or scars. There were multiple bruises over lower limbs and back, and a fading bruise over left forehead. Petechiae were seen at lower limbs. BCG scar at left buttock not indurated. The rest of the examination was normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Frequent fevers associated with symptoms of easy bruising and gum bleeding

- What is your diagnosis?

- Acute leukemia or lymphoma

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Frequent fever.
- No obvious source. Although parents report that there was fever soon after MMRV vaccination, MMRV vaccination related fever tends to occur 1 week after the infection.
- The symptoms of increased bruising and the finding of petechiae suggest that there is either a low platelet count or platelet dysfunction. Given that the boy was previously well, it is more likely to be an acquired cause of low platelets.
- The finding of pallor and petechiae suggests that there is at least two-cell-line involvement. This is either due to bone marrow failure or bone marrow infiltration/replacement.
- The finding of lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly suggests that there is a lymphoproliferative event happening. Coupled with the above, it would suggest that it is more of a bone marrow infiltration/replacement rather than bone marrow failure, e.g., acute leukemia or lymphoma.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- The parents did not report that he was getting pale. This is most likely because the parents did not notice it (e.g., it came on gradually, or parents are not trained to recognize pallor).

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- There was no loss of weight or loss of appetite. These two symptoms and signs tend to come on when the malignancy is present for a longer period of time.
- There is no serious bacterial infection. Although leukemia/lymphoma can cause neutropenia, it may not necessarily result in bacterial infection.
- There was no bone pain. This is often a late sign in leukemia/lymphoma.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- I do not think there is a good alternative diagnosis.
- The child seems to have a bone marrow infiltrative condition.
- If it is no leukemia or lymphoma, it might be rare causes such as neuroblastoma.

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

o Two-cell-line involvement

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o No abdominal mass (though neuroblastoma can be extra-abdominal)

o No bony lumps (which one might expect if the neuroblastoma is disseminated enough to replace the bone marrow)

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

o Bony swellings

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

o Symptoms and signs of spontaneous bruising are suggestive of very low platelet levels. This caries a risk of bleeds that can be life-threatening, i.e., risk of intracranial bleeds, etc.

o Sign of pallor. This suggests anemia and puts the child at risk for high output failure and also having little reserve if there is a bleed.

o At risk of having neutropenic sepsis, though clinically the child does not appear to have it.

- What is your plan for this patient?

o Admit to an isolation room in the hospital.

o Institute infection control procedures to prevent him from getting an infection.

o Do investigations to look for the cause and complication

■ Full blood count (FBC) (to see the white blood counts, hemoglobin, platelet, presence of blasts).

■ Peripheral blood film (to look for presence of blasts).

■ Urea, creatinine, electrolytes (to look for evidence of tumor lysis syndrome).

■ Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)(elevated LDH level is in keeping with rapid cell growth, e.g., malignancy).

■ Group and cross match (in case packed red blood cell and/or platelet transfusion is needed).

■ May need to arrange for a bone marrow aspirate and biopsy if blood counts and blood film are suggestive of bone marrow replacement/malignancy.

■ Set an intravenous line for venous access.

■ Refer to a hematologist/oncologist for urgent review.

■ Review white blood counts. If neutropenia is present, may need to consider starting IV antibiotic to cover for neutropenic sepsis.

■ Discuss FBC results with a senior doctor to see if he needs packed red blood cell transfusion and/or platelet transfusion.

# Fits, Falls, and Faints

Jeremy Lin • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF FITS, FALLS AND FAINTS

"Fits, falls, and faints" describes a group of conditions that are often associated with transient varying impaired consciousness and/or occurrence of motor manifestations. They may occur only once or occur repeatedly; and often with complete recovery and minimal sequelae.

# SYMPTOMS OF FITS, FALLS AND FAINTS

- An eyewitness description of the event is extremely important, as very often the patient may not be able to tell or recall what happened. One should put in much effort to find and talk to the eyewitness(es).
- Symptoms of varying impaired consciousness can include

- Blanking out, less responsive than normal, unresponsive
- Excessive sleeping, difficult to arouse
- Amnesia, confusion, disorientation, uncharacteristic behaviors

- Symptoms of motor manifestations can include

- Tumbling over, losing balance, collapsing to the floor, not moving, limb stiffening, limb jerking, twisting of limbs, convulsive movements

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

The causes of "fits, falls, and faints" are broadly classified into

- Seizure events (fits)

- Primary seizure/unprovoked seizure
- Secondary/provoked seizure

- Nonseizure events (falls and faints), also known as paroxysmal nonepileptic events (PNEs)

- Predominantly impaired awareness
- Predominantly loss of balance or onset of weakness
- Predominantly motor movements

The associated features (pre-event, during event and post-event) help in further differentiating between the possible causes of "fits, falls, and faints."

# 1. Seizure Events

- A seizure is an event resulting from abnormal, excessive, and hypersynchronous discharges of a set of neurons in the central nervous system (CNS).

318

- A seizure can be one-off or recurrent.
- The 2017 seizure classification is based on three key features: where the seizure begins in the brain, level of awareness, and other features of the seizure.

o Where seizures begin in the brain

- Focal onset
- Generalized onset
- Unknown onset

o Level of awareness during a seizure

- Aware
- Impaired awareness
- Awareness unknown

o Other features of seizures

- Motor (tonic–clonic, tonic, clonic, myoclonic, atonic, etc.)
- Nonmotor (absence, automatism, etc.)

# ILAE 2017 classification of seizure types basic version

Focal onset

Aware

Impaired
awareness

Motor onset
Nonmotor onset

Focal to bilateral tonic-clonic

Generalized onset

Motor
Tonic-clonic
Other motor
Nonmotor (absence)

Unknown onset

Motor
Tonic-clonic
Other motor
Nonmotor

Unclassifiedᵃ

ᵃDue to inadequate information or inability to place in other categories.

FIGURE 30-1

The basic ILAE 2017 operational classification of seizure types. Definitions, other seizure types, and descriptors are listed in the reference paper, Epilepsia, 58(4):531–542, 2017 doi: 10.1111/epi.13671.

- Seizures may be classified into primary seizures and secondary/provoked seizures

o Primary seizures, which are events caused by unprovoked, abnormal electrical discharges of the brain

■ First unprovoked manifestation; for some, this is the first manifestation of epilepsy

■ Epilepsy

□ Is diagnosed clinically when there are two or more unprovoked seizures

□ Is classified as

- Idiopathic (defined as an epilepsy of predominately genetic or presumed genetic origin and in which there is no gross neuroanatomic or neuropathologic abnormality. Included here are epilepsies of presumed multigenic or complex inheritance, but for which currently the genetic basis has not been elucidated)

- Symptomatic (defined as an epilepsy of an acquired or genetic cause, associated with gross anatomic or pathologic abnormalities, and/or clinical features, indicative of underlying disease or condition)

- Cryptogenic (defined here as an epilepsy of presumed symptomatic nature in which the cause has not been identified)

o Secondary/provoked seizures, i.e., secondary to an acute process affecting the CNS, such as:

■ Extra-cranial causes of fever, e.g., childhood febrile fits

■ Intra-cranial infection, e.g., meningitis, encephalitis, cerebral abscess

■ Space occupying lesion in the brain, e.g., tumor, arteriovenous malformation

■ Cerebrovascular accident, e.g., intracranial bleed, stroke

■ Raised intracranial pressure, e.g., hydrocephalus, hypertensive emergency

■ Head injury, e.g., concussion, contusion

■ Anoxia/hypoxia/hypotension, e.g., breath holding (blue) spells, acute anoxic (pallid) seizure, arrhythmia, hypoxic ischemic insult

■ Metabolic cause

□ Hypoglycemia

□ Electrolyte imbalances, e.g., hyponatremia, hypocalcemia, uremia, hyperammonemia

■ Drug (legal and illegal), poisons, substance abuse, e.g., alcohol

■ Heat stroke

## 2. Paroxysmal Nonepileptic Events

- Paroxysmal PNEs are episodes with transient varying impaired consciousness with/without motor manifestations but are not seizures.

- In our text, we have excluded all seizures (both provoked and unprovoked) from PNE, for the simplified dichotomy of seizures and

1

nonseizure events. But do note that in other texts, authors may include provoked nonepileptic seizures as part of their definition of PNE.

• The phenotype of PNE can be

o Predominantly impaired awareness
o Predominantly loss of balance or onset of weakness
o Predominantly motor movements

• These events may be physiological or pathological (Table 30-1.)

TABLE 30-1

COMMON CAUSES OF PNE

|  Phenotype | Pathogenesis | Infant/Toddler | Child/Adolescent  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Predominantly impaired awareness | Physiological |  | • Reflex syncope (vasovagal situational, carotid massage) • Orthostatic hypotension • Day-dreaming  |
|   |  Pathological | • Hypoglycemia • Brief resolved event (BRE) (see Chapter 13) | • Hypoglycemia • Cardiogenic syncope • Transient ischemic attacks (TIA) • Psychogenic  |
|  Predominantly loss of balance or onset of weakness | Physiological |  | • Not mindful of the environment, e.g., walking into a pothole  |
|   |  Pathological | • Hypoglycemia • Benign paroxysmal vertigo | • Hypoglycemia • Movement disorders (e.g., tics, dyskinesia) • Vestibular disorders • TIA • Psychogenic  |
|  Predominantly motor movements | Physiological | • Shuddering, chills, rigors, tremors • Self-gratification/masturbation | • Hyperventilation • Sleep myoclonus  |
|   |  Pathological | • Gastroesophageal reflux with Sandifer syndrome | • Movement disorders (e.g., tics, dyskinesia) • TIA • Psychogenic  |

Note: Conditions shown in bold are more likely to be potentially life-threatening.

![img-3.jpeg](img-3.jpeg)

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History Taking: Get a Detailed Description of What Happened

- Get history of the event, post-event, and pre-event.
- Always ask for any video of the event. This is the most helpful and has been increasingly possible with the availability of smartphones.
- If there was an eye witness, get the account from that person. One should put in much effort to find and talk to the eyewitness(es).
- Take a history from the patient (if old enough). Check with the patient (if old enough) if he was aware of the event and obtain the patient's account and recollection (if any) of the event.
- In general, a patient is less likely to remember a seizure event (brain involvement) but more likely to recall a PNE.

# 2. History Taking: Other Aspects

- Ask for associated symptoms.
- Do a systems review.
- Review growth and development.
- Family history
- Travel history
- Immunization history
- Social history
- Drug/supplement intake

# 3. Physical Examination

- Always carry out a thorough head-to-toe physical examination including plotting height, weight, head circumference, and evaluating the development.

# 4. Determine whether It Was a Seizure or Not a Seizure

- Look at the nature of the event and post-event.
- The following features are suggestive of a seizure:

- Event:

- The level of awareness can be normal or impaired or unknown.
- The more common motor manifestations include

  - Patient going limp/soft
  - Patient stopping and staring/blanking out
  - Tonic-clonic movements in the anterior-posterior plane
  - Tonic-only movements
  - Clonic-only movements
  - Myoclonic jerks

- The motor manifestations may be generalized, or focal, or start focal and then become bilateral.
- The movements are difficult to be stopped by restraint or distraction.
- There may be associated eye or head deviation.
- Do remember that some seizures do not have motor manifestations.

o Post-event:

- There may be amnesia of the event and post-ictal drowsiness that lasts <30 min (focal to bilateral seizure, generalized seizure).
- There may be associated transient focal weakness suggestive of Todd paralysis (usually lasting ≤24 hours).

o If it was a seizure, determine if it was primary or provoked (i.e., due to a secondary cause).

o Look at the pre-event to determine if it is primary or provoked seizure.
o Ask for associated symptoms.
o The following features are suggestive of a primary/unprovoked seizure:

- Sudden onset, no obvious triggers or specific circumstances (unprovoked).
- Risk factors: family history of epilepsy, family history of febrile fit, a child who is neurodevelopmentally abnormal (delay or regression) suggestive of brain malformation or a past history of possible CNS injury from various causes, including infections and trauma, increasing the risk for epilepsy.

o The features suggestive of a secondary cause (provoking factor) are many. Possible symptom and signs include:

- Fever, headaches, altered conscious state, neck stiffness, abnormal neurological signs (suggestive of intracranial infections, e.g., meningitis, encephalitis)
- No fever, has headaches, altered conscious state, abnormal neurological signs (suggestive of raised intracranial pressure, e.g., due to intracranial hemorrhage, space-occupying lesion, hypertensive emergency)
- Head injury, headache, altered conscious state, abnormal neurological signs (suggestive of acute intracranial pathology, e.g., concussion, contusion, intracranial hemorrhage)
- Crying (breath-holding spells) or pain/unpleasant event (reflex anoxic syncope)
- Cyanosis (suggestive of apnea, breath-holding, hypoxic ischemic insult)
- Pallor (suggestive of anoxic reflex syncope, hypotension preceding seizure, arrhythmia)
- Vomiting, diarrhea, not eating well (suggestive of or causing hypoglycemia, hyponatremia)
- History of fluctuating mental state, food aversion (suggestive of inborn error of metabolism)
- Intake of legal or illegal drugs that predispose to seizures

# 5. If It Was Not a Seizure, Was It a Paroxysmal Nonepileptic Event?

• Determine whether it is

o Predominantly impaired awareness
o Predominantly loss of balance or onset of weakness
o Predominantly motor movements

# • Predominantly impaired awareness

o Pre-event: There may be specific triggers such as

- Occurring after standing for a long time (orthostatic hypotension)
- Occurring soon after standing up from a recumbent position (orthostatic hypotension)
- Occurring after pallor (vasovagal syncope; orthostatic hypotension, cardiogenic syncope; hypoglycemia)
- Occurring after giddiness or greying of vision (vasovagal syncope; orthostatic hypotension, cardiogenic syncope, transient ischemic attacks [TIA])
- Occurring after palpitations or exertional symptoms (cardiogenic syncope)
- Occurring during boredom (day dreaming)
- Has risk factor for hypoglycemia (e.g., vomiting, diarrhea, not eating well)
- The child is usually able to recall the pre-event (all of the differentials)

# o Event:

- The child is usually aware and able to recall some parts of the events (most of the differentials)
- Distractible, i.e., can "snap out of it" when called (seen in day-dreaming).
- Not distractible, i.e., seen in the other differentials except for day-dreaming.
- If there was impaired awareness and the child recovered quickly spontaneously or recovered quickly upon placing in a recumbent position, this suggests vasovagal syncope or orthostatic hypotension; sometimes also seen in syncope due to cardiac causes.
- Inconsistency in features/history (psychogenic)

# o Post-event:

- Presence of transient focal weakness or numbness (suggestive of TIA)
- Any abnormal examination findings should prompted further evaluation

# • Predominantly loss of balance or onset of weakness

o Pre-event: There may be specific triggers such as

- Symptoms of vertigo
- Day-dreaming/not being mindful
- Has risk factors for hypoglycemia

# o Event

- The child usually able to recall the event (most of the differentials)
- Movements may be

☐ Tics (repetitive movements usually involving parts of the body shoulders and above)

☐ Inconsistent (psychogenic seizures)

☐ Twisting (movement disorders, e.g., dystonia or choreoathetosis)

- Distractible (psychogenic)
- Inconsistency (psychogenic)

# Post-event

- Presence of transient focal weakness or numbness (suggestive of TIA)

# Predominantly motor movements

# Pre-event

- Occurring soon after feeding (Sandifer syndrome)
- Occurring after breathlessness/rapid breathing with peripheral numbness (hyperventilation)
- Occurring with a cold environment (tremors, shuddering)
- Occurring before a spike of fever (chills, rigors)
- Occurring when falling asleep or during sleep (sleep myoclonus)

# Event

- Movements may be

- Twisting (movement disorders, e.g., dystonia or choreoathetosis)
- Jerks (myoclonic jerks)
- In lateral planes (physiological movements, e.g., rigors, tremors, shuddering)
- Inconsistent (psychogenic seizures)
- Stopped with restraint (physiological movements) or distraction (psychogenic seizures, self-gratification/masturbation, etc.)

# 6. What Were the Physical Examination Findings?

- There are many potential constellations of findings.
- The ones listed below are the more common ones but the list is not exhaustive.
- Hemodynamic changes (can occur in orthostatic hypotension, hypotension, hypovolemia, arrhythmia).
- Hypoxia (may be the cause or the result of the event).
- Signs of raised intracranial pressure (high blood pressure, bradycardia, altered mental state, papilloedema, sixth nerve palsy, hypertonia, hyper-reflexia, abnormal posturing).
- Signs of CNS infection (neck stiffness, altered conscious state, fever, abnormal CNS examination).
- An abnormal head size (microcephaly, macrocephaly) may suggest the existence of a neurological condition.
- Developmental delay or abnormal neurological examination suggests a neurological cause.
- Neurocutaneous stigmata, e.g., café au lait spots, ash leaf spots, portwine stain over the cranial nerve V nerve distribution. These features increase the risks of an underlying epilepsy.
- Dysmorphism suggests the possibility of a genetic condition.
- Abnormal mental state examination (one should look for an underlying cause before attributing it to a mental health issue)

- Hyperventilating the child up to 3 min triggered an absence seizure (blank staring with loss of awareness confirmed with inability to recall a test word). This simple clinical examination should be done in anyone with concerns of absence seizures.
- Abnormal cardiac examination may suggest a risk for cardiogenic syncope.
- Hepatosplenomegaly (see Chapter 24)
- Unusual odor (Inborn error of metabolism, drugs, poisons, substance abuse, alcohol)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Secondary causes of seizures (e.g., CNS infection, CNS space-occupying lesion, CNS bleed, head injury, hypoglycemia, hypoxia)
- Increasing tempo in seizures
- Status epilepticus
- Developmental regression
- Abnormal neurological examination
- Symptoms and signs suggestive of hypoglycemia
- Symptoms and signs suggestive of TIAs
- Brief Resolved Unexplained Event (see Chapter 13)
- History of arrhythmia or condition that predisposes to arrhythmia
- Family history that predisposes to arrhythmia
- Symptoms of chest pain, palpitations, loss of consciousness with physical exertion, or hemodynamic abnormalities (suggestive of a cardiogenic cause, e.g., arrhythmias, structural cardiac lesion)
- Patients with syncope and who do not show quick recovery of consciousness after being placed in a recumbent position (look for another cause of syncope other than vasovagal syncope)

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 1.5-year-old boy presents with his second episode of loss of consciousness.

He is the first child of nonconsanguineous parents, born at full term, and with no family history of epilepsy. He has been well with no medical issues and is neurodevelopmentally so far.

His first episode of loss of consciousness occurred 2 months ago.

Pre-event, he was well with no concurrent fever or illness. His mother reported that he was throwing a tantrum with prolonged crying when he gradually fell to the ground with loss of responsiveness, stiffness of his four limbs, and uprolling of his eyes. His mother noticed that he was "blue-looking" right from the onset of the event. The episode lasted less than 30 seconds and was spontaneously aborted. Following the event, he was drowsy and slept for an hour. His mother brought him to a doctor who diagnosed a seizure event and ordered an electroencephalogram (EEG). The EEG demonstrated focal abnormalities in the centrotemporal regions and the child was diagnosed as having Benign Rolandic Epilepsy. No medications were started.

His second episode of loss of consciousness occurred today. Again, he had been well with no fever or illness. Mother reported the boy was very upset about being deprived of a toy. He had been crying for the past 10 minutes and he then was seen to have held his breath for a while and turned blue in his lips. He then fell to the ground with loss of consciousness associated with stiffening and jerking of limbs as well as uprolling of eyes. The event lasted less than 10 seconds. Post-event, he was drowsy and slept for an hour. He was rousable and did recognize his mother.

Further systemic questioning reviewed a picky eater but no other concerns.

On examination, the boy is well thrived and not dysmorphic. His vital signs were normal with an oxygen saturation of 100% in room air. He is not pale. There are no neurocutaneous stigmata. His neurological and cardiac examinations were normal. The rest of the examination was normal. He is developmentally appropriate.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

What is the clinical problem?
What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

What is the clinical problem?

- Recurrent short episodes of loss of awareness with stiffening and jerking of limbs, occurring after prolonged crying

What is your diagnosis?

- Provoked seizure (anoxia due to a breath-holding cyanotic spell)

List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Pre-event

- Consistent provoking event of prolonged crying with breath-holding
Cyanosis preceding the loss of consciousness (transient brain anoxia)

• Subsequent symptoms consistent with a seizure event

■ Complete loss of consciousness
■ Tonic-clonic movements
■ Post-ictal drowsiness

• Otherwise neurodevelopmentally normal child with no family history of epilepsy.
• This child does not have epilepsy as his seizures, though recurrent, were both provoked.

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

• An abnormal EEG. However, the EEG abnormalities found in this case do not explain the events. The EEG may be a red herring.

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

• There was no information provided on his growth, especially his head circumference. It would be good to have it as we expect it to be normal if the diagnosis is indeed anoxia related.

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

• Another type of anoxic reflex seizure (pallid spells).
• Hypercyanotic attack due to underlying congenital heart disease.

• List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

• Pallid spells have preceding events resulting in the child appearing pale prior to the loss of consciousness. The mechanism of action involves a typically unpleasant event triggering an excessive vagal or parasympathetic response resulting in transient brain anoxia.
• There was cyanosis prior to the event. Congenital cyanotic cardiac conditions like Tetralogy of Fallot can cause similar blue spells (usually without seizures).

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

• Pallid spells in older children.
• He did not have any cardiac murmur.

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

• No mention about whether the child had signs of clubbing or plethora to suggest chronic hypoxia as part of Tetralogy of Fallot

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

• No

• What is your plan for this patient?

• Check for iron deficiency as this is associated with breath-holding cyanotic spells and treatment has shown to reduce frequency of such attacks
• Give parents education on tantrum management, which should reduce event recurrence.

# Reference

Pediatrics. 2016 Aug;138(2). pii: e20161488. doi: 10.1542/peds.2016-1488.

# Floppy Baby or Child

Furene Wang • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF A FLOPPY BABY OR CHILD

- A floppy baby or child usually refers to a patient who has hypotonia.
- Hypotonia is defined as reduced muscle tone, or a decreased resistance of the muscle to passive movement.

# SYMPTOMS OF A FLOPPY BABY OR CHILD

Symptoms of hypotonia/floppiness include

- Presence of abnormal postures such as frog-legged posture when lying supine.
- Difficulty in carrying the child because the child slips through the caregiver's hands when held under the arms.
- Symptoms of delay in achieving the gross motor milestones.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Hypotonia or floppiness can be due to a central nervous system cause, a peripheral nervous system cause or other causes.

# 1. Central Causes

- A central cause is due to a lesion in the central nervous system (CNS) (also known as the upper motor neuron unit).
- This can be

- In the CNS above the spinal cord (pyramidal neuron in the motor cortex, myelinated nerve fiber, corticospinal tract).
- In the CNS at the spinal cord just before the level of the anterior horn cell.
- Pathology in the upper motor neuron unit can present as increased or decreased muscle tone; this depends on when the nature of the pathology and when the pathology occurred.
- In some central causes, e.g., Down syndrome, the hypotonia is persistent.
- In others, e.g., spinal cord injury, hypotonia is present during the acute phase, and over time, the hypotonia will evolve gradually to hypertonia.

- Neuroanatomical location: central nervous system (CNS) above the level of spinal cord. The causes include

- Genetic: Down syndrome, Prader–Willi syndrome, other chromosomal anomalies, single gene disorder, etc.
- Vascular: acute phase of hypoxic ischemic encephalopathy, acute phase of stroke
- Infection: acute phase of meningoencephalitis, acute phase of intrauterine (TORCH) infections

331

- Trauma: acute phase of brain injury (concussion, contusion, intracranial hemorrhage)
- Malformation: intracranial neuronal migration defects
- Endocrine: hypothyroidism, hypoglycemia
- Metabolic: hyperammonemia, hyperglycinemia
- Drug toxicity: phenytoin, phenobarbitone

- Neuroanatomical location: CNS at the level of the spinal cord. The causes include

- Malformation (acute phase)
- Trauma (acute phase)
- Cord compression by space-occupying lesion (acute phase)
- Autoimmune/inflammatory: transverse myelitis (acute phase)

## 2. Peripheral Causes

- A peripheral cause is due to a lesion in the lower motor neuron unit.

- This can be at the level of the anterior horn cell, the peripheral nerve, the neuromuscular junction, the muscle.

- Causes

- Anterior horn cell: spinal muscular atrophy (SMA), poliomyelitis
- Peripheral nerve roots and nerves (demyelinating or axonal): Guillain–Barré syndrome, hereditary motor-sensory neuropathies
- Neuromuscular junction: myasthenia gravis, botulism
- Muscle: muscular dystrophies, congenital myopathies, inflammatory myositis, metabolic myopathies

## 3. Other Causes

- An unwell child, for example, due to sepsis from sources outside of the CNS or cardiac failure, can be hypotonic.
- Thus, one should reassess the tone after recovery from these causes to see if the hypotonia is persistent.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

### 1. History Taking: Is There Really Hypotonia?

- Ask for the symptoms
- The following symptoms in the history are suggestive of hypotonia:

- Presence of abnormal postures such as frog-legged posture when lying supine
- Difficulty in carrying the child as the child slips through the caregiver's hands when held under the arms
- Symptoms of delay in achieving the gross motor milestones

### 2. History Taking: Determine the Neuroanatomical Level of the Lesion

- Ask about the distribution, strength, other areas affected, sensation, responsiveness, cognitive ability, and other developmental milestones.
- Use Table 31-1 to determine the anatomical level.

TABLE 31-1

NEUROANATOMICAL LOCALIZATION OF THE LEVEL OF THE LESION BASED ON SYMPTOMS

|  Clinical Feature | CNS above the Spinal Cord | CNS at the Level of the Spinal Cord | Peripheral Cause  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Distribution | Generalized | Depends on where the level of the lesion is; may be generalized or not generalized | Depends on where the level of the lesion is; may be generalized or not generalized  |
|  Strength | Normal or may have mild weakness | Significant weakness; distribution corresponds to the level of the lesion | Significant weakness; distribution corresponds to the level of the lesion  |
|  Other areas of weakness | May have mild axial weakness (if any) | May involve the bladder and bowel | May involve bulbar, respiratory, and oculomotor muscles  |
|  Sensation | Not affected | May be affected | May be affected  |
|  Responsiveness or cognition | Often impaired | Not affected | Not affected  |
|  Other developmental milestones | Usually associated with global developmental delay | Gross motor and fine motor delay affecting areas below the level of the lesion | Variable effects on gross motor and fine motor milestones depending on the cause and which nerves are affected  |

### 3. When the History Is Suggestive of a Central Cause Above the Spinal Cord

- When was the onset?

- Features that suggest possible antenatal onset: decreased fetal movements, polyhydramnios, breach position.
- Weak from birth?
- Weak only in the later part of life?
- Was it after an identifiable event?

- Is it static or progressive?

- Look for the cause

- Family history
- Antenatal history (maternal health and fetal health)
- Perinatal history
- Trauma
- Hypoxia, ischemia
- CNS infection
- Endocrine: hypothyroidism, hypoglycemia
- Metabolic: hyperammonemia, hyperglycinemia
- Drug toxicity: phenytoin, phenobarbitone

- Assess for associated complications

- Is there difficulty in sucking, chewing, or swallowing?
- Is there choking?

1

- Any prior respiratory infection (atelectasis, aspiration)?
- Is there weakness of respiratory muscle and/or risk for respiratory distress/failure?
- Seizures

### 4. When the History Is Suggestive of a Central Cause at the Level of the Spinal Cord

# • When was the onset?

- Features that suggest possible antenatal onset: decreased fetal movements, polyhydramnios, breach position.
- Weak from birth?
- Weak only in the later part of life?
- Was it after an identifiable event?

# • Is it static or progressive?

# • Look for the cause

- Family history
- Antenatal history
- Perinatal history
- Malformation
- Trauma
- Neoplasm
- Autoimmune/inflammatory: transverse myelitis

# • Assess associated complications

- Is there difficulty in sucking, chewing, or swallowing?
- Is there choking?
- Any prior respiratory infection (think about atelectasis, aspiration)?
- Is there weakness of respiratory muscle and/or risk for respiratory distress/failure?
- Bowel and bladder function
- Skin breakdown, sores

### 5. When the History Is Suggestive of a Peripheral Cause

# • When was the onset?

- Features that suggest possible antenatal onset: decreased fetal movements, polyhydramnios, breach position.
- Weak from birth?
- Weak only in the later part of life?
- Was it after an identifiable event?

# • Is it static or progressive?

# • Is there fatigability?

# • Look for the cause

- Family history
- Antenatal history
- Perinatal history
- Autoimmune/inflammatory

1

# • Assess associated complications

• Is there difficulty in sucking, chewing, or swallowing?
• Is there choking?
• Any prior respiratory infection (atelectasis, aspiration)?
• Is there weakness of respiratory muscle and/or risk for respiratory distress/failure?
• Skin breakdown, sores

# 6. Physical Examination

# • General inspection

• Microcephaly or dysmorphism suggests a central cause
• A "frog-legged" posture

# • Neurological examination

# • Tone

■ Assess the child in both the active and restful states. Tone is usually higher when the child is awake and active as compared to when at rest.
■ The decreased resistance to passive movement may result in an increased range of movement of the joints.
■ The "slip sign" refers to a young child slipping through an examiner's hands when held up from under the arms. He or she may adopt a rag doll posture when held in horizontal suspension and facing downwards. When held to sit, there may be difficulty keeping the head in an upright posture. The hypotonic infant's arm will be easily held across the chest toward the neck on the contralateral side, beyond the midline, consistent with the "scarf sign."

# • Power

■ Grade the power of the child.
■ Assess the facial muscles, extraocular muscles, and look for any ptosis.
■ Weakness may also cause the child to have difficulties in sucking and swallowing.
■ A weak cry may reveal bulbar weakness.

# • Reflexes

■ A CNS disorder affecting the brain would have normal or brisk deep tendon reflexes and usually no or mild weakness.
■ A central lesion at the level of the spinal cord would usually result in decreased reflexes.
■ The effect of a neuromuscular disorder on the deep tendon reflexes is dependent on the etiology (see algorithm).

• Look at tongue and muscles: atrophy or fasciculations are seen in anterior horn cell disease.

• Sensation: there may be loss of sensation in spinal cord and peripheral neuropathies.

# • Developmental assessment

• For premature infants, assessment should be according to the corrected and not chronological age.

• Is the child cognitively impaired?

- A child with a central cause above the spinal cord is more likely to have cognitive impairment.
- A child with a lesion at the spinal cord or a peripheral cause of hypotonia should have normal cognition and appear bright.
- A child with ptosis or facial weakness may appear “dull looking” but may have normal cognition.

• Examine the rest of the body to look for complications and the cause.

○ Eyes for exposure keratitis, cataracts, retinal changes
○ Respiratory system to look for evidence of respiratory failure, poor respiratory effect, aspiration pneumonia
○ Cardiovascular system to look for evidence of heart failure
○ Abdomen to look for organomegaly
○ Skin for sores, rashes

![img-4.jpeg](img-4.jpeg)

7. Algorithm

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

The child with hypotonia and progressive symptoms, especially weakness:

- May be at risk for respiratory distress or failure
- May be at risk for swallowing difficulties and aspiration
- May be at risk for cardiac failure
- May need urgent evaluation for treatable causes, for example:

- Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) for Guillain-Barré syndrome

- Plasma exchange, IVIG and/or steroid for myasthenic crisis

Special attention is needed during anesthesia for children with neuromuscular disorders:

- Muscle relaxants may have more profound or prolonged effect.
- They may be prone to malignant hypothermia precipitated by neuromuscular blockers and propofol.

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 2-week-old neonate has been transferred to the Pediatrics Intensive Care Unit from a private hospital for further workup and management of hypotonia.

He is the second child of a nonconsanguineous marriage. His 2-year-old brother is well. There is no family history of neuromuscular disorders.

His mother is 25 years of age and does not have any significant medical history. Antenatal scans showed polyhydramnios for unknown reasons. His mother was well during the pregnancy. There had been no febrile illness or rash. Screening for fetal anomalies was unremarkable.

He was delivered via natural vaginal delivery at 38 weeks of gestational age. His birth weight was 2.46 kg (10th percentile), length 51 cm (50th percentile), and head circumference 35 cm (50th percentile). He was floppy at birth with a weak cry. Apgar scores were 5 at 1 min and 7 at 5 min of life. He was intubated in view of poor respiratory effort.

Attempts had been made to extubate him but failed in view of respiratory distress after extubation. He has been fed via a nasogastric tube and is tolerating his feeds well.

On examination, he is intubated and awake. He is afebrile, blood pressure 70/45 mmHg, and heart rate 130/min. His oxygen saturation is 100% on an FiO₂ of 21%. There is mild wasting at the temporal areas bilaterally. The pupils are 3 mm, equal, and reactive. There is no ptosis. The anterior fontanelle is normotensive. He is lying supine and has a frog-legged posture at the lower limbs. There are some antigravity movements at the elbow and fingers bilaterally. No movements at the shoulder. No spontaneous movement of the lower limbs is seen. No fasciculations in limbs or tongue. Deep tendon reflexes are 1+ (present but decreased) at the biceps, triceps, supinators, knees, and ankles. There are no joint contractures. Plantar responses are equivocal. There is no ankle clonus.

He does not have a bell-shaped chest and has good chest rise with the ventilator-assisted breaths. There are vesicular breath sounds. Heart rate is regular. The apex beat is not displaced first, and second heart sounds are heard and normal. There are no additional heart sounds. The liver is palpable 2 cm below the right subcostal margin. There is no splenomegaly. No rashes. His genitalia are normal. His peripheries are warm. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds.

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- A floppy neonate with significant weakness

- What is your diagnosis?

- Peripheral cause of hypotonia; likely a muscular disorder (myopathy)

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Appears bright and looking around
- Significant weakness
- Respiratory weakness
- Proximal pattern of weakness suggesting a muscular disorder
- Decreased deep tendon reflexes

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Poor Apgar scores may suggest birth asphyxia, which is a central cause of hypotonia.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information provided on whether the newborn's alertness or responsiveness improved after stabilization of the respiratory status (lethargy or hyperalertness may represent mild hypoxic ischemic encephalopathy).
- No information about progress over the past 2 weeks, especially in terms of the severity of weakness.

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

SMA
- Neonatal myasthenia gravis

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support and/or go against the alternative diagnoses.

SMA:

Children with SMA can have proximal pattern of weakness.
- However, the deep tendon reflexes should be absent. Fasciculation is usually present.

- Neonatal myasthenia gravis:

The clinical findings would be similar to those of this patient.
■ Ptosis and extraocular muscle weakness may also be present.
There may be varying muscle weakness depending on the time of day

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Likely a muscular disorder (myopathy)

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Evidence of severe weakness of respiratory muscles needing ventilator support
- Likely difficulty in sucking and swallowing but unable to determine as he remains ventilated

• What is your plan for this patient?

- Referral to a neurologist.
- Determine the etiology of hypotonia and weakness.
- Check creatine kinase level.
- Genetic testing for SMA.
- Electromyography (EMG) and nerve conduction studies (NCS) can be considered but technically difficult in a very young infant.
- Conduct muscle biopsy (these investigations can be done on a step-wise approach).
- Optimize nutrition via tube feeding or gastrostomy approach.
- Refer to the pulmonologist for management of respiratory and ventilation requirements.
- Provide regular physiotherapy to prevent contractures.
- Ensure vaccinations completed, including influenza vaccinations, as these children have little reserves when unwell from respiratory infections.
- Suggest genetic counseling, if found to be a hereditary condition.
- The diagnosis of myopathy was confirmed by muscle biopsy performed.

# Frequent Infections

Soh Jian Yi • Denise L.M. Goh • Lynette P. Shek

# DEFINITION OF FREQUENT INFECTIONS

- "Frequent infections" refers to the situation where a person is perceived to have more infections than the average human being in that age group.
- There are no fixed norms as to what is a normal number or rate of infections. The normal number or rate is in part dependent on environmental exposure and the seasonal variation of infections. For example, it is normal for a school going kid to have more than six viral infections when starting school.
- There are some suggested guidelines as to when one might need to worry that the frequency of infections is not normal (reference: Jerry Modell Foundation for Immunodeficiency):

- Four or more ear infections within 12 months
- Two or more sinus infections within 12 months
- Two or more pneumonias within 12 months
- Two or more deep-seated infections, e.g., meningitis, cholangitis, septicemia
- Recurrent deep skin or organ abscesses
- Two proven episodes of urinary tract infection within 6 months, or three within a year

# SYMPTOMS OF FREQUENT INFECTIONS

- More frequent infections than peers or other family members.
- The sites of infection can be always the same or different with each episode of infection.
- The symptoms of the infection may be similar to other people or may be more protracted (e.g., take longer to recover), or more serious (e.g., disseminated disease) compared to other people.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

Factors predisposing to frequent infections include

- Environmental factors
- Structural abnormality and/or obstruction
- Foreign body in situ
- Other systemic condition apart from immunodeficiency
- Immunodeficiency (primary or secondary)

These factors are elaborated below

341

# 1. Environmental Factors

- Poor air quality
- High air humidity
- Poor water sanitation
- Poor water hygiene
- Contaminated food supply
- Poor food hygiene
- Unhygienic food utensils, e.g., baby bottles, pots, bowls, cups
- Poor hand hygiene
- Crowded living conditions
- High population density
- Daycare/kindergarten/school-going age
- Siblings/household contacts
- Epidemiology of infective agents, e.g., malaria in the tropics, seasonal influenza infection
- Health care policies, e.g., immunization programs

# 2. Structural Abnormality and/or Obstruction

- Each tissue type and organ in the human body has a defined structure that protects against the entry or presence of pathogens. Examples include the outer fibrous layer of organs, the motile cilia in the respiratory tract that helps sweep potential pathogens away.
- Any abnormality in these areas can predispose to frequent infection. The abnormalities can be congenital, or acquired. Congenital causes often manifest soon after birth. Acquired causes are usually secondary to another factor, and tend to manifest later on life.
- Examples of structural abnormality/obstruction and their related infections:

- CNS: congenital cranial and spinal cord defects, congenital inner ear malformations, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) fistula after skull fracture → frequent meningitis
- Respiratory: bronchial cyst, cystic fibrosis, immotile cilia syndrome, bronchial compression due to an external mass → frequent lower respiratory tract infections
- Urological: vesico-ureteric reflux, posterior urethral valve, ureteropelvic junction obstruction → frequent urinary tract infections (UTI)
- Upper airway: Eustachian tube dysfunction, tonsillar and/or adenoidal hypertrophy → frequent otitis media
- Skin: dermatitis → frequent staphylococcal skin infections

# 3. Foreign Body In Situ

- Prosthetic device in situ, e.g., ventriculoperitoneal (VP) shunt, artificial heart valve, stent
- Medical device in situ, e.g., urinary catheter, intravenous line
- Other foreign bodies

# 4. Other Systemic Condition apart from Immunodeficiency

- Diabetes mellitus
- Staphylococcus aureus carrier
- Any form of dermatitis
- Swallowing dysfunction
- Gastroesophageal reflux disease
- Allergic rhinitis
- Tonsillar and adenoid hypertrophy/enlargement
- Cystic fibrosis
- Immotile cilia syndrome

# 5. Immunodeficiency (Primary or Secondary)

- Each component of the immune system, such as a type of white blood cell or protein, is part of one or more protective responses against infection.
- Loss of the component or an abnormal component of the immune system may impair that protective response and hence predisposes the individual to infections that would otherwise be cleared by the immune response.
- Immunodeficiencies can be classified into primary or secondary. Secondary immunodeficiencies are more common than primary.

- Primary immunodeficiency:

- Humoral: X-linked agammaglobulinemia, common variable immunodeficiency
- Combined: severe combined immunodeficiency, DiGeorge syndrome
- Phagocyte: chronic granulomatous disease
- Others

- Secondary immunodeficiency:

- Human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infection
- Malnutrition
- Malignancy
- Chemotherapy, immunosuppressive therapy, e.g., steroids, tacrolimus, rituximab
- Chronic inflammatory diseases such as systemic lupus erythematosus, rheumatoid arthritis
- Protein loss, e.g., protein losing enteropathy, nephrotic syndrome
- Asplenia (congenital or acquired)

- Patients with immunodeficiency

- Can get the same kinds of infections immunocompetent people get, e.g., ear infections, sinusitis, pneumonia.
- Have a risk that their infections occur more frequently, are often more severe, and have a greater risk of complications.
- Can get frequent infections at the same sites or at different sites, i.e., affect only one body system or more than one body system.

- The infective agent may be a common pathogen or an unusual pathogen.
- Manifestations are dependent on the component(s) affected and severity of the impairment of the component(s).

- Humoral: sinopulmonary infections, e.g., otitis media, sinusitis, pneumonia, diarrhea
- Combined: sinopulmonary infections, unusual/opportunistic infections
- Phagocyte: skin, respiratory tract and deep tissue infections, including abscesses
- Failure to thrive: commonly seen in many immunodeficiencies

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History Taking: Establish the Presence, Severity, Frequency, and Location of Infections

- Take a detailed history of each infection (see Chapter 29 as needed).
- Establish the presence, severity, frequency, type and location of each infection.
- Obtain information about investigation, treatment, response to treatment, etc.

# 2. History Taking: Obtain Information about Predisposing Causes of Frequent Infections as well as the Consequences on the Patient

- Predisposing cause

- Based on the symptoms, look for a predisposing cause (see Table 32-1).
- If there is a suspicion that the predisposing cause is an immunodeficiency (primary or secondary), see Table 32-2. Determine the level of the immune defect based on the pathogens involved.

|  Frequent streptococcus throat infection | • Streptococcus carrier in throat | No  |

- Current medication use, especially any immunosuppressant.
- Systems review.
- Environmental factors
- Family history of frequent infections, death from infections, consanguinity.

• Consequences

- Growth
- Development

1

- Threat to life
- Quality of life, e.g., hospitalization, missing school, psychosocial stress on the individual, family, and caregiver, financial burden

### 3. Examine the Patient for Clues to the Cause, Existing Infections, and Complications

- A head-to-toe physical examination is needed
- Look for signs of existing infections

- Fever
- Hemodynamic status
- Site of infection

- Look for complications as a result of frequent infections

- Failure to thrive
- Developmental delay
- Organ damage, e.g., bronchiectasis, chronic kidney disease, obstructive hydrocephalus
- Psychosocial effect on individual, caregiver, family, etc.

- Look for clues to the cause of frequent infections. Possible clues include

- Head, Ear, Eyes, Nose, Throat: nasal polyp, swollen turbinate, serous otitis media, enlarged tonsils, oral hygiene, gingivitis, caries
- Dysmorphic features
- Drooling, swallowing issues
- Clubbing
- Lungs: evidence of chronic lung disease, pulmonary edema
- Heart: murmur
- Abdomen: hepatomegaly, splenomegaly, ballotable kidney, enlarged bladder
- Spine: evidence of spinal dysraphism
- Bone/joints: evidence of infection
- Superficial lymph nodes throughout the body; can be enlarged as a common feature in many immunodeficiency disorders and in malignancy
- Foreign body in situ, e.g., medical device
