4. Algorithm

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Septic shock
- Hemodynamic instability
- Primary or secondary immunodeficiency
- Diabetes mellitus
- Failure to thrive

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 2-year-old boy presents with an abscess on the trunk. This is the fourth such abscess in the last year. The first two occurred on the scalp. He required incision and drainage by the surgeons, followed by a course of oral antibiotics. The cultures at both these episodes grew S. aureus. The third episode was a perianal abscess that occurred at 8 months of age. This has not totally resolved, despite treatment, and is now a fistula for which he is still receiving care.

He was born at full term via normal vaginal delivery, with a birth weight of 3 kg. His mother's HIV test was negative. He was well antenatally and perinatally, and discharged well from the hospital soon after birth. The neonatal screening was normal. The umbilical cord dropped off at 1 week of age. He was on full breastfeeding and started on weaning solids at 6 months of age, without incident. His vaccinations are up to date, and he had no problems with any of them. He is not on any conventional or traditional long-term medication.

He has been on follow-up with the polyclinic, and his height, weight, and occipital–frontal circumference were previously on the 50th percentile and are now trending between the 25th and 50th percentile lines. He is able to climb stairs with support, 2 feet per stair. He can say three-word phrases to indicate his wants, feeds himself with a spoon (messily), and tries to help with wearing shoes. He can stack up to three blocks in a tower, and scribbles.

He is the first child of nonconsanguineous parents. He has one maternal uncle, two maternal aunts, and one paternal uncle. He has three cousins from both maternal aunts. His grandparents, aunts and uncles, parents, and cousins are all healthy with no history of recurrent infections.

On examination, his temperature is 38.1°C, heart rate 112/min, respiratory rate 21/min, blood pressure 94/55 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 99%.

His height, weight, and occipital–frontal circumference are between the 25th and 50th percentile lines. He is not dysmorphic, appears alert, and is comfortable. His nails and hair appear normal. He has a fistula at the 3 o'clock position at the perianal region that is not discharging. There is a 3 × 2 cm firm, erythematous, warm, fluctuant swelling over the left flank; there is no punctum.

He is not pale or jaundiced. Pupils are equal and reactive. His throat, nose, and ears are normal. There is no oral thrush or ulcers. There is no lymphadenopathy.

Heart rate is regular. On auscultation, S1 and S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds.

On auscultation of the lungs, there were vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft and nontender. There is no mass felt. The liver is felt 1 cm below the right costal margin. Bowel sounds are normal. No organomegaly is noted. His peripheries are warm. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds. His genitalia are normal. Musculoskeletal examination is normal. Neurological examination is normal.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Recurrent skin abscesses in various locations
- Has a current abscess with fever

- What is your diagnosis?

- Primary immunodeficiency disorder, likely involving phagocytes

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Many skin abscesses – consistent with "severe infections"
- In different locations – so less likely a structural cause
- Affecting the skin only = phagocytic disorder
- Absence of infections in other tissues (e.g., meningitis or urinary tract infection) that would suggest another arm of primary immunodeficiency

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis

- None. The absence of a family history of immunodeficiency or failure to thrive does not exclude immunodeficiency.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

o None

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

o Carrier of S. aureus (e.g., Panton-Valentine leukocidin [PVL] positive S. aureus) who is reinoculating self and getting abscesses

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

o Skin abscesses and perianal abscesses that are growing staphylococcus

o Areas infected are all within easy reach of scratching

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o The staphylococcus infections are usually folliculitis or cellulitis rather than abscesses.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

o Nose swab for culture; culture will show staphylococcal carriage

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

o No evidence of sepsis or shock.

o His growth is trending downward slightly. This may be due to a chronic illness or catch-down growth or constitutional growth delay.

- What is your plan for this patient?

o Perform blood cultures.

o Start empirical antibiotics: intravenous cloxacillin, based on typical organisms causing skin abscess as well as knowledge of previous culture results.

o Urgent referral to an immunologist.

o Referral to surgeons.

o Monitor child serially; if the child becomes unwell, consider further investigations and treatment as warranted.

# Frequent Urination

Yap H.K. • Ng K.H.

# DEFINITION OF FREQUENT URINATION

- Frequent urination is passing urine more often than usual.
- The normal number of times varies according to the age of the person. Among young children up to 3 years old, urinating 8–12 times each day is typical. This decreases to 6–8 times per day for older children, and to 4–6 times per day among teenagers.

# SYMPTOMS ASSOCIATED WITH FREQUENT URINATION

# Symptoms

- Passing urine more than 8 times a day, often every 10–30 minutes. Each void can be small volume or large volume of urine. This can be associated with thirst or polydipsia.
- Passing urine more often than peers.
- Getting up one or more times a night to pass urine (nocturia).
- Bed wetting (nocturnal enuresis).

The above may be associated with the voiding-related symptoms listed below

- Unexpected immediate need to void (urgency)
- Difficulty in initiating voiding (hesitancy)
- "Burning" sensation in the perineum/urethra during voiding (dysuria)
- Holding maneuvers to postpone voiding including crossing of legs and squatting with a hand pressing the perineum in girls
- Post-micturition dribbling

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Normal Physiology

- Urine is produced by the kidneys.
- The amount of urine produced is dependent on the following factors:
  - Prerenal: how much perfusion there is to the kidney
  - Renal: renal function, renal response to antidiuretic hormone (ADH), presence of osmotically active substance

- Urine is then stored in the bladder.

- Normal bladder function involves storage of urine at low pressure and emptying it at a socially acceptable time. This involves the following

- Normal anatomy of urethra, bladder, and ureters
- Accommodation by the detrusor in increasing volumes of urine at low pressure with normal sensation
- Closed bladder outlet despite increases in abdominal pressure

353

- Absence of involuntary bladder contractions
- Normal neural control of the bladder (involves the brain, spinal cord, and peripheral ganglia)

- Autonomic control is mediated by sympathetic and parasympathetic nerves.
- Somatic control is mediated by pudendal nerves.
- Sympathetic innervation arises from the thoracolumbar spinal cord.
- Parasympathetic and somatic innervation arises from the sacral segments of the spinal cord.

- The child learns over time to be continent and to establish normal voiding patterns

- The age at which continence and normal voiding patterns is achieved varies greatly in children.
- Developmental aspects of bladder control:

- Less than 1 year old:

- Voids about once an hour.
- Do not always void in a coordinated fashion due to contraction of pelvic floor musculature.

- As children get older:

- Bladder capacity increases.
- Most void 3 to 8 times per day.
- Ability to empty improves.
- Voids uninterrupted by 3 years of age.

## 2. Pathophysiology of Frequent Urination

- Frequent urination due to polyuria

- Increased prerenal load

- Increased intravascular volume, e.g., drinking a lot of fluids (behavioral polydipsia), given a lot of intravenous fluids

- Renal cause (inability to concentrate urine)

- Inability of renal tubules to concentrate, e.g., chronic kidney disease
- Inability of renal tubes to respond to ADH, e.g., nephrogenic diabetes insipidus
- Lack of ADH, e.g., central diabetes insipidus
- Osmotically active substance present in the renal ultrafiltrate, e.g., glucosuria seen in uncontrolled diabetes mellitus (DM), sodium and potassium chloride in Bartter and Gitelman syndrome, sodium bicarbonate in renal tubular acidosis
- Drugs, e.g., diuretics

- Frequent urination due to bladder dysfunction

- Failure to store urine adequately or failure to empty completely

- Due to a neurogenic bladder

- Neurospinal dysraphism (myelomeningocele, lipomeningocele, tethered cord)
- Diastematomyelia

☐ Sacral agenesis
☐ Cerebral palsy
☐ Spinal trauma
☐ Spinal tumor

■ Due to a functional cause

☐ Primary bladder neck dysfunction (incomplete opening of the bladder neck during voiding)

■ Due to anatomical cause

☐ Posterior urethral valves
☐ Ureterocele
☐ Bladder wall tumor

■ Manifestations

☐ Hesitancy, weak urinary stream, pelvic pain or discomfort during voiding, sense of incomplete emptying; these symptoms are suggestive of neurogenic bladder or primary bladder neck dysfunction.
☐ May be associated with overflow incontinence.
☐ May be associated with post-micturition dribbling.
☐ Neurological signs in lower limbs suggestive of lumbosacral involvement.
☐ Cutaneous signs in the sacral region such as sacral dimple or tuft of hair suggest the possibility of a neurogenic bladder.
☐ Constipation or fecal soiling suggestive of neurogenic bladder or functional cause.

○ Overactive bladder/irritation to trigone

■ Organic causes

☐ Cystitis
☐ Hypercalciuria

■ Functional causes

☐ Overactive bladder and urge incontinence
☐ Extraordinary daytime urinary frequency (pollakiuria)

■ Manifestations

☐ Urgency
☐ Wetting
☐ Holding maneuvers

○ Frequent urination due to other causes

■ Irritation of external genitalia (balanitis, vulvitis): may have pain on passing urine
■ Ectopic ureter: manifests as continuous dribbling of urine

![img-6.jpeg](img-6.jpeg)

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Is It Really Frequent Urination?

- Ask the patient/parent what they mean by frequent urination.
- Assess if their perception of frequent urination is normal for age or not.
- Frequent urination may be due to frequency or polyuria.
  - Frequency is defined as more than 8 voids during waking hours.
  - Polyuria is defined as urine volume > 2 L/1.73 m²/day or > 2–3 mL/kg/h.

# 2. Is the Frequent Urination due to Polyuria, Bladder Dysfunction or Other Causes?

- See Table 33-1. Assess the parameters listed to differentiate between polyuria and bladder dysfunction. If it is polyuria, go to step 4. If it is not polyuria, go to step 3.

# 3. If It Is Not Polyuria, Determine the Likely Cause

- History

- Ask for the associated symptoms.
- Is there dysuria? This is suggestive of cystitis, hypercalciuria, or irritation of external urinary meatus.
- Any history of recurrent urinary tract infections (UTI)?
- Is there hematuria? This is suggestive of UTI, hypercalciuria, bladder tumor.
- Are there symptoms of irritation of the external genitalia?
- Any discharge from the external genitalia? This is suggestive of UTI or irritation of external urinary meatus.
- Is there any voiding symptoms such as urgency or holding maneuvers? This is suggestive of overactive bladder.

TABLE 33-1

|  DIFFERENTIATING BETWEEN BLADDER DYSFUNCTION AND POLYURIA  |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Parameter | Bladder Dysfunction | Polyuria  |
|  Volume of urine passed per void | May be normal or small | May be normal or large  |
|  Wetting (day or night) | May be present | May be present  |
|  Nocturia | May be present | May be present  |
|  Polydipsia | Absent | Present  |
|  Voiding symptoms, e.g., hesitancy, urgency, dysuria, dribbling | Present | Absent  |
|  Constipation/fecal soiling | May be present | Absent  |
|  Sacral cutaneous signs | May be present | Absent  |
|  Neurological deficits | May be present | Absent  |
|  Psychosocial factors | May be present | Absent  |
|  Urine dipstick | May be positive for leukocytes and nitrites | May be positive for glucose, specific gravity may be low  |

o Is there wetting?

- Is the wetting continuous or intermittent? Continuous wetting suggests ectopic ureter, neurogenic cause or anatomical cause.
- Is the child wetting only during the day but is dry at night? This is suggestive of functional cause.

o Is the voiding volume small? Expected bladder capacity in millilitres = 30 + (30 x age in years). Voiding small volumes is suggestive of bladder dysfunction.

o Is there evidence of neurological deficits in the lower limb? This is suggestive of a neurogenic bladder.

o Is there any evidence of bowel incontinence with poor anal tone? This suggestive of a neurogenic cause.

o Is there any evidence of fecal sciling? This is suggestive of functional cause.

o Is there constipation? This is suggestive of a neurogenic cause or functional cause.

o Any lump or unusual skin feature over the lumbosacral region? This is suggestive of a neurogenic cause.

o Is there any psychosocial factor present at home, or in school?

o Is there a family history of stone disease? This may suggest hypercalciuria

o Look for the consequence of long-term bladder dysfunction, e.g., chronic kidney disease due to obstructive uropathy.

• Physical examination

o Vital signs

- Fever = suggestive of upper tract UTI
- Hypertension = suggestive of chronic kidney disease, pain, anxiety
- Hypotension = suggestive of shock, e.g., sepsis, dehydration

o Hydration status

o Abdominal examination. Look especially for ballotable kidneys (hydronephrosis), a palpable bladder (especially one that is present after voiding), the state of the external genitalia, anal tone, and anal reflex.

o Look at the spine (for evidence of spinal dysraphism).

o Look at the lower limbs (posture, muscle bulk).

o Full neurological examination.

o Look for evidence of chronic kidney disease (growth failure, anemia, rickets).

o Observe urinary stream if it is possible/socially acceptable.

# 4. If It Is Polyuria, Determine the Likely Cause

• History

o Quantify the amount of water drunk. Does the patient drink because the patient is thirsty?

o Determine if there is the ability to concentrate urine.

- If water intake is cut back, what happens to urine output? If the urine output decreases, then the concentrating ability is present, and the cause is an increased intravascular volume; if urine output does not decrease, then the cause is impaired concentrating ability.

- Any polyphagia? This is suggestive of DM.
- Any loss of weight or loss of appetite? This is suggestive of DM, renal tubular acidosis, chronic kidney disease.
- Any drug or alternative medication or substance like caffeine?
- Are there symptoms suggestive of chronic kidney disease?
- Any muscle weakness or cramps? This is suggestive of electrolyte abnormalities such as Bartter or Gitelman syndrome.
- Any symptom of a central nervous system issue, e.g., neurological deficit, symptoms of raised intracranial pressure?
- Any family history of DM or diabetes insipidus or renal disease?

- Physical examination

- Vital signs

- Drowsiness – consider diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA), other causes (see Chapter 5)
- Hypertension – consider chronic kidney disease, raised intracranial pressure, pain, anxiety
- Tachypnea – consider acidosis due to DKA or renal disease

- Hydration status

- Euvolemic or hypervolemic suggests increased intravascular volume.
- Dehydration suggests impaired concentrating ability of kidneys.

- Any wasting or cachexia? This is suggestive of DM or chronic renal failure.

- Any evidence of chronic kidney disease?

- Abdominal examination – any sign of obstructive uropathy causing chronic kidney disease?

- Full neurological examination – any sign of space-occupying lesion in the brain; spinal causes of long-standing neurogenic bladder

- Fundoscopy

- Hypertensive changes
- Papilledema (severe hypertension, raised intracranial pressure)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Hemodynamic instability
- Severe dehydration
- Severe hypertension
- Sepsis
- Evidence of diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA)
- Evidence of chronic kidney disease
- Evidence of obstructive uropathy

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 5-year-old girl sees you at the clinic for increased urinary frequency. Her mother says that for the last 3 weeks, she has been going to the toilet to void every 15–30 minutes, at least 8–10 times a day. The amount of urine voided each time is variable, sometimes consisting of only a few drops. She usually wets her underwear as she cannot get to the toilet in time. She does not complain of dysuria. She drinks about

1 L of fluids in the day, and always drinks a glass of milk prior to bedtime. She occasionally soils her underwear. Her mother has just given birth to a baby boy 2 months ago.

Clinical examination reveals a well-thrived child with height and weight in the 50th percentile. Blood pressure is normal. No masses are found on abdominal examination. No cutaneous abnormalities are seen in the lower back. Neurological examination of the lower limbs is normal. There is no redness in the vulval region. Urinalysis is normal, with no evidence of pyuria, hematuria, or proteinuria. Urine dipstick examination was negative for glucose, with a specific gravity of 1.015.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Frequent urination

- What is your diagnosis?

- Overactive bladder

- List the findings that support this diagnosis.

- Voids more than usual for age
- Not likely polyuria because variable amount of urine, sometimes drops only; absence of polydipsia also makes polyuria very unlikely
- Urinary frequency associated with voiding symptoms, namely, urgency and wetting
- Associated with fecal soiling
- Absence of symptoms of straining, hesitancy, or poor urinary stream; so not likely neurogenic bladder, anatomical cause or functional bladder neck obstruction
- Absence of sacral cutaneous signs or neurological signs in the lower limbs; Absence of abdominal masses to suggest hydronephrosis and enlarged bladder; so not likely neurogenic bladder

- Absence of signs of vulval irritation
- Absence of urine microscopic evidence of UTI

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Psychosocial factor – new sibling born to the family

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Information about her hydration status
- Information about when she was toilet trained
- Information about whether there is a change in her night urination pattern, e.g., any nocturnal enuresis, any nocturia, whether diapers are dry or overflowing if she wears diapers at night
- Information about whether she has associated urgency
- Information about whether she uses any holding maneuver, e.g.,

- Hand against perineum
- Squatting with heel against perineum
- Squatting position with legs crossed against perineum (Vincent's curtsy)

- Information about family history of renal stone disease

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Extraordinary daytime frequency

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Urinate small amounts of urine at times
- Does not drink excessive amounts of fluids.
- No evidence of infection. Normal physical examination
- Possibly triggered by psychosocial factors (new baby in the family)

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Extraordinary daytime frequency is usually not associated with voiding symptoms such as urgency and wetting.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Frequent urination occurs only during the waking hours.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Overactive bladder.
- But we have not found the cause of the overactive bladder. For example, is it functional? Or is there something irritating the trigone in particular cystitis? Is there a family history of renal stone disease?

- Is there any complication?

- No complications

- Is there any red flag symptom?

- No red flag symptoms in this child, especially that suggestive of obstructive uropathy

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Exclude urinary tract infection as a cause by doing a urine dipstick test, urine microscopy, and urine culture.
- Advise parents on bladder training:

- Keep a voiding dairy to chart the time of voids and episodes of wetting.
- Encourage timed voidings with progressive holding of void till 1 hour, eventually aiming for every 2–3 hours.
- Encourage complete voiding, including practicing double voiding.

• If symptoms persist after 2–3 months or there are associated recurrent urinary tract infections, consider referring to a pediatrician, pediatric nephrologist, or urologist to exclude frequency and organic causes of urgency.

- May require to send urine for calcium:creatinine ratio to exclude hypercalciuria
- May require ultrasound of the bladder and kidney to look for pelvicalyceal dilatation and bladder wall thickening
- May require urodynamic study
- May require treatment with anticholinergics such as oxybutynin

# Headache

# DEFINITION OF HEADACHE

Headache refers to pain in any area of the head. It affects children and adolescents and is one of the most common reasons for referral to Pediatric Neurology. The prevalence generally increases with age, with girls being more affected after puberty.

# SYMPTOMS OF HEADACHE

- Pain in the head.
- Younger children may not be able to describe or indicate pain.

- They may manifest with head hitting, squinting, tantrums, being upset, unable to carry out activities that he/she used to enjoy, etc.

- Please also note that these symptoms of head hitting, squinting, tantrums, being upset, and not able to enjoy activities may also be due to other problems apart from headache.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Headaches in children and adolescents are classified into primary and secondary headaches.
- Primary headaches are headaches that are "idiopathic," i.e., not due to a serious underlying disease or pathology. Primary headaches are likely the result of a complex interplay between genetic, developmental, and environmental risk factors. Examples are migraine headaches, tension-type headaches, and cluster headaches.
- Secondary headaches are headaches caused by a serious underlying disease or pathology. This can be intracranial or extracranial.
- A careful history and thorough physical examination is needed to differentiate between primary and secondary headaches.

# 1. Primary Headache

- The exact mechanism of primary headaches is not known.
- The diagnostic criteria for the various types of primary headaches, as defined by the International Headache Society (second edition), can be found at www.ichd-3.org

- Types of primary headaches

- Migraine

- Migraine without aura
- Migraine with aura
- Retinal migraine

363

- Childhood periodic symptoms that are commonly precursors of migraine (cyclical vomiting, abdominal migraine, etc.)
- Tension-type headache
- Cluster headache
- Others

- Notes about migraine

In younger children, the duration of migrainous headache is usually shorter than that of adults, and may last for less than an hour. The location of the headache may be bifrontal rather than unilateral. The young child may also not be able to describe photophobia or phonophobia, but this can be inferred from their resting in a dark and quiet room when the headache occurs. Thus, the diagnostic criteria need not be fully met before the diagnosis of migraine is made in a young child.

# 2. Secondary Headache

- Secondary headache can be due to intracranial or extracranial causes.
- Intracranial causes of secondary headache include

- Infection: meningitis, encephalitis, brain abscess
- Head trauma (accidental or non-accidental injury): e.g., intracranial bleed, concussion, contusion, post-head trauma headache
- Space-occupying lesion, e.g., tumor, abscess or vascular malformation (either by themselves or causing raised intracranial pressure)
- Vascular disorder

- Intracranial bleed

- arteriovenous malformation rupture, subdural, epidural, intracerebral or subarachnoid hemorrhage
- the headache can be due to meningeal irritation or raised intracranial pressure

- Ischemic stroke, transient ischemic attack (TIA)
- Vasculitis, arteritis
- Cerebral venous thrombosis

- Seizures, post-ictal headache

- High cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) pressure related headache

- Hydrocephalus
- Blocked ventriculoperitoneal (VP) shunt
- Metabolic, toxic, hormonal
- Idiopathic intracranial hypertension

- Low CSF pressure related headache: due to the excessive loss of CSF (e.g., lot of CSF was taken during a lumbar puncture)

- Psychiatric

- Extracranial causes of secondary headache include

- Systemic causes

- Non-intracranial febrile illness, e.g., upper respiratory tract infection (URTI)

- Hypertension
- Fasting-related headache
- Hypoglycemia
- Dehydration

○ Sleep related

- Obstructive sleep apnea
- Sleep deprivation

○ Eye: myopia, eye strain, uveitis

○ ENT: sinusitis

○ Oral cavity

- Dental disorders, such as caries. However, the older child and adolescent should be able to localize the pain to the oral cavity.
- Temporomandibular joint (TMJ) disorder.

○ Neck: e.g., trauma, whiplash injury

○ Medication: e.g., side effect, overuse, adverse reaction to drug

○ Substance withdrawal: e.g., caffeine withdrawal

○ Psychosomatic

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History Taking

- Taking a detailed history of the headache is essential in clinching the diagnosis and directing subsequent management.
- Is it really a headache?

○ Figure out if it is really pain over the head region, or is it something else like dizziness or neck pain.

- What is the nature and characteristic of the headache?

○ Use the acronym "SOCRATES" to determine the nature and characteristic of the headache.
○ S: Where is site of maximal pain? Is it unilateral? Bilateral?

- Unilateral: can be migraine or a secondary cause of headache.

- Bilateral: migraine may be bifrontal in younger children. Tension-type headaches are usually bilateral or "band-like."

○ O: When was the onset of the pain? Was it an acute or gradual onset?
○ C: What is the character of the pain?

- Migrainous headaches tend to be throbbing in nature.

- Tension-type headaches "tight" or "pressing."

○ R: Does the headache radiate?

- Some migrainous headaches may be associated with retro-orbital pain.

○ A: What are the associated symptoms (see section on systemic review for more information)?
○ T: What is the temporal course and the duration?

- Is it acute (<3 months) or chronic (3 months or more)?

- First episode or recurrent?

1

■ Nonprogressive or progressive (increasing in frequency, increasing in duration or increasing in pain score)?

○ E: Exacerbating and relieving factors

■ Exacerbating factors

□ Noisy/bright environment: migrainous headaches are made worse when the environment is noisy and bright.
□ Sleeping: raised intracranial pressure, obstructive sleep apnea (OSA).
□ Lack of sleep: migraine, tension-type headache, sleep deprivation.
□ Stress: migraine, tension-type headache.
□ Worsened by straining (Valsalva maneuver), coughing, urination, defecation: raised intracranial pressure.
□ Medication: overuse of analgesics can ironically be a cause of chronic headaches.
□ Menstruation: migraine.
□ Fasting: ketosis, hypoglycemia.
□ Food, e.g., ingredients known to trigger migraine.
□ Caffeine withdrawal: migraine.

■ Relieving factors

□ Resting in a dark quiet place: migraine
□ Relieved by sleep: migraine, sleep deprivation, obstructive sleep apnea
□ Relieved by medication
□ Stress relief: migraine, tension-type headache

○ S: Severity of the pain.

■ Severe pain is a red flag.
■ For older patients, a pain scale from 1 to 10, is used to quantify the severity of the pain and treatment progress.
■ For younger children, the visual pain rating scale is used (refer to Fig. 34.1).
■ Some young children may not be able to describe the pain in detail. It is useful to ask the parents if the child is still able to carry out activities that he/she used to enjoy when the headaches occur. The child in significant pain will not be able to do the usual activities.

Wong-Baker FACES Pain Rating Scale. Wong-Baker FACES Foundation (2016). Wong-Baker FACES® Pain Rating Scale. Retrieved 1 June 2017 with permission from http://www.WongBakerFACES.org. Originally published in Whaley & Wong's Nursing Care of Infants and Children. © Elsevier Inc.

- Do a good systemic review as the patient may not have recognized associated symptoms or triggers. Get a family history, travel history.

- There are many potential associated symptoms and triggers so it is very important to ask for the associated symptoms and do a thorough systemic review.

- Some of the more common ones are listed here (but this list is not exhaustive):

- Fever: need to look for the cause. May be due to intracranial infection, extracranial infection, or inflammatory condition.
- History of head and/or neck injury: determine when this occurred and the severity of the injury.
- History of high blood pressure: need to find out the cause and assess if this is a hypertensive emergency or urgency.
- Neurological symptoms: e.g., numbness, weakness, dizziness, loss of consciousness, speech problems, change in vision, loss of balance. need to find the cause
- Change in behavior, decline in cognition/academic performance: need to determine the cause.
- Headache and vomiting after a period of recumbence or sleep: e.g., early morning headache associated with vomiting after a night's sleep or after a nap. This is a red flag symptom.
- Altered mental state: need to find the cause (see Chapter 5).
- Seizure: need to find the cause (see Chapter 30).
- Relationship to fasting: hypoglycemia, ketosis
- History suggestive of hypoglycemia (see Chapter 11).
- History suggestive of refractory error, eye strain: e.g., cannot see well, long hours of close work
- History suggestive of sinusitis: e.g., nasal congestion, nasal discharge, fever
- History suggestive of dental issues, TMJ pain.

- Take a good history about sleep (duration, symptoms of obstructive sleep apnea).

- Take a good developmental history: look for any regression or plateauing or delay.

- Take a history about medications, supplements, caffeine, and vaccination history.

- Take a good history about the social background and psychosocial stresses.

- Take a family history: e.g., family history of headaches.

## 2. Physical Examination

- Appearance

- In great pain: this is a red flag.
- Toxic or ill looking: this is a red flag.

• Conscious state

o Altered mental state: A child or adolescent who complains of headache and has altered mental state almost certainly has a secondary cause of the headache and needs to be managed promptly.

• Vital signs

o Fever:

■ This can be due to intracranial infection or extracranial infection or inflammation.

o Hypertension can either be the cause or effect of the headache.

■ Hypertension causing headache, e.g., hypertensive emergency, raised intracranial pressure.

■ Hypertension due to headache; e.g., a child with primary headache can have elevated blood pressure due to the pain.

• Growth parameters

o Increasing head circumference in a young infant or toddler could indicate increasing intracranial pressure.

o A child with faltering growth may have an underlying brain tumor causing diencephalic syndrome and hypopituitarism.

o An obese child or teenager may have obstructive sleep apnea and thus poor quality of sleep leading to headaches.

• Head, eyes, ears, nose, and throat, and face

o Check visual acuity; look for signs of conjunctivitis or iritis.

o Feel face for tenderness over the sinus areas, TMJ joint.

o Ask patient to open and close mouth to see if there is pain.

o Look into the nose.

o Look at the dentition, the pharynx.

o Look at the ears, ear canal, and tympanic membrane.

• Neck

o Neck stiffness/pain may be a sign of meningism (meningitis, subarachnoid hemorrhage [SAH]) or neck pathology (musculoskeletal condition).

o An existing VP shunt: a patient with a VP shunt is at risk of shunt blockage or shunt infection, which can present as headaches.

• Look for signs of meningitis.

o Brudinski sign: flexion of the neck causes flexion of the legs.

o Kernig sign: inability to extend the knee after it is flexed to a right angle at the hips and knees.

• Do a thorough neurological examination.

• Skin

o Petechiae or bruising over the skin: this may indicate bleeding tendencies, or unreported trauma, contributing to a higher chance of intracranial bleed.

o Skin rash (e.g., malar rash): this may indicate systemic lupus erythematosus and central nervous system vasculitis.

• Neurocutaneous stigmata such as café au lait spots: a child with neurofibromatosis Type 1 is at a higher chance of having headaches, either primary or secondary (due to intracranial neoplasms).

- Complete the rest of the examination. Ensure that a thorough head-to-toe examination has been done.
- Fundoscopy: papilledema would be indicative of increased intracranial pressure and/or hypertensive emergency.

### 3. Classify the headache based on the Temporal Course of the Headache and Severity

- Acute headache that is severe or progressive
- Acute headache that is not severe or progressive
- Acute recurrent headache that is not progressive
- Chronic nonprogressive headache
- Chronic progressive headache
- Mixed type headache

![img-13.jpeg](img-13.jpeg)

4. Algorithm

# 5. Acute Headache that Is Severe or Progressive

- A headache that is severe (very painful) or progressive (increasing in frequency, pain, or duration) is one that requires your very close attention as it is a red flag for a possible serious and/or life-threatening condition (i.e., a secondary cause for a headache).

- Associated with head injury:

- Think of possible intracranial bleed, concussion, contusion, and post-head injury headache.
- Think of possible neck injury.

- Not associated with head injury; has fever

- Central nervous system (CNS) infection
- Altered mental state: think about encephalitis, meningitis, brain abscess, vasculitis, hypoglycemia
- Photophobia and neck stiffness: think about meningitis
- Vomiting: consider encephalitis, vasculitis, meningitis, brain abscess, gastroenteritis

- Not associated with head injury; no fever

- Acute headache that is very severe (very painful, worse headache of his/her life): think of intracranial bleed, e.g., SAH, ruptured arteriovenous malformation (AVM)
- Sudden onset of severe headache: think of intracranial bleed, e.g., SAH, ruptured AVM
- High blood pressure: think of

- Hypertensive emergency/urgency
- Raised intracranial pressure (significant head injury, space-occupying lesion, vascular disorder, high CSF pressure related headache)
- Pain causing high blood pressure

- Headache is progressive (increasing frequency, increasing duration or increasing in pain score)

- Significant head injury
- Space-occupying lesion
- Vascular disorder
- High CSF pressure related headache, e.g., hydrocephalus
- Hypertensive emergency, urgency

- Headache after getting up from sleeping/recumbence

- Raised intracranial pressure raised intracranial pressure (significant head injury, space-occupying lesion, vascular disorder, high CSF pressure related headache)
- Sleep deprivation, obstructive sleep apnea

- Headache and vomiting after sleeping or recumbence:

- Raised intracranial pressure raised intracranial pressure (significant head injury, space-occupying lesion, vascular disorder, high CSF pressure related headache)

- Headache that wakes a patient up from sleep: most likely a secondary cause

• Headache relieved by sleeping: think of OSA, sleep deprivation, migraine
• Headache that is exacerbated by coughing, sneezing, exertion, straining, exercise: think of raised intracranial pressure (significant head injury, space-occupying lesion, vascular disorder, high CSF pressure related headache)

• Occipital headache: think of SAH

• Recent neurological deficits, developmental delay (e.g., speech problems, change in vision, loss of balance, or difficulty moving a limb):

■ Abnormal neurological examination: most likely a secondary cause
■ Neck stiffness = meningism (SAH)
■ Seizure (see Chapter 30)
■ Change in behavior, decline in cognition/academic performance, think of a secondary cause
■ Altered mental state: likely a secondary cause. Think of

□ Raised intracranial pressure (significant head injury, space occupying lesion, vascular disorder, high CSF pressure related headache)

□ Significant head injury
□ Seizure
□ Hypertensive emergency, urgency
□ Hypoglycemia
□ Medication, substances

• Recent lumbar puncture: may be a sign of low CSF pressure but also need to exclude CNS infection as a result of the puncture
• Photophobia: think of migraine, meningitis
• History suggestive of refractory error, long hours of close work: think of eye strain
• Jaw-related symptoms: think of pathology in the TMJ or dentition
• Relationship to fasting (hypoglycemia, ketosis)
• Related to medications, supplements, caffeine: side effects, adverse reaction, withdrawal
• Food: think of migraine
• Menses related: think of migraine
• Stress: migraine, tension type headache, sleep deprivation
• Contact history, e.g., infection (CNS, non-CNS)
• Normal mental state, normal exam, no other red flags, no other secondary causes: think of migraine, tension headache, cluster headache

# 6. Acute Headache that Is Not Severe or Progressive

• Is there any other red flag symptom or sign? If yes, look for a secondary cause for the headache.
• Patients with acute headache should be reviewed for progression and red flags.

• The more common conditions that can cause acute headache that is not severe or progressive and with no red flags are

o Migraines
o Tension headache
o Cluster headache
o Systemic causes

■ Non-intracranial febrile illness, e.g., URTI
■ Fasting-related headache
■ Hypoglycemia

o Sleep related

■ Obstructive sleep apnea
■ Sleep deprivation

o Eye: myopia, eye strain, uveitis
o ENT: sinusitis
o Oral cavity

■ Dental disorder such as caries. However, the older child and adolescent should be able to localize the pain to the oral cavity.
■ Temporomandibular joint (TMJ) disorder.

o Neck: e.g., trauma, whiplash injury
o Medication: side effect, overuse, adverse reaction to drug
o Substance withdrawal, e.g., caffeine
o Post-ictal headache
o Low CSF pressure related headache: this is most commonly due to the excessive loss of CSF (e.g., a lot of CSF was taken during a lumbar puncture)
o Stable head trauma
o Psychiatric, psychosomatic

# 7. Acute Headache that Is Recurrent and Not Progressive

• Is there any other red flag symptom or sign? If yes, look for a secondary cause for the headache.
• Did the history and physical examination identify a secondary cause for the headache?
• Many of the secondary causes listed above under "Acute Headache that Is Not Severe or Progressive" can also cause recurrent, nonprogressive acute headache.
• Among those listed above, the more common secondary causes are

o Post-head injury headache
o Sleep related (obstructive sleep apnea, sleep deprivation)
o Eye strain
o Sinusitis
o Medication (side effect, overuse, adverse reaction to drug)
o Substance withdrawal, e.g., caffeine
o Psychosomatic

- If there is no evidence of a secondary cause and the child has a normal examination, primary headache would be more likely. This includes migraine, tension-type headache, and cluster-type headache.
- In primary headaches, the child should be pain free and normal in between headaches.
- If the child is not pain free or normal in between headaches, please review to see if there is a secondary cause for the headache.

### 8. Chronic Headache that Is Nonprogressive

- Is there any other red flag symptom or sign? If yes, look for a secondary cause for the headache.
- Did the history and physical examination identify a secondary cause for the headache?
- The more common secondary causes are

- Post-head injury headache
- Sleep related (sleep deprivation, obstructive sleep apnea)
- Eye strain
- Sinusitis
- Medication
- Substance related
- Psychosomatic

- If there is no evidence of a secondary cause and the child has a normal examination, primary headache would be more likely. This includes migraine, tension-type headache, and cluster-type headache.

- In primary headaches, the child should be pain free and normal in between headaches.
- If the child is not pain free or normal in between headaches, please review to see if there is a secondary cause for the headache.

### 9. Chronic Headache that Is Progressive

- This is a red flag sign.
- The cause of this type of headache is more likely secondary and sinister.
- Look hard for a secondary cause for the headache.
- The sinister secondary causes to consider urgently include

- Space-occupying lesion (e.g., tumor or abscess or vascular malformation)
- Increasing intracranial pressure, e.g., hydrocephalus, blocked VP shunt, idiopathic intracranial hypertension
- Vasculitis

### 10. Mixed-Type Headache

- Some patients experience a mixed type of headache, i.e., having an acute headache that is more severe or different from the patient's usual chronic nonprogressive headache.
- The acute headache needs to be evaluated as stated above (i.e., severe? progressive? red flags?) as they can have a cause of acute

headache that is unrelated to the underlying chronic headache (e.g., a patient with a migraine can have an SAH)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Acute headache that is very severe (very painful, worse headache of his/her life)
- Sudden onset of severe headache
- Headache is progressive (increasing frequency, increasing duration or increasing in pain score)
- Headache and vomiting after sleeping or recumbence
- Headache that wakes a patient up from sleep
- Occipital headache
- Symptoms of neurological deficits (e.g., speech problems, change in vision, loss of balance, or difficulty moving a limb)
- Abnormal neurological examination
- Neck stiffness
- Seizure
- Altered mental state
- Change in behavior
- Decline in cognition/academic performance
- Hypertensive urgency, hypertensive emergency
- Headache that is exacerbated by coughing, straining, exercise
- Signs and symptoms of raised intracranial pressure

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 12-year-old girl presents to the clinic with the problem of worsening headaches.

The headaches first began a year ago. At the onset, the headaches were infrequent, occurring approximately once a month. The headaches were generalized dull aches with pain score of 5 out of 10. They lasted for around 1 hour each time and would resolve spontaneously. She usually experienced the headaches at the end of the day after returning home from school. They do not affect her usual activities and she was still able to play and complete her schoolwork. She was an active child who participated in the physical education classes. There had not been any head injury. There was no associated nausea or vomiting, no visual disturbances, and no photophobia or phonophobia. Her elder sister who is 20 years old had been diagnosed with migraine several years ago.

Over the past two weeks, this child's headaches had increased in frequency, occurring almost daily. The pain score was still 5 out of 10. The headaches still lasted for about 1 hour each time and stopped spontaneously. She has not been given any medication. She vomited once during the headache but did not feel better after that. Her school teachers had informed her parents that she seemed unable to keep up with the lessons, appearing more tired than usual. There had not been any

weakness or sensory loss. However, her parents noticed that she seems to be walking into objects that are beside her on a few occasions. She did not have fever, neck stiffness, or any respiratory symptom. There had not been any loss of appetite or weight. The remaining review of systems was unremarkable. There had not been any recent travel or contact history.

On examination, she was well appearing and well perfused. Her temperature was 37.0°C, heart rate 90/min, respiratory rate 20/min, blood pressure 110/80 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 99%.

Her height and weight were both on the 50th percentile. She was alert and conscious. She was not pale or jaundiced. Pupils were equal at 3 mm and reactive. Optic discs appeared normal on fundoscopy. Her neck was supple. Her ear, nose, and throat examination was normal. Her extraocular movements were intact. There was bitemporal hemianopia. The rest of the cranial nerves were intact. Cerebellar examination was unremarkable. Tone, power, and reflexes of the limbs were normal. Tandem gait was normal. Heart rate was regular. Auscultation revealed first and second heart sounds without murmurs or rubs. Breath sounds were vesicular without adventitious sounds. The abdomen was soft and nontender without any hepatosplenomegaly. She had no lower limb edema. Musculoskeletal examination was normal. There was no skin rash.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- Progressive headaches with neurological deficit (bitemporal hemianopia)
- What is your diagnosis?
- Intracranial space-occupying lesion, likely compressing the optic chiasm
- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- Progressive headache (increasing in frequency)

- Possible symptom of raised intracranial pressure (vomiting once, did not feel better after that)
- Visual field deficit

- Walking into objects beside her
- Objective finding of bitemporal hemianopia

- Disturbance of normal function (inability to keep up in school)

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None.
- A progressive headache is one that is increasing in frequency, duration, OR pain score. It does not mean that it has to be all three features. Hence, even though her headache is not increasing in duration or pain score, it is still a progressive headache.

- If the diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information provided on other features of raised intracranial pressure (pain awakening her from sleep, early morning headaches, headache worse when straining).
- No information about whether the visual acuity is affected.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Brain abscess
- Intracranial bleed (e.g., subarachnoid hemorrhage from vascular malformation or aneurysm)

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Both can present with worsening headache and similar neurological deficits if the lesion is near the optic chiasm.

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Brain abscess: No fever or any other infective symptom.
- Intracranial bleed: History of worsening is subacute and not very intense. Headache due to intracranial bleed would be much more acute in onset and would be more severe in pain. Bitemporal hemianopia is not a common symptom associated with intracranial bleed.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Brain abscess: other signs of infection if intracranial abscess was due to hematogenous spread
- Intracranial bleed: altered mental state and drowsiness may be seen

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Intracranial neoplasm compressing against optic chiasm.
- Possible causes of the neoplasm include pituitary adenoma or other suprasellar mass such as a germinoma.

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Possible early symptom of raised intracranial pressure (vomiting without nausea)
- Visual field deficit (bitemporal hemianopia) indicating compression of the optic chiasm. Need to attend to the underlying cause quickly to prevent further visual loss

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Admit the child, preferably to high dependency.

- Escalate to a senior on call.
- Arrange for urgent neuroimaging (preferably MRI brain to delineate anatomy in a more detailed manner. CT brain would also suffice in the acute setting if MRI brain is not available. The CT brain would be able to show the intracranial lesion and any possible hemorrhage or hydrocephalus).
- Close monitoring of the patient's conscious level as this may deteriorate over time. Child may need intubation.
- Urgent referral to a neurosurgeon and/or neurologist depending on what the brain imaging shows

# Heart Murmur

Quek Swee Chye

# DEFINITION OF HEART MURMUR

A heart murmur is an audible sound heard over the chest; it is usually heard through a stethoscope, though rarely it is palpable (i.e., a thrill is felt).

# SYMPTOMS OF HEART MURMUR

- The patient usually learns of the presence of a murmur only after it is detected by a doctor during an examination.
- The patient may complain feeling a buzz or a vibration when a grade 4 and above murmur is present (these murmurs are associated with a thrill).

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- A heart murmur is caused by turbulence of blood flow within the heart chambers or across the heart valves. The more turbulent the flow, the louder the murmur.
- The pathophysiology of a murmur can be

- Physiological; e.g., innocent murmur
- Pathological; e.g., due to

- Congenital malformation (multifactorial, single-gene defect, chromosomal disorder)
- Genetic condition that manifests later on in life, e.g., Marfan syndrome
- Acquired structural heart defect, e.g., rheumatic valvular disease
- Infection, e.g., infective endocarditis

- Signs that suggest that the heart murmur may be pathological include

- Loud murmur
- Pansystolic murmur
- Early or midsystolic click
- Diastolic murmur
- Harsh murmur
- Abnormal heart sounds
- Cyanosis

- Causes of heart murmurs may also be divided into two groups (acyanotic or cyanotic).

- Acyanotic and has murmur

- Physiological: Still's murmur, pulmonary flow murmur, venous hum
- Pathological:

- Aortic regurgitation
- Aortic stenosis

379

☐ Atrial septal defect
☐ Mitral regurgitation
☐ Mitral stenosis
☐ Patent ductus arteriosus
☐ Pulmonary regurgitation
☐ Pulmonary stenosis
☐ Tricuspid regurgitation
☐ Ventricular septal defect

# ○ Cyanotic with murmur and has

■ Reduced pulmonary blood flow from right ventricular outflow obstruction

☐ Pathological congenital heart condition, e.g. Tetralogy of Fallot (blue).

■ Increased pulmonary flow leading to respiratory distress

☐ Pathological congenital heart condition e.g. transposition of the great arteries, double outlet right ventricle, hypoplastic left heart.

☐ Severe cardiac failure due to an underlying cardiac condition.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History Taking

- When and how the murmur was discovered
- Any symptoms of central cyanosis, e.g. blue tongue
- Any symptoms of congestive cardiac failure, e.g., palpitations, breathlessness, decreased effort tolerance, unable to lie flat, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, leg swelling
- Any fever, any recent fever
- Any rash
- Any pallor
- Any past history suggestive of inadequately treated streptococcal throat infection
- Any syncope, altered conscious state
- Any other medical problem
- Growth parameters and growth history
- Developmental status
- Systemic review
- Family history: arrhythmia, heart problems, heart failure, sudden death, anemia

# 2. Do a Thorough Head-to-Toe Clinical Examination

- Vital signs
- Central cyanosis versus no central cyanosis
- Jugular venous pressure (JVP)

- Nature of the pulse
- Apex beat
- Palpable thrills or heart sounds
- Characteristic of the murmur

- Timing and character of the murmur
- Site of maximal intensity
- Loudness of murmur (grade 1 to 6 for systolic murmurs)
- Radiation
- Effect of physical maneuvers
- Note: It is not unusual to have a combination of murmurs if there are more than one defect. For example, a patient may have aortic stenosis (AS) and aortic regurgitation (AR), in which case an ejection systolic murmur and an early diastolic murmur will be present.

- Characteristic of the heart sounds

- Single or dual heart sound
- Timing of the heart sounds
- Loudness of heart sounds, e.g., loud P2 (pulmonary hypertension)
- Any other sounds, e.g., opening snap, third heart sound, fourth heart sound

- Look for signs of complications and etiologies such as

- Congestive heart failure (tachycardia, tachypnea, gallop rhythm, lung crepitations, elevated jugular venous pressure, enlarged turgid liver, peripheral pitting edema)
- Respiratory failure (tachypnea, retractions, use of accessory muscles of respiration, central cyanosis)
- Syncope, altered conscious state
- Infective endocarditis (fever, Janeway lesions, new murmur)
- Failure to thrive (may be a symptom of the etiology or a complication of heart failure)
- Dysmorphism (may be suggestive of a genetic cause)
- Developmental delay (may be a symptom of the etiology or a complication of heart failure)
- Any other malformation (may be suggestive of a genetic cause)

### 3. Algorithm

- Cyanotic, has murmur, does not have respiratory distress

- Pathological heart condition, e.g., Tetralogy of Fallot (blue)
Note: When a patient with a right heart outlet obstruction is in a hypercyanotic spell, the murmur may disappear during the spell.

- Cyanotic, has murmur, has respiratory distress

- Pathological congenital heart condition e.g. transposition of the great arteries, double outlet right ventricle, hypoplastic left heart.
- Severe cardiac failure due to an underlying cardiac condition.

- Acyanotic and has murmur (see Table 35-1)

5

TABLE 35-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF ACYANOTIC HEART MURMUR

|  Site of Maximum Intensity of Murmur | Other Features | Diagnosis  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Ejection systolic murmur (ESM)  |   |   |
|  Left sternal border | - Humming, musical, vibratory-sounding murmur - Usually louder in the supine position and may only be audible in the supine position | Innocent murmur  |
|  Pulmonary area | - Except flow murmur, usually murmur is harsh - May have splitting of P2 but not fixed - No click | - Pulmonary flow murmur - Pulmonary stenosis (supravalvular, subvalvular) - Peripheral pulmonary stenosis - Pink Fallot Tetralogy  |
|  Pulmonary area | - Usually a harsh murmur - May have splitting of P2 but not fixed - Has a click (suggestive of valvular involvement) | Pulmonary stenosis (valvular)  |
|  Pulmonary area | - Fixed splitting of the second heart sound | Atrial septal defect (ASD)  |
|  Aortic area | - Usually a harsh murmur with radiation to the neck - May have suprasternal thrill - Presence of a click is suggestive of valvular involvement - The presence of low-volume pulse is suggestive of severe stenosis | Aortic stenosis (AS) - Supravalvular - Valvular - Subvalvular  |
|  Apex | - Presence of a click is suggestive of valve prolapse - Radiation to the axilla or lower left sternal age | Mitral valve prolapse (MVP) with mitral regurgitation (MR)  |
|  Pan systolic murmur (PSM)  |   |   |
|  Lower left sternal edge | - Usually a harsh murmur - An MDM flow murmur at apex is suggestive of a large lesion - A loud P2 is suggestive of increased pulmonary flow/ pulmonary hypertension | Ventricular septal defect (VSD)  |
|  Lower left sternal edge | - Usually a soft murmur - May have elevated JVP and/or pulsatile liver | Tricuspid regurgitation (TR)  |
|  Apex | - Usually a harsh murmur - No elevated JVP and/or pulsatile liver | Mitral regurgitation (MR)  |

TABLE 35-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF ACYANOTIC HEART MURMUR—cont'd

|  Site of Maximum Intensity of Murmur | Other Features | Diagnosis  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  **Early diastolic murmur (EDM)**  |   |   |
|  Mid-left sternal border | • Accentuated in end expiration when patient is sitting up and bent forward • May be associated with widened pulse pressure if AR is severe | Aortic regurgitation (AR)  |
|  Pulmonary area | • Not associated with widened pulse pressure • No collapsing pulse | Pulmonary regurgitation  |
|  **Mid-diastolic murmur (MDM)**  |   |   |
|  Apex | • Loud S1, opening snap, pre-systolic accentuation | Mitral stenosis (MS)  |
|  **Continuous murmur**  |   |   |
|  Left infraclavicular | • Continuous "machine-like" murmur • May have bounding pulse, widened pulse pressure | Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA)  |
|  Right clavicular | • Murmur diminishes with jugular vein pressure or neck turning | Venous hum  |

#### 4. Determine the Etiology of the Murmur Using Information Obtained from History and Physical Examination

- Isolated or part of multisystem disease or syndrome
- Congenital or acquired

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Symptoms and signs of heart failure
- Symptoms and signs of respiratory failure
- Symptoms and signs of pulmonary hypertension
- Symptoms and signs of arrhythmia
- Symptoms and signs of infectious endocarditis
- Syncope, altered conscious state
- Growth failure
- Dysmorphism

### CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 12-year-old girl sees you for an upper respiratory tract infection. On auscultation, there was a loud heart murmur detected. You examined her carefully again and found the following signs. She was not cyanosed. The pulse was bounding. There was a precordial heave, and a systolic thrill felt at the lower left sternal edge. Heart sounds were normal. There was a loud pansystolic murmur along the lower left sternal border. There was an early diastolic murmur heard at the mid-left sternal edge.

- What is the clinical problem?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Underlying structural heart defect

- What is your diagnosis?

- Doubly committed subarterial ventricular septal defect (VSD) complicated by aortic valve regurgitation

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Features of VSD: acyanotic, PSM at lower left sternal edge, harsh murmur, thrill
- Features of AR: acyanotic, bounding pulse, early diastolic murmur heard at the mid-left sternal edge

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information about the vital signs – may have widened pulse pressure from AR, displaced, heaving apex beat,
- No information about liver size or peripheral pitting edema
- No information about respiratory examination
- No information about growth
- No information about pubertal development
- No information about whether she is dysmorphic or has other health problems

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- AS/AR

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Acyanotic, systolic and diastolic murmur

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- AS murmur is typically best heard at right upper sternal border (but may be widely transmitted if loud)

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ No information about intermittent cyanosis or cyanotic spells
○ No information about the vital signs
○ No information about respiratory examination
○ No information about growth
○ No information about pubertal development
○ No information about whether she is dysmorphic or has other health problems

○ Pathological heart murmur
○ Doubly committed subarterial VSD complicated by aortic valve regurgitation
○ Not enough information to determine if this is an isolated defect or part of a multisystemic disease or syndrome

○ Check vital signs.
○ Evaluate for heart failure or respiratory distress.
○ Refer to a cardiologist.

# III-Looking Baby or Child

Denise L.M. Goh • Nicola S.P. Ngiam

# DEFINITION OF AN ILL-LOOKING BABY OR CHILD

- An infant is defined as a person who is more than 28 days and less than 1 year of age.
- A child is defined here as a person who is between 1 and 18 years of age.
- Ill-looking is defined on initial inspection as having one or more of the following clinical problems:
  - Altered consciousness
  - Abnormal breathing
  - Abnormal color

# SYMPTOMS ASSOCIATED WITH AN ILL-LOOKING BABY OR CHILD

- Symptoms of altered consciousness:

- Disoriented/confused
- Drowsy
- Does not respond to name calling
- Does not respond to stimuli or pain
- Unarousable
- As assessed by the modified Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) to have impairment of conscious level in response to defined stimuli
  - Eye-opening response to voice or pain only
  - Respond only to commands for limb movement or demonstrates only reflex movement to pain
  - Inability to communicate coherently
- As assessed by the AVPU (acronym for alert, voice, pain, unresponsive) scale to have altered conscious state

- Symptoms of abnormal breathing:

- Symptoms of respiratory distress, e.g., tachypnea, retractions (suprasternal, subcostal, intercostal), stridor, grunting, nasal flaring, difficulty in breathing or increased breathing effort
- Symptoms of depressed or inadequate respiration, e.g., little or no chest movement, little or no breathing effort, shallow breathing, low rate of breathing, irregular rate of breathing, gasping

- Symptoms of abnormal color can be one or more of the following:

- Blue, cyanosis (tongue, lips, face, fingers, etc.)
- Pale
- Dusky
- Grey
- Mottled

386

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

In an ill-looking infant or child, the more common presenting clinical signs/symptoms/problems are usually one of the following:

- Altered mental status (see Chapter 5)
- Seizures (see Chapter 30)
- Respiratory distress (see Chapter 12)
- Low blood pressure/shock (see Chapter 9)
- Physical injuries (see Chapter 37)

![img-14.jpeg](img-14.jpeg)

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

![img-15.jpeg](img-15.jpeg)

Source: Source: https://www.industrydocuments.ucsf.edu/docs/zzjx0269

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

- Check pulse

- Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
- Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

- When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

- If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- See Table 36-1 for the normal ranges for vital signs (Ref: 2015 AHA Pediatric Advanced Life Support Provider Manual).

- Airway: Is the airway compromised?

- Assess if the patient is able to maintain the airway open. If patient is not able to do so, perform measures to keep the airway open, e.g., insert an oropharyngeal airway if the child is obtunded, and do head-tilt chin lift if there is no neck injury.
- Presence of secretions warrants suctioning in most instances.
- Presence of stridor is an indication of partial obstruction of the upper airway obstruction.

- Exclude foreign body, croup, extrinsic compression of the airway, e.g., mediastinal mass, neck mass.

- Be cautious if stridor is present as airway stimulation from suctioning or using a tongue depressor can trigger laryngospasm.

TABLE 36-1

NORMAL RANGES FOR VITAL SIGNS

|  Age | Heart Rate (per Minute) | Respiratory Rate (per Minute) | Systolic Blood Pressure (mmHg)  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Infant (1 month to 1 year) | 100–190 | 30–53 | 72–104  |
|  Toddler (1–2 years) | 98–140 | 22–37 | 86–106  |
|  Preschool (3–5 years) | 80–120 | 20–28 | 89–112  |
|  School-age (6–11 years) | 75–118 | 18–25 | 97–120  |
|  Adolescent (12–15 years) | 60–100 | 12–20 | 110–131  |

# • Breathing: Is the breathing compromised?

# • Assess the respiratory rate and effort

- Tachypnea, subcostal and intercostal retractions, and nasal flaring are evidence of respiratory distress in children.
- Grunting is a sign of respiratory distress usually seen in neonates and young babies. It is due to the patient trying to increase their own PEEP (positive end expiratory pressure) to help keep their airway/alveoli open so as to improve oxygenation.
- Symptoms and signs of respiratory depression indicate that breathing is ineffective, and respiratory support in the form of assisted ventilation should be instituted.

- Regular reassessment of breathing effort is important as the patient's status can change over time.
- Assess with pulse oximetry for hypoxia – normal SpO₂ should be more than 94% in room air.
- Auscultate the lungs to detect adventitious sounds or unequal breath sounds

- Rhonchi or wheezing indicates airway narrowing. Common causes are bronchospasm or inflammation.
- Crepitations indicate alveolar fluid. Common causes are pneumonia or pulmonary edema.
- Unequal air entry: common causes are pneumothorax or lung collapse on one side.

# • Circulation: Is the circulation compromised?

# • Assess the heart rate and rhythm

- Tachycardia is the first sign of circulatory compromise in infants and children.
- Bradycardia may be a sinister sign suggestive of prolonged circulatory compromise or cardiac arrhythmia; bradycardia with a heart rate of less than 60/min indicates impending arrest.

- Assess pulses and pulse volume. Pulse volume may be reduced in circulatory compromise, bounding in septic shock.
- Assess the blood pressure for hypotension or hypertension.
- Assess the capillary refill time – delayed capillary refill (>2 seconds) indicates poor peripheral perfusion.
- Assess the peripheries for temperature and color.

- Cool peripheries indicate poor peripheral perfusion.
- Duskiness, being grey, or mottled indicates a state of impaired circulation and should be taken as a sinister sign.
- Pallor indicates anemia or peripheral vasoconstriction or poor perfusion.

- Look at the cardiac rhythm if the child is on an ECG monitor to determine if there is sinus rhythm or an arrhythmia.

# • Disability: Is there any disability?

- Assess the conscious level of the child using the GCS or the AVPU scale

■ Unresponsiveness or GCS of 8 or less is associated with an inability to protect the airway. The airway may need to be secured with endotracheal intubation.

- Look at the pupils for size, symmetry, and response to light.
- Identify any seizure.
- Determine the blood glucose level.

• Exposure: Any abnormality on exposure?

- Assess for hyper or hypothermia.
- Look for rash, which may help identify the underlying diagnosis.
- Look for evidence of physical injuries or bleeding.

### 3. Take a Focused Medical History

• SAMPLE history

- Signs and symptoms
- Allergies
- Medications
- Past medical history
- Last meal
- Events leading to the deterioration

- Obtain more relevant information if necessary relating to the presenting complaint.
- This information will aid in arriving at the diagnosis and planning management.

### 4. Perform a Physical Examination

• Perform a head-to-toe examination.

### 5. Perform Relevant Investigations

• Order relevant investigations as guided by the history and physical examination so as to determine the diagnosis, the etiology, the severity, the complications, etc.

LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- An infant or child who is not breathing, who is unconscious, or who does not have a pulse is in a life-threatening situation.
- Hypoxia is the main cause of cardiopulmonary arrest in young children.
- Tachycardia is the earliest/first sign of impaired circulation in children. Hypotension is a late sign of impaired circulation. Do not wait for a low blood pressure to begin resuscitation.
- Bradycardia with a heart rate of less than 60/min indicates impending cardiac arrest.
- Respiratory distress in young children can rapidly lead to apnea or inadequate breathing effort as they have little reserve and will fatigue easily.

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO 1

A 3-month-old boy presents to the Children's Emergency Department with fever, cough, and rhinitis for 4 days. He was seen by a family physician on the second day of illness and was given some nose drops. His parents bring him in today because they are worried that he is not feeding well and he seems to have trouble breathing.

His fever reaches a maximum of 39.1°C. The cough is chesty. His nasal discharge is clear to yellowish and his nose is very congested. His feeding has been becoming more difficult. He is fully breastfed and, and over the last 2 days, he would be fretful when feeding. He spends less time on the breast compared to normal. Over the last 2 days, he has had two episodes of vomiting, each occurred after vigorous coughing.

Over yesterday, he has become increasingly breathless and is more lethargic than usual. His last diaper change was 12 hours ago.

On examination, the infant is lethargic. He is tachypneic and has subcostal and intercostal retractions. He is grunting and looks slightly dusky.

Airway: His nose is congested but he otherwise seems able to maintain his airway.

Breathing: His respiratory rate is 60/min, and SpO2 is 88% on room air. Auscultation of the lungs reveals bilateral crepitations with equal breath sounds bilaterally.

Circulation: His heart rate is 150/min and blood pressure is 70/40 mmHg. The capillary refill time is 3 seconds and his peripheries are cool and mottled. His central pulses are palpable but peripheral pulses are weak. The cardiac rhythm on the monitor is sinus tachycardia.

Disability: He responds to voice by opening his eyes and crying. He is moving all four limbs. His pupils are 2 mm in diameter, and equal and reactive to light.

Exposure: There is no rash apart from the mottling on the limbs. There is no evidence of trauma or bleeding. His temperature is 39°C.

On further examination, the oral mucosa appears dry and his anterior fontanelle is slightly sunken. First and second heart sounds are heard with no murmurs or gallop. His abdomen is soft and nontender. His liver is palpable 1 cm below the right costal margin. The spleen is not palpable and kidneys are not ballotable. Bowel sounds are present. On neurological examination, tone and reflexes are normal in all four limbs. The back is normal.
