# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- An ill-looking infant in respiratory distress

- What is your diagnosis?

- Bilateral bronchopneumonia
- In compensated hypovolemic shock due to dehydration

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Ill-looking

- in obvious respiratory distress (grunting and subcostal and intercostal retractions, hypoxia)
- is lethargic
- his color is dusky.

- Bilateral bronchopneumonia

- Cough and significant fever
- Evidence of respiratory distress (grunting and subcostal and intercostal retractions, hypoxia)
- Bilateral crepitations on auscultation of the lungs

- In compensated shock due to hypovolemia as the result of dehydration

- Tachycardia, mottling, delayed capillary refill time, poor peripheral pulses, and cool peripheries indicate shock.
- A normal blood pressure indicates compensated shock.
- A history of poor feeding and poor urine output along with dry mucous membranes and a sunken fontanelle suggests dehydration as the cause of the shock.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Fontanelle is only slightly sunken.
- His urine output has only dropped in the last 12 hours.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information provided on whether there is weight loss to support dehydration

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Acute bronchiolitis
- Septic shock due to bronchopneumonia

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Acute bronchiolitis

- Correct age group
- Respiratory prodrome
- Presence of respiratory distress
- Bilateral abnormal auscultatory findings

- Septic shock due to bronchopneumonia

- Ill-looking child
- Underlying source of infection (bronchopneumonia)
- Tachycardia, mottling, delayed capillary refill time, poor peripheral pulses, and cool peripheries indicate shock

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Acute bronchiolitis: No rhonchi heard in lungs
- Septic shock due to bronchopneumonia

- Pulse pressure not wide
- Presence of signs of dehydration

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Investigation for respiratory virus likely to be positive (but bearing in mind that secondary bacterial infection can also occur in a viral infection)

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Bilateral bronchopneumonia with hypovolemic shock from dehydration

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Respiratory distress is present. Respiratory distress should be taken seriously and treated immediately because, if respiratory distress is left untreated in infants, they fatigue easily and can become apneic, leading to respiratory failure. Respiratory failure is the main cause of cardiac arrest in children, particularly infants.
- Patient has tachycardia. This is the earliest first sign of shock in children.
- Lethargy and poor perfusion are present. Lethargy indicates due inadequate perfusion to the brain.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Airway

- Check if infant can maintain airway.
- Suction the nose, mouth, and pharynx to clear secretions.

- Breathing

- Provide high flow oxygen in the form of a non-rebreather mask.
- Put the infant on continuous heart rate and oxygen saturation monitoring.
- Prepare the bag valve mask device and be ready to provide assisted ventilation early.
- Reassess breathing regularly.

- Circulation

- Secure venous access and take bloods for capillary blood glucose levels, full blood count, electrolytes, urea, and creatinine.
- Consider a capillary blood gas and a blood culture.
- Fluid resuscitate with 20 mL/kg of normal saline over 15–20 min.

- Escalate to a senior.

1

- Reassess the circulation for improving heart rate, capillary refill time, and mentation.
- Administer empiric antibiotics after the blood culture, e.g., intravenous ampicillin.
- Set up a maintenance and replacement intravenous fluid infusion to correct for dehydration.
- Transfer to the high dependency unit.

## CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO 2

An 8-year-old girl presents to the Children's Emergency with abdominal pain and vomiting for 2 days. She managed to go to school today but her mother was called to pick her up early as she was looking quite unwell.

The abdominal pain is central and described as a vague discomfort. She vomited once yesterday after dinner and had a further three episodes of non-bilious, non-projectile vomiting today. There is no fever or diarrhea. There is no photophobia or altered behavior and no dysuria. Her mother gave her some paracetamol syrup for the abdominal pain but it did not help.

She was born at full term and antenatal history was uneventful. Other than the occasional upper respiratory tract infection and one episode of otitis media, she has been well. She has not had any previous surgery. There is no positive contact or travel history. She is developmentally normal and her vaccinations are all up to date.

On examination, it was found that the child is lethargic. She opens eyes to calling and is able to answer questions coherently. She is slightly pale and is tachypneic.

Airway: clear, no stridor.

Breathing: Her respiratory rate is 36/min and SpO₂ is 99% in room air. There are no subcostal or intercostal retractions. The lungs are clear bilaterally on auscultation.

Circulation: Her heart rate is 140/min and blood pressure is 90/64 mmHg. Her capillary refill time was 3 seconds and her peripheral and central pulses were well felt. The ECG monitoring showed a rhythm of sinus tachycardia.

Disability: She is lethargic. She opens her eyes to voice. She is able to move her limbs to command and responds to questions coherently. Her pupils are 3 mm in diameter bilaterally and react to light.

Exposure: There is no rash or any other skin change. There is no evidence of trauma or bleeding. Her temperature is 37.2°C.

On further examination, it is found that her mucosa is dry and her eyes are sunken. Her lungs are clear on auscultation. First and second heart sounds are heard and there are no murmurs. The apex beat is not displaced. Her abdomen is soft and scaphoid. It is nontender and there is no organomegaly. Bowel sounds are sluggish. Her neck is supple. Tone and power in the upper and lower limbs are normal.

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Ill-looking child

- What is your diagnosis?

- Diabetic ketoacidosis with compensated hypovolemic shock

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Ill-looking; on initial inspection she is in lethargic, opens eyes to calling and is tachypneic.
- Diabetic ketoacidosis

- History of abdominal pain and vomiting and absence of significant abdominal physical signs
- Suggestion of metabolic acidosis in view of tachypnea without respiratory distress
- Significant dehydration without much symptoms suggestive of losses
- Depressed sensorium

- Dehydration

- Dry mucous membranes, sunken eyes, tachycardia (moderate dehydration; may be more extravascular fluid loss than intravascular fluid loss)

- Compensated shock because of

- Tachycardia and delayed capillary refill time indicate circulatory impairment but has a normal blood pressure

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Metabolic acidosis could be due to ketosis as a result of insufficient caloric intake due to vomiting.
- Vomiting and abdominal pain are nonspecific symptoms; can also be caused by viral gastroenteritis.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- No information provided on whether there is weight loss or decreased urine output to support dehydration

1

- No information as to whether there was preceding polyuria or polydipsia to suggest uncontrolled diabetes mellitus
- No information about whether there was sick contact in school or any dietary indiscretion to explain the gastrointestinal symptoms
- No information provided on skin turgor

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Viral gastroenteritis with dehydration

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Has gastrointestinal symptoms (vomiting and abdominal pain) and evidence of dehydration

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Looks too sick for a typical gastroenteritis
- Amount of vomiting unlikely to cause this degree of dehydration
- No significant fever

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Can have fever
- Can have abdominal signs, e.g., hyperactive bowel sounds
- Can have positive contact history
- Can have dietary indiscretion (unhygienic or inadequately cooked food)

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Diabetic ketoacidosis with compensated hypovolemic shock

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Tachycardia. This is the first sign of circulatory insufficiency in children.
- Her depressed sensorium is out of proportion to the degree of circulatory insufficiency; suggestive of another cause of change in sensorium, e.g., neurologic or metabolic cause, toxins.
- Tachypnea without respiratory distress suggests the presence of metabolic acidosis.

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Provide high flow oxygen in the form of a non-rebreather mask.
- Put the child on continuous heart rate and oxygen saturation monitoring.
- Secure venous access and take bloods for venous blood glucose levels, full blood count, electrolytes, urea and creatinine, blood ketone levels, and a capillary blood gas.
- Escalate to a senior.
- Transfer the child to the high dependency unit.
- Review blood results. The presence of hyperglycemia, metabolic acidosis, and ketonemia would be consistent with diabetic ketoacidosis. If so, inform the endocrinologist. correct dehydration over 48 hours and intravenous insulin infusions will be required.

# Injuries (Accidental and Non-accidental)

Chew Su Yah • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF INJURIES

- An injury is defined as “a body lesion at the organic level as a result from an acute exposure to energy (mechanical, thermal, electrical, chemical, or radiant) in amounts that exceed the threshold of physiological tolerance or as a result of insufficiency of a vital element (e.g. lack of oxygen)” (modified from Baker, 1984).
- Injuries are the main cause of death, and a major cause of ill health and disability in childhood.
- The causes of injuries vary according to age.
- The causes of injuries during childhood include

- Falls
- Traffic related
- Physical trauma, e.g., assault, sports related, bites
- Fires/burns/scalds
- Submersion
- Suffocation/strangulation
- Poisoning
- Others, e.g., electrical injury

- The above-mentioned injuries may be accidental (unintentional) or non-accidental (intentional)
- Non-accidental injury should be considered as a differential in any child who presents with an injury.
- Non-accidental injury is associated with significant morbidity and mortality.
- Prompt recognition of non-accidental injury can help protect the child from further harm.

# SYMPTOMS OF INJURIES

- Accidental or non-accidental injuries can manifest with similar symptoms.
- The symptoms depend on the mechanism and the anatomical site involved.
- The symptoms can include

- Bruises
- Open wounds
- Burns/scalds
- Pain, swelling or deformity of extremity/ inability to use extremity
- Respiratory distress

399

- Chest pain
- Abdominal pain
- Vomiting
- Seizures
- Altered conscious state
- Headache
- Weakness
- Cardiovascular collapse
- Death

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Injuries in children can be classified in the following ways:

- Based on anatomical site(s):

- Local or multiple sites (defined as injury to two or more body areas)
- The differentiation between local and multiple traumas is a dynamic process, as the initial impression may change as more evidence emerges.

- Based on mechanism of injury: blunt or penetrating injury
- Based on intensity of the force: minimal force, significant force or critical force

- These classifications help in ensuring correct triage and management of injuries:
- The pathophysiology of injuries is

- Direct tissue damage, e.g., physical force, chemical injury

- Tissue damage secondary to hypoxic-ischemic insult

- Shock
- Disruption of blood flow, e.g., bleeding externally or internally into a body cavity or in a solid organ

- The common diagnoses associated with injuries are

- Skin and soft tissue injury: bruising, burns, scalds, lacerations
- Bones and joints injury: fractures, hemarthroses, effusions, dislocations, subluxations
- Central nervous system injury: concussion, contusion, hematoma, hypoxic-ischemic insult
- Spinal cord/nerve injury: cord/nerve contusion, cord transection, nerve laceration, nerve transection
- Intra-abdominal injury: solid organ contusion, solid organ laceration, solid organ rupture, bowel perforation, bowel ischemia
- Chest injury: fractures, lung contusion, pneumothorax, hemothorax, cardiac contusion, cardiac tamponade
- Shock: hypovolemic shock, distributive shock, obstructive shock, neurogenic shock
- Death

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

Continued from previous page

![img-17.jpeg](img-17.jpeg)

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

  - Check pulse

    - Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
    - Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

  - When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

- If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- Take a focused history and do a relevant physical examination to establish the anatomical site of injury, mechanism of injury, force of injury, extent of tissue damage, and extent of indirect tissue injury.
- Identify injury(ies) and complication(s).
- Manage the injury(ies) and complication(s) as indicated
- Consider the relevant differential diagnoses for the injuries seen (see Table 37-1).
- Evaluate if the injury is accidental or non-accidental

  - See if there are any risk factors for non-accidental injury (see Table 37-2).

  - Take note of the caregiver's and/or history giver's verbal and nonverbal communication.

- The following are indicators of possible non-accidental injuries:

  - History findings

    - Delay in seeking medical attention
    - Injury pattern inconsistent with the history of mechanism of injury
    - The caregiver's behavior is hostile or defensive
    - History provided is inconsistent, implausible, vague, or varies from person to person

- History of repetitive injuries that are not explained by normal childhood activities
- History of previous non-accidental injury
- History of failure to thrive and/or failure to receive adequate care such as immunization
- Family history of non-accidental injury or unexplained death

# Physical findings

- Bruising or fractures in a non-mobile infant
- Bruises on uncommonly injured body surfaces, e.g., over areas like buttock, chest, genitalia, ears, neck, inner arms, and inner legs
- Presence of pathognomonic injuries such as pinch marks, cane marks, cigarette burns, circumferential immersion scalds, glove or stocking distribution of burns
- Metaphyseal fractures, posterior rib fractures, scapular/sternal/spinous process fracture
- Multiple injuries and injuries of different forms, e.g., burns, fractures
- Injuries at different stages of healing
- Retinal hemorrhage
- Evidence of poor care, e.g., failure to thrive, poor oral care, and neglected wound

TABLE 37-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS FOR PHYSICAL INJURIES

|  Differential Diagnoses | Characteristics  |
| --- | --- |
|  **Bruises**  |   |
|  Non-accidental injury | Bruising in non-mobile infants Bruises over areas like buttock, chest, genitalia, ears, neck, inner arms, and inner legs Bruises that look like pinch marks Bruises that look like cane marks  |
|  Accidental injury | Occurs in mobile children Common on extremities and areas of bony prominences Not usually seen on the chest or abdomen  |
|  Mongolian spots | More common in Asian, African-American, and Latino children; usually present since birth Painless, nontender, and appearance does not change over the course of days  |
|  Coining or cupping | Folk remedy  |
|  Coagulopathy | History of bleeding or liver disease, family history  |
|  Henoch–Schönlein purpura | Purpuric rash usually over the buttocks and lower extremities; may have associated abdominal pain and arthritis  |
|  Photodermatologic reaction | Occurs after skin contact with a chemical (psoralens) in a certain plant species, with subsequent exposure to sunlight  |
|  Sepsis/meningococcemia | Ill-looking child with features of sepsis, disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC)  |

TABLE 37-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS FOR PHYSICAL INJURIES—cont'd

|  Differential Diagnoses | Characteristics  |
| --- | --- |
|  **Burns/Scalds**  |   |
|  Non-accidental injury | Glove or stocking distribution Scalds isolated to buttocks and genitalia Burns with a recognizable shape (e.g., iron/cigarette butt)  |
|  Accidental injury | Splash burns tend to have irregular margins  |
|  Infection (impetigo, staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome) | Presence of signs of infection such as fever and malaise  |
|  **Skeletal Fractures**  |   |
|  Non-accidental injury | Fractures in nonmobile infants Fracture pattern is not consistent with the mechanism of injury Multiple fractures at different stages of healing Metaphyseal fractures Posterior rib fractures Scapular/sternal/spinous process fracture  |
|  Accidental injury | Developmental age and mechanism of history are consistent with the injury pattern  |
|  Osteogenesis imperfecta | Fractures associated with osteopenia; presence of other features such as Wormian bones of the skull, blue sclera, large fontanelles, hearing loss, and dental abnormalities  |
|  Rickets | Growth retardation, metaphyseal flaring, rachitic rosary, frontal bossing, elevated parathyroid hormone levels, and decreased vitamin D levels  |
|  Osteodystrophy of prematurity | Occurs usually in the first year of life when premature infants have poor bone mineralization due to low calcium stores and diuretic use  |
|  Cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) | Generally not associated with posterior rib fractures  |
|  **Head Injury**  |   |
|  Non-accidental injury | Severity and type of head injury are inconsistent with the nature of the reported injury Retinal hemorrhages seen May have other evidence of injuries  |
|  Accidental injury | Severity and type of head injury are consistent with the nature of the reported injury  |
|  Bleed from a vascular malformation | Distinctive appearance on MRI head scans  |
|  Bleeding diathesis | History of bleeding and family history  |
|  Metabolic disorders | Subdural and retinal hemorrhages have been associated with glutaric aciduria type I; genetic consultation and urine organic acid testing can help diagnose  |

Continued

TABLE 37-1

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS FOR PHYSICAL INJURIES—cont'd

|  Differential Diagnoses | Characteristics  |
| --- | --- |
|  **Abdominal Injury**  |   |
|  Non-accidental injury | Severity and type of abdominal injury are inconsistent with the nature of the reported injury  |
|  Accidental injury | Severity and type of abdominal injury are consistent with the nature of the reported injury  |
|  Infections | Clinical history and imaging studies are useful in delineating the cause  |
|  Tumor | Clinical history and imaging studies are useful in delineating the cause  |

TABLE 37-2

RISK FACTORS FOR NON-ACCIDENTAL INJURY

|  Child | Caregiver | Family  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  - Emotional/behavioral difficulties - Developmental delay/intellectual disability - Physical disabilities - Chronic illnesses - Unwanted child - Unplanned pregnancy | - Low self-esteem - Poor impulse control - Substance/alcohol abuse - History of abuse - Mental illness - Unrealistic expectations - Poor knowledge of child development | - Social isolation - Poverty - Unemployment - Low education achievement - Single parent - Nonbiologically related male living in the same family - Partner violence  |

#### 4. If the History, Physical Examination Are Suspicious of Non-accidental Injury

- Conduct diagnostic evaluation for other injuries (see Table 37-3).
- Refer the patient to child protection services and/or a medical social worker.
- Inform the police.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Altered conscious state
- Apnea or hypopnea
- Centrally cyanosed
- Hypoxia
- No pulse
- Hypotension
- Multiple sites of injuries
- Critical force involved

TABLE 37-3

DIAGNOSTIC EVALUATION FOR OTHER INJURIES

|  Diagnostic Evaluation | AGE OF THE CHILD  |   |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|   |  ≤1 Year | 1-2 Years | 3-5 Years | ≥6 Years  |
|  Plain radiographs to evaluate bones with suspected fractures | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes  |
|  Abdominal CT if there are signs or symptoms of abdominal trauma | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes  |
|  Head CT if there is an abnormal neurologic examination | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes  |
|  Occult abdominal trauma laboratory studies (ALT, AST, amylase, lipase, urinalysis, hemoccult stool) | Yes | Yes | Yes | Use clinical judgment  |
|  Skeletal survey | Perform a skeletal survey |   | Consider a skeletal survey  |   |
|  Dilated fundoscopy if there is evidence of intracranial injury on head CT | Yes | Yes | Yes | Use clinical judgment to decide  |
|  Strongly consider head CT even if neuro examination is normal | Yes | Use clinical judgment to decide if there is a need to scan in children 1 years and above  |   |   |
|  Consider dilated eye examination if neuro examination is normal | Yes | Use clinical judgment to decide if there is a need to scan in children 1 years and above  |   |   |

## CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 9-month-old infant girl presents to you with right thigh swelling and tenderness for 5 days. Her mother reports that she has been unusually fussy and cries whenever she is picked up or has her diaper changed. She has also stopped crawling in the last 5 days. She does not recall any significant trauma, but the child has started crawling recently and has occasional falls resulting in bruises on her body. There has been no fever, vomiting, or rash. She is feeding normally and has been in good health.

Her prenatal and birth history were normal. She has no previous hospitalizations or surgeries. She is not taking any medication and has no drug allergies. She has no history of easy bruising and has tolerated her vaccinations at birth well without hematoma at the injection sites. However, she has missed her recent vaccinations. There are no developmental concerns. Her mother is the main caregiver. She recently started a new job working night shifts and the child is cared for by her live-in boyfriend.

On examination, her temperature is 37.1°C, heart rate 120/min, respiratory rate 25/min, blood pressure 105/65 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 98%. Her weight and height are on the 25th percentile and occipital frontal circumference on the 50th percentile. She is alert and calm while lying in her stroller but cries when picked up. There is no pallor or features of dysmorphism. Her fontanelle is normal. Her pupils are equal and normal. Neurologically, she is moving all her limbs except for her right lower limb. Her right thigh is swollen and deformed. She cries on palpation of her right thigh. There is no erythema, warmth, or open wound. Her right hip, knee, ankle, and foot are normal. Her reflexes are normal. There are a linear and four circular bruises over her buttock and thighs. Her cardiac and respiratory examinations are normal. Her abdomen is soft and nontender. There is no abdominal distension or organomegaly. Her bowel sounds are normal.

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Multiple blunt injuries of mild to moderate severity.

- What is your diagnosis?

- Probable right femur fracture and soft tissue injuries, likely to be non-accidental in nature.

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Multiple injuries: two areas are potentially involved – suspected bony injury of right thigh and bruises over her buttocks and thighs.
- Injuries are blunt because there is no break in the skin.
- The severity of the bony injury is moderate.
- The severity of bruising is mild to moderate.
- Acute onset of swelling, tenderness, and refusal to use the right lower limb suggest an injury to the right lower limb.

• Suspicion of non-accidental injury

- Bruising over buttocks and thighs is suggestive of non-accidental injury as they are not common areas of injuries in childhood.
- Injury pattern inconsistent with the history of injury – falls from crawling are unlikely to cause fracture of the femur and bruising to the buttocks.
- Has multiple injuries and of different forms.
- Some delay in bringing the child to medical attention.
- Child had failed to receive her routine vaccinations.
- Possible risk factors associated with non-accidental injury include a single parent and the child is cared for by mother's live-in boyfriend.

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

o None

• If the diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or provided in this case.

o X-ray findings of shaft of femur fracture.
o Skeletal survey may show healing fractures or highly suspicious fractures such as classic metaphyseal fractures.
o No family history of easy bruising/bleeding, or conditions such as osteogenesis imperfecta.
o No previous history of prematurity or rickets.
o Need further history on risk factors for non-accidental injuries, e.g., unplanned or unwanted pregnancy, history of abuse in caregiver, domestic violence in the household, and history of alcohol or substance abuse in caregiver.

• What are your alternative diagnoses?

o Injuries from accidental trauma
o Easy bruising from coagulopathy
o Pathological fracture due to abnormal bone, e.g., underlying bone cyst, osteogenesis imperfecta

• List the findings in this case that support this alternative diagnosis.

o Accidental trauma can occur in a mobile child and it can be unwitnessed.
o In coagulopathy and in abnormal bone, the injury is inconsistent with the force applied or sustained

• List the findings in this case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o She has had no previous history of easy bleeding or bruising with vaccinations to suggest a congenital coagulopathy. No previous history is consistent with an acquired cause of coagulopathy
o There are no other somatic features of osteogenesis imperfecta present in this child.

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

o Non-accidental injuries: bruising to buttocks and thigh, right femoral fracture

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

o No obvious complications. Significant blood loss from femur fracture is unlikely as the child is hemodynamically normal. There is no obvious abusive head injury, or blunt abdominal injuries.
o Red flag present: Child is suspected of having sustained non-accidental injury; risk for further harm.

• What is your plan for this patient?

o Perform right femur X-ray to confirm the diagnosis of fracture.
o Provide analgesia and immobilize the fracture with a splint.

- Referral to an orthopedics specialist for the management of the fracture.
- Perform the following tests to look for occult injuries:

- Skeletal survey to identify occult fractures.
- Occult abdominal trauma laboratory studies (alanine aminotransferase [ALT], aspartate aminotransferase [AST], amylase, lipase, urinalysis, stool for occult blood).
- Consider head CT for occult head injury.

- Perform the following tests to look for possible differentials:

- Clotting work-up (full blood count, prothrombin time, partial thromboplastin time, von Willebrand factor, factor levels)
- Assessment of bone health (calcium, phosphorous, alkaline phosphatase, 25-hydroxy vitamin D levels)

- Discuss with the parent regarding the concerns of non-accidental injuries.

- Report to the child protection officer.

- Referral to the medical social worker.

- Referral to police

# Jaundice

## (Before 3 Months of Life)

Karthik S.V. • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF JAUNDICE

- Jaundice refers to the yellow staining of the skin, mucous membranes, and sclerae secondary to elevated blood levels of bilirubin.
- In healthy children, total serum bilirubin levels should be less than 17 umol/L (1 mg/dL) and are usually much lower, in the range of 0–0.5 umol/L (0–0.3 mg/dL).
- Clinical jaundice is often noted only after the total bilirubin levels are over 34 umol/L (2 mg/dL). The jaundice is usually quite obvious when the bilirubin level is over 85 umol/L (5 mg/dL).
- This chapter deals with jaundice in a neonate and young infant (approximately less than 3 months old).
- See Chapter 39 for the approach to jaundice in a patient who is 3 months or older.

# SYMPTOMS OF JAUNDICE

- Yellowish discoloration of skin, mucous membranes, and/or sclerae.
- This is most often noticed by the caregiver rather than the patient himself/herself.
- In neonates, the jaundice tends to progress cephalocaudally. The yellow discoloration is most often noticed by the caregiver rather than the patient himself/herself.
- Symptoms of acute encephalopathy. In the neonate, the blood brain barrier is immature. When there is a high level of unconjugated bilirubin, there is a risk of the bilirubin crossing into the brain and having toxic effects. If this occurs, the condition is known as bilirubin encephalopathy (kernicterus). The symptoms and signs of acute bilirubin encephalopathy include lethargy, decreased feeding, change in tone, a high-pitched cry, arching, fever, seizures, and death.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Jaundice in the first 2 weeks of life may be physiological or pathological.
- Jaundice beyond the first 2 weeks of life is more likely to be pathological and must be investigated.
- Jaundice (hyperbilirubinemia) may be caused by increased serum levels of unconjugated bilirubin, increased conjugated bilirubin, or both.

411

- Unconjugated bilirubin is also known as indirect bilirubin; conjugated bilirubin is also known as direct bilirubin.

# 1. Unconjugated Hyperbilirubinemia

- Pathophysiology

o This is said to be present when the hyperbilirubinemia is predominantly unconjugated (i.e., ≥80% of the total bilirubin is unconjugated).

o The pathophysiological mechanisms include

- Increased bilirubin production
- Decreased uptake of bilirubin by liver
- Decreased bilirubin conjugation
- Increased enterohepatic circulation of bilirubin

- Common conditions associated with unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia

o Causes of increased bilirubin production

- Hemolysis
  - ABO and Rh (D) incompatibility
  - Red blood cell membranopathies: hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis
  - Red blood cell enzyme deficiencies: glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency, pyruvate kinase deficiency
  - Hemoglobinopathies: sickle cell disease, thalassemia
  - Autoimmune hemolytic anemias
  - Infections: malaria, sepsis with disseminated intravascular coagulation
  - Drug-induced hemolytic anemia, alternative medicine-induced hemolytic anemia

- Other causes

o Extravasation of blood: hematomas, cerebral/pulmonary/abdominal/other occult hemorrhage

o Polycythemia

o Causes of decreased uptake of bilirubin by liver

- Congenital portosystemic shunt
- Cardiac failure

o Causes of decreased bilirubin conjugation

- Prematurity
- Breast milk jaundice (appears later than breastfeeding jaundice and persists for longer)
- Metabolic

o Hypothyroidism

o Hypopituitarism

- Inborn errors of metabolism, e.g., Gilbert syndrome, Criggler-Najjar syndrome types I and II

- Infection, e.g., urinary tract infection (UTI), sepsis

o Causes of increased enterohepatic circulation of bilirubin

- Breast feeding jaundice

- Pyloric stenosis
- Small or large bowel obstruction
- Ileus

# • Manifestations of some of the common causes of unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia

# ○ Physiologic jaundice of the neonate

■ Refers to benign transient hyperbilirubinemia in a neonate and is multifactorial in etiology:

☐ Increased bilirubin production due to increased red blood cell breakdown
☐ Decreased update of bilirubin from plasma due to decreased ligandin in liver
☐ Decreased bilirubin conjugation from decreased uridine diphosphoglucuronosyl transferase activity
☐ Increased enterohepatic circulation of bilirubin

■ Appears between second and fifth days of life in the term infant and resolves after 10–14 days

# ○ Breastfeeding jaundice

■ Refers to jaundice in breastfed infants in the first 2–4 days of life
■ Related to increased enterohepatic circulation of bilirubin that occurs because breastfed infants receive fewer calories until the milk has “come in” and the reduced caloric intake is a stimulus to increased enterohepatic circulation

# ○ Breast milk jaundice

■ Refers to prolonged jaundice in totally breastfed infants that extends into the third and fourth weeks of life.
■ Risk factors are race and family history.
■ The pathophysiology is decreased bilirubin conjugation from decreased uridine diphosphoglucuronosyl transferase (UDPGT) activity.
■ The decrease in the activity is related to mutations in the UDPGT gene and maybe some substances in breast milk that inhibit bilirubin conjugation.

# 2. Conjugated Hyperbilirubinemia

# • Pathophysiology

○ This is said to be present when the conjugated bilirubin level is greater than 26 umol/L (1.5 mg/dL) or constitutes greater than 20% of the total bilirubin level.
○ This results from decreased bilirubin excretion caused by

■ Biliary obstruction (this type of jaundice is known as cholestatic or obstructive jaundice)

■ Hepatocellular dysfunction

○ Most of the time, both processes are involved though one of the processes may be more dominant than the other.

• Common conditions associated with conjugated hyperbilirubinemia

o Biliary obstruction (cholestatic or obstructive jaundice)

■ Extrahepatic causes

□ Intraluminal obstruction, e.g., choledocholithiasis (galstones), inspissated bile
□ Biliary tree abnormalities

- Biliary atresia

- Choledochal cysts and other developmental disorders of the bile ducts
- Common bile duct strictures or stenosis

□ Extraluminal compression, e.g., pancreatic lesions, enlarged lymph nodes

■ Intrahepatic causes

□ Drugs causing cholestatic jaundice: macrolides, semisynthetic penicillins, ceftriaxone, antifungals (ketoconazole, griseofulvin, and gluconazole)
□ Cholestatic disorders: progressive familial intrahepatic cholestasis (PFIC) – types 1, 2, and 3, Alagille syndrome, benign recurrent intrahepatic cholestasis (BRIC), chronic liver disease associated with cystic fibrosis, Caroli disease, congenital hepatic fibrosis and associated conditions, primary sclerosing cholangitis with/without associated autoimmune hepatitis

o Hepatocellular dysfunction

■ Congenital or acute viral hepatitis – cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection; Hepatitis A, B, or C; rubella; toxoplasmosis; syphilis; tuberculosis; listeriosis
■ Toxins

□ Sepsis
□ Drugs

- Acute drug-induced liver injury, e.g., paracetamol poisoning, valproate, macrolides, semisynthetic penicillins, ceftriaxone, antifungals (ketoconazole, griseofulvin, and gluconazole), isoniazid, rifampicin, phenytoin

- Total parenteral nutrition

■ Inborn errors of metabolism (IEMs)

□ Disorders of amino acid metabolism: tyrosinemia
□ Disorders of lipid metabolism: Niemann–Pick disease, Gaucher disease
□ Disorders of carbohydrate metabolism: galactosemia, fructosemia
□ Others: citrin deficiency, alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, cystic fibrosis, Zellweger syndrome, mitochondrial disease

■ Autoimmune hepatitis with/without associated sclerosing cholangitis

■ "Idiopathic" neonatal hepatitis

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Is There Jaundice?

- Check if there is yellowing of the sclera, skin, and mucous membranes.
- Need to distinguish this from carotenemia, a benign condition causing yellow pigmentation of the skin (xanthoderma) but not the sclera or mucous membranes. The yellowing is due to increased beta-carotene levels in the blood, most often due to ingestion of excessive amounts of carotene-rich foods like carrots, sweet potatoes, pumpkin, and squash.

# 2. What Is the Age of the Child?

- Jaundice occurring before 24 hours of life is always pathological.
- Jaundice before 2 weeks of age may be pathological or nonpathological.
- Jaundice after 2 weeks of age has a high risk of being pathological.
- Neonates are at risk of bilirubin encephalopathy (kernicterus). The symptoms are

- Initial phase: lethargy, slightly hypotonia, paucity of movement, poor suck, slightly high-pitched cry
- Intermediate phase: irritable, tone variable and usually increased, minimal feeding, high-pitched cry
- Advanced phase: deep stupor to coma, tone increased and may have retrocollis-opisthotonus, not feeding, shrill cry

# 3. Is It Conjugated or Unconjugated Hyperbilirubinemia?

- What is the quality of the jaundice?

- A lemon yellow hue is more often associated with unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia.
- A greenish hue to the jaundice suggests prolonged obstructive/conjugated hyperbilirubinemia.
- An orange-yellow color is seen more with hepatocellular dysfunction.

- Conduct a urine and stool inspection if possible.

- Features suggestive of conjugated hyperbilirubinemia include tea-colored urine, pale stools (note that pale stools occur only when there is complete biliary obstruction).

- Sometimes, it is not possible to differentiate between conjugated and unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia without actually measuring serum total and direct bilirubin.

# 4. Ask About Risk Factors and Associated Symptoms to Determine Possible Etiology and Complications

- The possible symptoms that give clues to etiology are many and the more common ones are listed below.

- Perinatal and birth history

- Maternal history of infection (intrauterine infections).

- Risk factors for polycythemia (infant of diabetic mother, intrauterine growth retardation).
- Preterm babies are at risk of jaundice.
- Babies with difficult deliveries may sustain injuries or hematomas. Look for bruising or hematomas.
- Babies who have not received vitamin K have higher risk of significant (internal) bleeding.
- Babies who have had total parental nutrition are at risk of jaundice.
- Knowledge of the mother's and baby's blood group will inform risk for ABO incompatibility.
- Knowledge of the parents' and baby's Rh status will inform risk for Rh incompatibility.
- G6PD status of the child (this is screened for in many countries but not in all countries).
- Abnormal thyroid screening results (this is screened for in many countries but not in all countries).
- Ask about screening for other IEMs (this is screened for in many countries but not in all countries).

# • Feeding history

- Exclusively breastfed neonates are at risk of breastfeeding and breast milk jaundice.
- Assess if the baby is receiving adequate nutrition or hydration (urine output, stool output, weight loss, weight gain, baby hungry/crying/satisfied).
- Babies who are not receiving adequate nutrition or hydration usually have significant weight loss; such babies are at risk of jaundice.

- Family history of, e.g., neonatal jaundice, prolonged jaundice, liver disease, blood disease, similar condition, consanguinity

- Symptoms suggestive of hemolysis (pallor, color of urine)

- Pallor and tea-colored urine (extravascular hemolysis; but this can also be seen in conjugated hyperbilirubinemia with anemia)
- Pallor and red-colored urine (suggestive of intravascular hemolysis)
- Predisposition to hemolysis (family history, G6PD status, infection, drug exposure, alternative medication)

- Symptoms suggestive of obstruction

- Fever, pain (cholangitis related symptoms)
- Exposure to drugs or alternative medicine
- Abdominal swelling, abdominal mass

- Symptoms suggestive of hepatocellular cause

- Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEMs)
- Right hypochondrial/epigastric pain
- Fever, nausea, vomiting
- Travel history
- Food hygiene and hand hygiene history

- Weight
- Diet history (calories, carbohydrate intake, alcohol intake)
- Contact history
- Exposure to drugs or alternative medicine

- Symptoms suggestive of liver dysfunction

- Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEMs)
- Bleeding tendencies (skin, gums, gastrointestinal [GI], lung, brain, etc.)
- Symptoms of hypoalbuminemia (generalized edema, ascites, pleural effusions)
- Acute or chronic?

- Symptoms suggestive of acute causes (previously well, well thrived, normal development, no symptoms of chronic liver disease)
- Symptoms suggestive of chronic liver disease (may have failure to thrive)
- Symptoms of portal hypertension (dilated abdominal veins, history of GI bleeding)

- Symptoms of dysmorphism, other malformations

- Symptoms of other complications

- Bilirubin toxicity (see start of this chapter)
- Failure to thrive (genetic cause, intra-uterine cause, inadequate nutrition, malabsorption, etc.) (see Chapter 25)

# 5. Physical Examination

- Do a head to toe examination
- There are many possible findings
- Some common or pertinent signs, and their significance, are listed below.
- Altered conscious state may be due to hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, an IEM, bilirubin encephalopathy, hypoglycemia, sepsis, etc. (see Chapter 5)
- Abnormal vital signs
- Failure to thrive suggests a chronic cause (genetic cause, IEM, inadequate intake, malabsorption, etc.) (see Chapter 25)
- Dysmorphism suggests a genetic cause, intrauterine infection
- Signs suggestive of hemolysis:

- Pallor
- Tea- or red-colored urine
- Liver size usually normal
- Spleen may or may not be enlarged

- Signs suggestive of obstruction:

- Systemic signs of acute infection (fever, looking unwell, etc.) may be present.
- Tenderness in the right hypochondrium may be present.
- Liver may or may not be enlarged.
- Intra-abdominal mass may be present.

• Signs suggestive of a hepatocellular cause:

o Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEMs)
o Systemic signs of acute infection (fever, looking unwell, etc.) may be present.
o Tenderness in the right hypochondrium may be present.
o Liver may or may not be enlarged.

• Signs suggestive of liver dysfunction:

o Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEM)
o Signs of bleeding tendencies (skin, gums, GI, lung, brain, etc.)
o Signs of hypoalbuminemia (generalized edema, ascites, pleural effusions)

• Signs suggestive of acute causes: previously well, well thrived, normal development, no signs of chronic liver disease

• Signs suggestive of chronic liver disease: may have failure to thrive, clubbing, palmar erythema, enlarged spleen due to portal hypertension

o Signs of portal hypertension (splenomegaly, dilated superficial abdominal veins, ascites) suggest a more chronic cause

• Signs of bruising, bleeding (causes include thrombocytopenia due to hypersplenism, clotting factor deficiencies due to liver dysfunction and/or vitamin K deficiency) (see Chapter 14)

• Signs of ADEK deficiencies (due to prolonged cholestasis and fat absorption, visual impairment, corneal changes, rickets, peripheral neuropathy, bruising, bleeding)

# 6. Algorithm

• A liver function test is usually needed in narrowing down the differentials for jaundice.
• See flowchart.

![img-18.jpeg](img-18.jpeg)

1

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Any neonate who is jaundiced in the first 24 hours of life
- Any neonate who is jaundiced beyond 2 weeks of life
- Lethargic or irritable neonate with reduced feeding possibly indicating bilirubin encephalopathy, hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, or an IEM
- Presence of pale stools and dark urine in a young infant requiring urgent work-up as this may reflect serious underlying biliary problems that require urgent intervention.
- Any sign of bleeding
- Any sign of severe pallor
- Hypoglycemia

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 5-week-old baby, who was born at term, was referred to the hospital with persistent jaundice. She had been exclusively bottle fed with an infant formula since birth. Apart from a 24 hours period of phototherapy on day 3 of life, the neonatal period was uneventful. The parents reported that the baby had been more noticeably jaundiced recently. The stools had been consistently pale over the past 2 weeks with passage of dark urine as seen on the nappies. Physical examination confirmed clinical jaundice. The liver was felt up to 3 cm beneath the right costal margin and was slightly firm in consistency. The rest of the systemic examination was unremarkable.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

• Jaundice with hepatomegaly. The pale stools and dark urine suggest a cholestatic or obstructive pathology.

• What is your diagnosis?

• There are several possible causes of obstructive jaundice.
• The most important cause to consider at this age is biliary atresia, as urgent surgical intervention is needed and can be life-changing.

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

• Jaundice
• Hepatomegaly
• Pale stools
• Dark urine

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

• None

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

• No information provided on the weight/growth of child
• No information provided on the liver function of the child
• No information provided on the presence/absence of infection, especially cholangitis
• No information about what newborn screening tests were done and their results

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

• Other causes of complete biliary obstruction, e.g., choledochal cyst
• Hepatocellular disease is less likely

• For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

• Jaundice
• Hepatomegaly
• Pale stools
• Dark urine

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

• None except that they are less common than biliary atresia

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case

• It is sometimes possible to feel a mass when the cause is a choledochal cyst

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

• Biliary atresia

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

• Nil

• What is your plan for this patient?

• There is an urgency to do investigations quickly to determine if this is biliary atresia as the window for preventing liver cirrhosis is closing
• Do a liver function test, including conjugated bilirubin, gamma glutamyl transferase.
• A fasting hepatobiliary ultrasound and a radionuclide-labeled scintiscan (e.g., HIDA scan) are essential to diagnose biliary atresia.

- On some occasions, even these investigations may yield equivocal results. In these situations, a liver biopsy or an intraoperative cholangiogram may be essential.
- Definitive management will depend on the underlying cause. Infants diagnosed with biliary atresia will need the Kasai portoenterostomy procedure as soon as possible.
- As there is significant cholestasis, look for the complications of cholestasis and manage/prevent them, e.g., inadequate caloric absorption, deficiency of fat-soluble vitamins (vitamins A, D, E, and K).

# Jaundice

## (Beyond 3 Months of Life)

Karthik S.V. • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF JAUNDICE

- Jaundice refers to the yellow staining of the skin, mucous membranes, and sclerae secondary to elevated blood levels of bilirubin.
- In healthy children, total serum bilirubin levels should be less than 17 umol/L (1 mg/dL) and is usually much lower, in the range of 0–0.5 umol/L (0–0.3 mg/dL).
- Clinical jaundice is noted only when the total bilirubin levels are greater than 34 umol/L (2 mg/dL). Jaundice is usually quite obvious when the bilirubin level is greater than 85 umol/L (5 mg/dL).
- This chapter deals with jaundice in an older child beyond 3 months.
- See Chapter 38 for the approach to jaundice in a neonate or infant.

# SYMPTOMS OF JAUNDICE

- Yellowish discoloration of skin, mucous membranes, and/or sclerae.
- This is most often noticed by the caregiver rather than the patient himself/herself.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Jaundice (hyperbilirubinemia) may be caused by increased serum levels of unconjugated bilirubin, increased conjugated bilirubin, or both.
- Unconjugated bilirubin is also known as indirect bilirubin; conjugated bilirubin is also known as direct bilirubin.

# 1. Unconjugated Hyperbilirubinemia

- Pathophysiology:

- Unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia is said to be present when the hyperbilirubinemia is predominantly unconjugated (i.e., ≥80% of the total bilirubin is unconjugated).

- Causes:

- Increased hemolysis

- Red blood cell membranopathies: hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis
- Red blood cell enzyme deficiencies: glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency, pyruvate kinase deficiency
- Hemoglobinopathies: sickle cell disease, thalassemia
- Autoimmune hemolytic anemias

423

- Infections: malaria, sepsis with disseminated intravascular coagulation, hemolytic uremic syndrome
- Drug-induced hemolytic anemia, alternative medicine-induced hemolytic anemia

- Impaired conjugation of the bilirubin by the liver

- Gilbert syndrome
- Criggler-Najjar syndrome

# 2. Conjugated Hyperbilirubinemia

- Pathophysiology

- This is said to be present when the conjugated bilirubin level is greater than 26 umol/L (1.5 mg/dL) or constitutes greater than 20% of the total bilirubin level.
- This can be due to decreased bilirubin excretion (e.g., biliary obstruction; also known as cholestatic or obstructive jaundice) and/or due to hepatocellular dysfunction. Most of the time, both processes are involved, though one of the processes may be more dominant than the other.

- Causes

- Biliary obstruction (cholestatic or obstructive jaundice)

- Extrahepatic causes

- Intraluminal obstruction, e.g., choledocholithiasis (gallstones), parasitic infections
- Biliary tree obstruction or wall pathology, e.g., choledochal cysts, common bile duct strictures
- Extraluminal compression, e.g., pancreatic lesions, enlarged lymph nodes

- Intrahepatic causes

- Drugs causing cholestatic jaundice: macrolides, semisynthetic penicillins, ceftriaxone, antifungals (ketoconazole, griseofulvin, and gluconazole), oral contraceptive pills, androgens, and estrogens
- Cholestatic disorders: progressive familial intrahepatic cholestasis (PFIC) – types 1, 2, and 3, Alagille syndrome, benign recurrent intrahepatic cholestasis (BRIC), chronic liver disease associated with cystic fibrosis, Caroli disease, congenital hepatic fibrosis and associated conditions, primary sclerosing cholangitis with/without associated autoimmune hepatitis

- Hepatocellular dysfunction as a cause of conjugated jaundice

- Acute viral hepatitis – predominantly caused by Hepatitis A, Hepatitis E, Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) and Cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection
- Acute drug induced liver injury, e.g., paracetamol poisoning, valproate, isoniazid, rifampicin
- Chronic viral hepatitis caused by Hepatitis B and C
- Autoimmune hepatitis with/without associated sclerosing cholangitis
- Nonalcoholic fatty liver disease

■ Metabolic liver disorders such as citrin deficiency, Wilson disease, Alpha-1-antitrypsin deficiency, and other rare inborn errors of metabolism (IEMs)

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Is There Jaundice?

- Check if there is yellowing of the sclera, skin, and mucous membranes.
- Need to distinguish this from carotenemia, a benign condition causing yellow pigmentation of the skin (xanthoderma) but not the sclera or mucous membranes. The yellowing is due to increased beta-carotene levels in the blood, most often due to ingestion of excessive amounts of carotene-rich foods like carrots, sweet potatoes, pumpkin, and squash.

# 2. Is It Conjugated or Unconjugated Hyperbilirubinemia?

• What is the quality of the jaundice?

○ A lemon yellow hue is more often associated with unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia.
○ A greenish hue to the jaundice suggests prolonged obstructive/conjugated hyperbilirubinemia.
○ An orange-yellow color is seen more with hepatocellular dysfunction.

• Carry out a urine and stool inspection if possible.

• Features suggestive of conjugated hyperbilirubinemia include tea-colored urine, pale stools (note that pale stools occur only when there is complete biliary obstruction).

• Sometimes, it is not possible to differentiate between conjugated and unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia without actually measuring the serum total and direct bilirubin.

# 3. Ask About Risk Factors, and Associated Symptoms to Determine Possible Etiologies and Complications

• The possible symptoms that give clues to etiology are many and the more common ones are listed below.
• Family history of, for example, similar condition, consanguinity, contact history
• Symptoms suggestive of hemolysis:

○ Pallor and tea-colored urine (extravascular hemolysis; but this can also be seen in conjugated hyperbilirubinemia with anemia)
○ Pallor and red-colored urine (suggestive of intravascular hemolysis)
○ Predisposition to hemolysis (family history, G6PD status, infection, drug exposure, alternative medication)

• Symptoms suggestive of obstruction:

○ Biliary colic (choledocholithiasis)
○ Fever, pain (cholangitis-related symptoms)
○ Drug exposure, alternative medicine exposure
○ Abdominal mass, abdominal swelling

1

• Symptoms suggestive of hepatocellular cause:

○ Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEM
○ Right hypochondrial/epigastric pain
○ Fever, nausea, vomiting
○ Travel history
○ Food hygiene and hand hygiene history
○ Diet history (calories, carbohydrate intake, alcohol intake)
○ Contact history
○ Drug exposure, alternative medicine exposure

• Symptoms suggestive of liver dysfunction:

○ Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEM
○ Symptoms and signs of bleeding tendencies (skin, gums, gastrointestinal [GI], lung, brain, etc.)
○ Symptoms and signs of hypoalbuminemia (generalized edema, ascites, pleural effusions)
○ Acute or chronic?

■ Symptoms suggestive of acute causes (previously well, well thrived, normal development, no symptoms of chronic liver disease)
■ Symptoms suggestive of chronic liver disease (may have failure to thrive
■ Symptoms of portal hypertension (dilated abdominal veins, history of GI bleeding) are suggestive of a more chronic cause

• Symptoms of dysmorphism, other malformations

• Symptoms of other complications

○ Failure to thrive (genetic cause, intra-uterine infection, inadequate nutrition, malabsorption, etc.) (see Chapter 25)
○ Developmental delay (genetic cause, intra-uterine infection, previous hepatic encephalopathy, previous hypoglycemia, pervious hyperammonemia, some forms of IEMs)

# 4. Physical Examination

• Do a head to toe examination
• There are many possible findings
• Some common or pertinent signs, and their significance, are listed below.
• Altered conscious state may be due to hepatic encephalopathy, an IEM.
• Abnormal vital signs
• Failure to thrive suggests a chronic cause (genetic cause, IEM, inadequate intake, malabsorption, etc.) (see Chapter 25).
• Dysmorphism suggests a possible genetic cause, an intrauterine infection.
• Signs suggestive of hemolysis:

○ Pallor
○ Tea or red color of urine

- Liver size usually normal
- Spleen may or may not be enlarged.

- Signs suggestive of obstruction:

- Systemic signs of acute infection (fever, looking unwell, etc.) may be present
- Tenderness in the right hypochondrium may be present
- Liver may or may not be enlarged
- Intra-abdominal mass may be present

- Signs suggestive of hepatocellular cause:

- Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEM
- Systemic signs of acute infection (fever, looking unwell, etc.) may be present
- Tenderness in the right hypochondrium may be present
- Liver may or may not be enlarged

- Signs suggestive of liver dysfunction:

- Altered conscious state (hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEM
- Signs of bleeding tendencies (skin, gums, GI, lung, brain, etc.)
- Signs of hypoalbuminemia (generalized edema, ascites, pleural effusions)

- Acute or chronic?

- Signs suggestive that the illness is acute (previously well, well thrived, normal development, no symptoms of chronic liver disease)
- Signs suggestive of chronic liver disease (may have failure to thrive, palmar erythema, clubbing)

- Signs of portal hypertension (splenomegaly, dilated superficial abdominal veins, ascites, enlarged spleen, caput medusae)
- Signs of dysmorphism, other malformations (suggestive of a genetic cause; intrauterine infections, other teratogens)
- Signs for developmental delay (genetic cause, intra-uterine infection, previous hepatic encephalopathy, previous hypoglycemia, pervious hyperammonemia, some forms of inborn errors of metabolism)
- Signs of bruising, bleeding (causes include thrombocytopenia due to hypersplenism, clotting factor deficiencies due to liver dysfunction, and/or vitamin K deficiency) (see Chapter 14)
- Signs of ADEK deficiencies (due to prolonged cholestasis and fat absorption, visual impairment, corneal changes, rickets, peripheral neuropathy, bruising, bleeding)

79

![img-19.jpeg](img-19.jpeg)

5. Algorithm
A liver function test is usually needed in narrowing down the differentials for jaundice.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Very irritable/lethargic/decreased conscious level (which may suggest hepatic encephalopathy, hyperammonemia, hypoglycemia, IEM)
- Abnormal vital signs (may be a sign of distributive shock, hypovolemic shock, etc.)
- Any sign of bleeding
- Any sign of severe pallor
- Hypoglycemia

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 9-year-old girl was admitted to the hospital with jaundice. Her parents had noticed a yellowish tinge to her skin and eyes over the past few days. They were also concerned that the child had intermittent fever (maximum recorded temperature was 38.2°C) over the past three days. The child had a reduced appetite, and felt nauseated with occasional vomiting. There were two episodes of loose stools a few days ago that has now completely settled. Parents also reported that the child had been passing dark urine, although stools were pigmented. The family had recently returned from a neighboring country. She was not on any medication and there were no relevant medical conditions in any family member. Physical examination revealed a well-grown child with both the height and weight around the 50th percentile for age. Vital signs were normal. She was alert and oriented. There was yellowish discoloration of the sclerae of her eyes and skin. There was no pallor, clubbing, or lymphadenopathy, and the rest of the general examination was unremarkable. Palpation of the abdomen revealed an enlarged and tender liver felt up to 2 cm beneath the right costal margin. There was no splenomegaly. The rest of the systemic examination was unremarkable.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

○ Acute onset of jaundice. This was accompanied by other systemic concerns such as fever, lethargy, and loss of appetite.

• What is your diagnosis?

○ Likely acute infectious hepatitis; most likely Hepatitis A

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

○ Previously well

○ Acute onset

○ Jaundice with dark-colored urine; no pallor, no pale stools

○ Fever, lethargy, loss of appetite

○ Tender hepatomegaly

○ Significant travel history

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis or if this diagnosis is correct, in this case.

○ Presence of diarrhea may not be consistent with the diagnosis of Hepatitis A

• List the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided.

○ No information given on whether she was vaccinated against Hepatitis A and Hepatitis B

○ Not enough information about diet history and food hygiene history

○ More details needed about travel history

○ Any contact history or family member with similar symptoms

○ Any bleeding tendency

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

○ Other forms of hepatitis, e.g., Hepatitis E, EBV, CMV

• For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

○ They have similar symptoms to Hepatitis A.

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

○ None

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

○ EBV and CMV may have additional signs and symptoms such as enlarged lymph nodes, periorbital swelling, and rash.

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Most likely acute infective hepatitis; probably Hepatitis A

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ None

○ May be at risk for dehydration and hypoglycemia if not feeding well or not retaining fluids

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Order investigations

■ Liver function test, gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT)

■ Blood glucose

■ Anti-Hepatitis A IgM antibodies

■ Further investigations will be indicated if the above test results are not consistent with Hepatitis A infection or if her clinical condition worsens, e.g., signs of liver failure

Management of acute Hepatitis A infection is symptomatic and supportive

Adequate hydration is to be maintained.
Specific antiviral therapy is neither available nor necessary.
Watch her. Although Hepatitis A is usually a self-limited viral illness with children recovering fully with no residual sequelae, it can progress to fulminant liver failure in around \(1\%\) of patients. Hence, close clinical monitoring and post-discharge follow-up are essential.
Check if there is a need to report to disease surveillance unit.
Check on the need to institute infection control policy, e.g., contact precautions, stool precautions.
May want to review family members who travelled with her.

# Lumps and Bumps

Nyo Yoke Lin

# DEFINITION OF LUMP AND BUMP

- A lump or a bump in a child is a mass or swelling that may be found in any part of the child's anatomy.

# SYMPTOMS

- The symptom is usually primarily related to the lump or the bump itself, i.e., a swelling.
- Sometimes, the patient will present with the associated symptoms but may not have noticed the lump; it is the doctor who then detects the lump or bump.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- The etiology can be quite varied and it can be quite difficult for the novice to reach a diagnosis.
- There are some lumps and bumps that are more common in the pediatric patient, and there are some that are more commonly found in certain regions of the anatomy.
- See Table 40-1.

TABLE 40-1

PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

|  Pathophysiology | Common Diagnoses  |
| --- | --- |
|  Inflammatory/infectious/autoimmune | - Abscess - Enlarged lymph nodes (infective lymphadenitis, Kawasaki disease, viral) - Infected sinus or fistula - Molluscum - Osgood–Schattler disease (knee) - Pott puffy tumor - Reaction to insect bite - Salivary gland enlargement (infective, autoimmune) - Warts  |
|  Traumatic | - Callus - Caput secundaum - Hematoma - Implantation dermoid  |

432

TABLE 40-1

PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES—cont'd

|  Pathophysiology | Common Diagnoses  |
| --- | --- |
|   | - Keloid - Mucocele - Pyogenic granuloma - Ranula  |
|  Iatrogenic | - Incision hernia - Stitch granuloma  |
|  Neoplastic | - **Benign neoplasm**     - Fibroadenoma of the breast, pilomatrixoma     - Hemangioma     - Histiocytoma/dermatofibroma     - Lipoma     - Neurofibroma - **Malignant neoplasm**     - Basal cell carcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, melanoma     - Lymph-node enlargement (leukemia, lymphoma, metastasis)     - Osteosarcoma     - Parotid gland tumor     - Primitive neuroectodermal tumor, e.g., neuroblastoma     - Rhabdomyosarcoma     - Testicular cancer, leukemia, seminoma, yolk sac tumor     - Thyroid cancer  |
|  Embryonic development, fetal maturation, and neonatal changes | - Arteriovenous malformation, hemangioma, lymphatic malformation, cystic hygroma - Brachial cyst/sinus/fistula - Dermoid cyst - Ectopic thyroid - Epidermal cyst - Hernia - Hydrocele - Pharyngeal pouch - Sebaceous cyst - Thyroglossal cyst - Undescended testis - Varicocole  |
|  Degenerative | - Ganglion  |
|  Metabolic | - Goiter - Breast tissue  |

- An approach to region-specific diagnoses and an approach to general lumps are described here
- A good history of the circumstance surrounding the discovery of the lump is necessary. Of greatest importance are the time, space, and associations.

# Time

- In particular, two aspects of time are of essence: how old the child is and how old the lump is.
- The younger the child or the earlier the discovery is, the more likely it is congenital.

# Space

- The location of the lump is important. Specifically, if the lump is in the head, neck, or groin region, some diagnoses are more common and important to consider.
- The learned student should try to determine from history the physical examination which "depth" of tissue the lump arises from.

# Associations

- The associated symptoms give clues to help the student narrow down the differentials.
- These associations include the following:

# Pain:

- Pain that is of recent onset, throbbing pain with warmth, swelling and loss of function suggest inflammation or infection.
- Pain that is of insidious onset and worse at night is suggestive of malignancy such as that of osteosarcoma.

# Changes in size:

- A lump that is steadily growing larger is suggestive of something progressive.
- A lump that fluctuates in size can help to narrow down the differential diagnosis. For example, a hernia can fluctuate in size with abdominal straining. A hydrocele tends to be larger at the end of the day compared to when the child had just woken up in the morning.

Does it disappear? A hernia may completely "disappear" when it is reduced.

Previous surgery/intervention: lump at the previous surgery site can suggest incisional hernia. Do note that an abscess that is partially treated with antibiotics may not display all the cardinal signs of inflammation.

Is there any other lump?

Other features: For example, the color and texture of the overlying skin; the surface, temperature, shape, and consistency of the lump

# • Physical examination

o Vital signs
o Growth
o A good examination of the lump

▪ Position of the lump in the anatomy – which part of the body

▪ Position of the lump in terms of depth/which tissue

▪ Color and texture of the overlying skin, surface

▪ Temperature

▪ Tenderness

▪ Shape

▪ Size

▪ Edge

▪ Consistency

▪ Fluctuance

▪ Translucency

▪ Pulsatility

▪ Compressibility

▪ Reducibility

▪ Relation to surrounding structures

o Examine the relevant draining lymphatic region
o Examine the body for other similar lumps, associated features
o Decide on which other body systems to examine based on the differential diagnosis

# 1. Region-Specific Algorithm to Lumps and Bumps

- Head lumps and bumps

o Differential diagnosis

- Common: caput secundeum, chignon, cephalohematoma, hemangioma, dermoid cyst, lymph node, parotid salivary gland swellings, infected preauricular sinus, scalp abscess, facial abscess, sebaceous cyst, lipoma, and mucocele of the lip
- Uncommon: encephalocele, Pott puffy tumor, basal cell carcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, and melanoma

# 2. General Approach to Lumps and Bumps that Can Occur Anywhere in the Body (Including Torso, Back, Abdomen, Limbs)

• Differential diagnosis

o Common: insect bite, warts, molluscum, hematoma, fracture/callus, hernia, abscess, lymph node, sebaceous cyst, lipoma, pyogenic granuloma pilomatrixoma, epidermal cyst, dermoid cyst, implantation dermoid, ganglion, vascular malformation, keloid
o Uncommon: rhabdomyosarcoma, osteosarcoma, chondrosarcoma, primitive neuroectodermal tumor, neurofibroma, histiocytoma/dermatofibroma, basal cell carcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, melanoma

# • Algorithm

• For lumps that occur outside the regions mentioned above as well as lumps that do not fall nicely into the space specific pathways, more detailed analysis of the history as well as the physical characteristics will be necessary.
• Tables 40-2 and 40-3 analyze different aspects of history and physical examination, the implications, as well as the possible diagnoses.

TABLE 40-2

|  Aspect of History | Implications | Examples  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  How old is the patient? | Certain lumps are common at some ages and not common in others | Sebaceous cysts are not common prior to adolescence  |
|  How old is the lump? When was the lump first noticed? | A lump noticed early on in infancy or since birth is more likely to be congenital | Dermoid cyst, hernia, epidermal cyst, vascular malformation  |
|  What made the patient/parent notice the lump? | A younger child may not complain about pain or a lump, and it is likely that the caregiver notices the lump | Sternocleidomastoid tumor is usually noticed by carer when the child looks preferentially to one side, and/or there is a lump in the neck found when bathing the infant.  |
|   | An older child should be able to complain about pain but may sometimes ignore a painless lump for prolonged periods |   |

|  Has the lump changed? | Insidious growth of a lump with very slow increase in size over years suggests benign neoplasm or accumulation of secretion | Lipoma, sebaceous cyst, ganglion, dermoid cyst  |
|   | Sudden increase in size of a preexisting lump with redness and pain suggests secondary infection | Infected sebaceous cyst  |
