# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Evidence of infection
- Evidence of rapid growth
- Evidence of bleeding
- Evidence of persistent pain
- Weight loss
- Solid, spherical mass (which suggests rapid growth)

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 1-year-old boy presents to the clinic. His parents noticed that the right scrotum was much larger than the left scrotum. The parents seem to think that this enlargement happened very recently. The child did not seem to be in pain. There was no fever. On examination, his vital signs were stable. The right scrotum was much larger than the left scrotum. There seems to be mass in the right scrotum. It was not tender.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Painless groin swelling that involves the right scrotum

- What is your diagnosis?

- Indirect inguinal hernia that is not strangulated

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Male
- Sudden onset
- Painless
- Swelling of the right scrotal sac
- Mass felt

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Whether or not the mass was reducible
- Whether or not one could get above the mass
- Whether or not the mass could be transilluminated

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Hydrocele, testicular tumor

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Hydrocele: Painless, swollen scrotal sac
- Testicular tumor: Painless, swollen scrotal sac, presence of a mass

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Hydrocele, tumor: none but maybe not so common in this age group

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Hydrocele: transilluminates, cannot be reduced
- Testicular tumor: can get over the mass, does not transilluminate, cannot be reduced

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Inguinal hernia that is not strangulated

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- None but may be at risk for strangulation

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Ultrasound of scrotum
- Referral to a surgeon

# Noisy Breathing

Mahesh Babu Ramamurthy • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF NOISY BREATHING

- Normal breathing is effortless and noiseless.
- Any noise during breathing is abnormal.

# SYMPTOMS OF NOISY BREATHING

- It is important to clarify what exactly the patient/parent means by noisy breathing.
- Even if a patient/parent uses a medical term, e.g., wheeze, it is still important to clarify what exactly they mean. Many parents use the terms incorrectly. That is, the parent thinks the child is wheezing, but when the clinician hears that noisy breathing sound, that sound is not wheezing but some other noise.

- Symptoms can be related to the

  - Timing of the noise

    - Noise during inhalation
    - Noise during exhalation
    - Noise during inhalation and exhalation

  - Nature of the noise

    - Intermittent or continuous
    - High pitched or low pitched
    - Hoarse, coarse, whistling sound

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Pathophysiology

- The respiratory tract is divided into extrathoracic structures, thoracic inlet, and intrathoracic structures.

  - Extrathoracic structures include the nasopharynx, epiglottis, glottis, subglottic area, and the upper trachea. Extrathoracic structures are surrounded by atmospheric pressure.
  - The thoracic inlet is the plane that separates the extrathoracic and intrathoracic structures.
  - Intrathoracic structures begin from the lower trachea and extend below. Intrathoracic structures are surrounded by intrathoracic pressure.

- Noisy breathing occurs when there is sufficient turbulent airflow in the airways. Turbulent airflow is usually generated when there is passage of air through an airway that has increased resistance, e.g., narrowed, partially obstructed airway.

448

- If there is no air flowing through (i.e., not breathing or if the obstruction is very severe or complete) or if the narrowing does not create significant airway resistance, there will be no noisy breathing. For example, there is no noisy breathing in apnea, choking, or severe asthma.

- Airway narrowings can be fixed or variable.

o Fixed: If the airway narrowing occurs in an airway structure that is rigid, the airway narrowing is fixed, i.e., there is no change in airway caliber during inhalation or exhalation. The fixed narrowing results in a constant amount of airflow resistance during inspiration and exhalation. As such, any noisy breathing generated is similar during inspiration and expiration. An example of fixed airway narrowing is subglottic stenosis.

o Variable:

- If the airway narrowing occurs in an airway structure that has flexible/pliable walls, then the airway narrowing can be variable, i.e., there are changes in airway caliber during inhalation and/or exhalation.
- The nature of these changes is dependent on the anatomical site of the narrowing.
- If the variable narrowing is in the extrathoracic airway, during inspiration, there is acceleration of airflow from the atmosphere toward the lungs, and the intraluminal pressure decreases. If the intraluminal airway pressure falls below the surrounding atmospheric pressure, the extrathoracic airway will collapse/narrow down even more, creating more airway resistance and potential noisy breathing. During expiration, the air is forced out of the lungs through the narrowed but expandable extrathoracic airway. Hence, extrathoracic narrowing is more likely to cause noisy breathing during inspiration than expiration.
- If the variable narrowing is in the intrathoracic airway, during inspiration, the intrathoracic pressure decreases, causing an increase in the size of the airway lumen. During expiration, there is increased pleural pressure, which leads to a decrease in size of the airway lumen. If the decrease in lumen size is significant, there will be increased airway resistance and potential noisy breathing. Hence, intrathoracic narrowing is more likely to cause noisy breathing during expiration than inspiration.

## 2. The Common Breathing Noises

- The common breathing-related noises heard are

o Stridor: a high-pitched, monophonic, grating, or creaking sound; usually occurs during inspiration but can also occur during exhalation
o Stertor: a low-pitch inspiratory noise that is akin to the sound of heavy snoring or gasping
o Snoring: the sound of stertor that occurs during sleep

- Snort: a rough sounding heard during inspiration and/or exhalation
- Rattle: a crackling sound that is heard during inspiration and/or exhalation
- Wheeze: a high pitched whistling sound; it can be heard during inspiration and/or exhalation
- Grunting: a short, low pitched, hoarse sound heard in exhalation.

### 3. Common Causes of Stridor

- Stridor is due to airflow going through a narrowed airway at the level of the larynx, and upper trachea.
- Differentiating stridor as acute, subacute, or chronic helps to narrow down the differential diagnoses.
- Acute onset stridor

- Acute = sudden onset, e.g., over hours

- Causes:

  - Noninfective

    - Foreign body aspiration – this happens especially in an infant or toddler
    - Secretions
    - Anaphylaxis – other manifestations include cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, skin manifestations
    - Airway burns – hot air/smoke/hot liquids/chemical
    - Trauma to face/neck

  - Infective

    - Mainly due to acute epiglottitis.
    - Usually presents as a febrile toxic child less than 5 years of age (though it can appear at any age) who characteristically has stridor, open mouth, drooling, and dysphagia.
    - Incidence has decreased due to Hib vaccination.

- Subacute onset stridor

- Subacute = comes on gradually and progresses over a few days.
- Causes in a relatively well-looking child:

  - Croup: Usually in children aged between 1 and 4 years. Starts with symptoms of upper respiratory tract infection, and progresses to a barking cough and inspiratory stridor within 1–3 days.

  - Most cases of croup are mild but rarely, croup can cause severe respiratory distress.

- Causes in an ill-looking child:

  - Retropharyngeal abscess: these children start initially with an upper respiratory tract infection, but it progresses to a sick-looking child with high-spiking fever. The neck is held in an extended position and there is associated dysphagia.
  - Peritonsillar abscess: this usually occurs in older children who are febrile and toxic. They present with unilateral throat pain, and the neck is usually turned to one side.

■ Bacterial tracheitis: children with bacterial tracheitis present with similar symptoms to croup, but these kids are older, have higher fevers, and look sicker than a child with croup.

# • Chronic onset stridor

○ Chronic = has been there for quite a while.
○ The stridor is usually mild and usually not associated with failure to thrive.
○ However, if there is significant airway obstruction, the work of breathing is increased. This is then associated with failure to thrive and feeding difficulties.

# ○ Causes:

■ Laryngomalacia
■ Tracheobronchomalacia
■ Laryngeal web
■ Laryngeal cysts
■ Vocal cord paralysis
■ Vocal cord dysfunction
■ Papillomatosis
■ Subglottic stenosis
■ Subglottic hemangioma
■ Vascular ring

# 4. Common Causes of Wheezing

• Wheezing = significant partial obstruction in the lower and distal airways

○ When the obstruction is mild, breath sounds are normal and no wheezing is heard.
○ When the obstruction is more severe, the wheeze can be heard, more often during expiration than inspiration.
○ When the obstruction is extremely severe, there will be no wheeze heard as there is insufficient airflow to generate a noise.

• Wheezing may be associated prolonged expiratory phase.

• A wheeze can either be monophonic or polyphonic.

○ Monophonic wheeze (one musical note): narrowing is in the proximal or central airway (tracheomalacia, extrinsic compression of trachea, etc.)
○ Polyphonic wheeze (multiple different musical notes heard simultaneously): narrowing is in the distal airway obstruction (asthma, bronchiolitis)

• A wheeze can be localized or generalized.

○ Localized (limited to one side of the chest or one lobe): anatomical causes like foreign body (there may be a history of choking), extrinsic compression by lymph nodes, etc.
○ Generalized: suggests diffuse involvement of the lung airways (asthma, anaphylaxis, bronchiolitis)

• Wheezing can be one-off, persistent or recurrent/intermittent.

○ One-off: foreign body, viral-induced wheeze, allergic reaction

1

- Persistent: suggests congenital or permanent structural abnormality
- Recurrent: suggests reversible/dynamic/variable causes, e.g., asthma, viral-induced wheeze, cystic fibrosis, primary ciliary dyskinesia, tracheobronchomalacia

- Wheezing may be associated with extrapulmonary symptoms:

- Coryza
- Cough
- Fever
- Features of atopic dermatitis, allergic rhinitis, allergic conjunctivitis
- Failure to thrive
- Ear infections
- Sinusitis
- Nasal polyp

# 5. Common Causes of Grunting

- Grunting is caused by the closing of vocal cords early in expiration and the continued exhalation against the closed glottis.
- It is an attempt to generate positive end expiratory pressure so as to improve oxygenation.
- It is a sign of respiratory distress usually seen in neonates and young babies.
- It is indicative of significant parenchymal illness like pneumonia and atelectasis. See Chapter 12 Breathlessness and Respiratory Distress.

# 6. Common Causes of Stertor, Snoring, Snort

- Stertor/snoring/snort is caused by vibrations of the nasopharynx, pharynx, and/or soft palate due to air flowing through a partial upper airway obstruction that is above the larynx.

- The common causes are

  - Infective

    - Upper respiratory tract infection

  - Noninfective

    - Causes of obstructive sleep apnea

      - Tonsillar-adenoid hypertrophy
      - Upper airway abnormalities, e.g., midface hypoplasia
      - Hypotonia
      - Obesity

    - Other causes

# 7. Common Causes of Rattle

- Rattle is due to vibrations/noise caused by airflow through phlegm/fluid in the lower respiratory tract.
- Common causes are

  - Infection, e.g., pneumonia, bronchitis, bronchiolitis
  - Asthma
  - Pulmonary edema

CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

![img-27.jpeg](img-27.jpeg)

1. 2017年，公司与上海浦东发展银行股份有限公司签订了《关于使用部分闲置募集资金进行现金管理的协议》。

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

  - Check pulse

    - Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
    - Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

  - When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

- If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- History: What exactly is meant by noisy breathing

- Determine if it is normal breathing sound or abnormal breathing sound.
- If it is abnormal, determine if it is stridor, stertor, snoring, snort, rattle, wheeze, grunting, etc.

- History: Look for the cause of the noisy breathing

  - Stridor

    - Determine if it is acute, subacute, or chronic.
    - Determine if it is during inspiration, expiration, or both.
    - Look for the cause of the airway obstruction.

    - Any preceding event

      - Choking
      - Aspiration of feeds or foreign body
      - Trauma to face/neck
      - Exposure to allergen
      - Exposure to noxious gas, chemicals, hot air

1

☐ Potential risk factors or causative factor

- Symptoms of infection (fever, cough)
- History suggestive of gastroesophageal reflux
- History suggestive of unfavorable anatomy
- Swallowing difficulties (acute epiglottitis, swallowing dysfunction, extraluminal mass)
- Loss of weight, loss of appetite, feeding difficulty (malignancy, sign of severe airway obstruction, causes of swallowing difficulties)

# ○ Wheezing, rhonchi

- Determine if it is the first time, recurrent, or persistent.
- Look for the cause of the lower airway obstruction

☐ Any preceding event

- Rhinorrhea, fever to suggest an infectious trigger to wheezing
- Choking
- Aspiration of feeds or foreign body
- Exposure to allergen
- Exposure to noxious gas, chemicals, hot air

☐ Potential risk factors or causative factor

- Personal or family history of atopy to suggest predisposition to bronchodilator-responsive wheeze
- Symptoms of infection (fever, cough)
- History suggestive of gastroesophageal reflux
- History suggestive of unfavorable anatomy

# ○ Grunting

- Look for lung parenchymal disease (collapse, pneumonia/bronchopneumonia, pneumonitis, pulmonary contusion, prematurity associated respiratory distress syndrome).
- Look for causes of pulmonary edema

☐ Intravascular fluid overload
☐ Inadequate myocardial contractility
☐ Submersion injury (near drowning)

# ○ Sterter, snoring, snort

- Determine if it is the first episode, intermittent, or chronic/persistent.
- If it is the first episode, see if there is evidence of upper respiratory tract infection.
- If it is intermittent/chronic

☐ Determine if there is evidence of obstructive sleep apnea.
☐ If there is, determine the cause.

# ○ Rattle

- Determine if it is the first episode, intermittent, or chronic/persistent.
- Look for evidence of infection, asthma, fluid overload.

• History: If there is a chronic respiratory symptom, look for the cause and complications.

○ Any underlying chronic respiratory disease?
○ Any symptoms of gastrointestinal reflux?
○ Any symptoms of aspiration?
○ Any personal history of atopy?
○ Any cardiac condition?
○ Any metabolic disease?
○ Any neurological condition?
○ Any immunodeficiency?
○ Any failure to thrive?
○ Any contact history for infection, tuberculosis, household cigarette smoke?
○ Immunization history
○ Medication history
○ Treatment history
○ Any effect on growth?
○ Any effect on development?
○ Any family history of genetic conditions, atopy, etc.?
○ Do a systems review.

• Physical examination

○ Vital signs

■ High heart rate – do not assume that it is because the child is anxious or frightened or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress and/or hypoxia.
■ Low heart rate – this can be seen when there is prolonged severe respiratory distress and the child is dying.
■ High blood pressure – do not assume that it is because the child is anxious or frightened or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress.
■ Low blood pressure – this can be seen when there is shock, and when there is prolonged severe respiratory distress and the child is dying.
■ High respiratory rate – do not assume that it is because the child is anxious or frightened or upset. It may be a sign of the child having severe respiratory distress and/or hypoxia.
■ Low respiratory rate or poor respiratory effort – this can be seen when the central nervous system is depressed, when the respiratory muscles are not working adequately, when the child is in severe respiratory distress, or when the child is tiring out.
■ Decreased oxygenation and or central cyanosis – assess if the child is in severe respiratory distress. Do not assume that the low SpO₂ value is due to poor contact with saturation probe or patient movement.

○ Posture of the child

■ Learning forward and bracing the shoulder girdle are signs of increased breathing effort required.

# • Any unusual sound

- Stridor (inspiratory, expiratory or biphasic)
- Wheezing
- Grunting
- Stertor, snoring, snort
- Rattle
- Coughing (wet sounding, dry sounding, barking cough, etc.)

# • Do a respiratory examination if there is no severe upper airway obstruction.

- Head, ears, nose throat
- Chest wall
  - Signs of injury
  - Chest expansion increased, decreased, asymmetrical

- Trachea position

- Apex beat

- Percussion note

- Air entry

- Adventitious sounds and their distribution

  - Sounds

    - Crepitations (fine or coarse)
    - Rhonchi (monophonic or polyphonic)

  - Other characteristic of the sound

    - Inspiratory, expiratory, or both
    - Localized or generalized
    - Unilateral or bilateral
    - Does it correspond to which part of the lung/anatomy

• Do a detailed cardiovascular examination.

• Examine the rest of the body.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

• Any sign of respiratory distress is worrying, but one should especially note the following red flags suggestive of severe respiratory distress:

  - Very irritable/lethargic/decreased conscious level
  - Markedly increased or markedly reduced respiratory rate
  - Very obvious signs of increased breathing effort
  - Central cyanosis/hypoxia
  - Biphasic stridor at any time or stridor in a child at rest – suggests severe partial upper airway obstruction

• Pay attention to the patient with moderate respiratory distress too as these patients may deteriorate despite treatment.

# CLINICAL SCENARIO

A 2-year-old boy is brought in to the pediatric clinic for consultation with history of noisy breathing. The mother gives a history of recurrent episodes of wheezing in the past 8 months. The child had four episodes of whistling

noise in the past 8 months. Each time this was preceded by an upper respiratory infection and then followed by the noisy breathing, which lasted for 7–10 days. He was given salbutamol inhaler by his doctor but did not respond to it. No history of stridor. No interval symptoms. No history of wheeze after activity or history suggestive of allergic rhinitis or atopic dermatitis. No family history of atopy.

On detailed questioning, mother did give a history that she had heard similar sounds, though of lesser intensity almost from birth till the child with 6–7 months of age. The baby used to have these noises especially when he was excited and while crying. The child was feeding well and thriving for age. Immunized for age.

On examination, both weight and height were on the 25th percentile. Vital signs are respiratory rate 32/min, heart rate 100/min, blood pressure 88/56 mmHg, and oxygen saturations 97% in room air. Patient is playful. Chest revealed a monophonic expiratory wheeze heard bilaterally. Good air entry heard bilaterally. Other systemic examinations were within normal limits.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Recurrent wheezing

- What is your diagnosis?

- Tracheomalacia

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Wheezing is during expiration; hence, it is arising from the intrathoracic airways.
- Monophonic wheeze suggests that it seems to be arising from a central airway like trachea or major bronchi.

- The noise was also increasing when the baby cried and was excited – hence suggesting a dynamic/variable cause.
- An infection that causes airway swelling/secretion can unmask an underlying latent tracheomalacia as in this child.
- Noise was heard in early infancy almost right from birth – suggests a congenital cause for this noise.
- Tracheomalacia is known to manifest in early infancy.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis, or if this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- None

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Recurrent bronchiolitis

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Associated with upper respiratory tract infection
- Wheezing that is intermittent
- Wheezing that is not responsive to bronchiodilator
- No personal history of atopy
- Young age of onset of wheezing

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Noisy breathing that was heard from very young
- Noisy breathing that was present when not having an upper respiratory tract infection
- Too many episodes of acute bronchiolitis for an otherwise healthy baby

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Associated with fever, positive contact history

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Tracheomalacia

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- None

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Observe.
- Give a red flag warning of respiratory distress.
- Discuss that certain coexisting conditions can trigger respiratory distress, e.g., respiratory tract infection.
- As the child is quite well in between infection, we may not need to scope to prove the underlying tracheomalacia.
- However, if the child does not outgrow it or if it worsens, the child may need imaging/bronchoscopy.

# Obesity

Lee Yung Seng

# DEFINITION OF OBESITY

- Obesity is a state of excessive adiposity.
- In clinical practice, it is usually defined as one of the following:

o Body mass index (BMI) more than 95th or 97th percentile of the local gender-specific BMI for age percentile chart

■ The use of 95th or 97th percentile cut-off depends on each country's practice.
■ The Centre for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) growth chart adopts 95th percentile using its BMI for age chart.

o BMI > 3 standard deviations above the WHO Child Growth Standards median (for birth to 5 years)
o BMI > 2 standard deviations above the WHO Child Growth Standards median (for ages 5–19 years)
o Above the international BMI cut-off points corresponding to an adult BMI of 30 kg/m² by age and sex for children aged 2–18 years (using the international chart by the International Obesity Task Force)
o More than 140% of ideal weight for height for the population with local gender-specific percentage ideal weight for height chart

# SYMPTOMS OF OBESITY

- Usually asymptomatic
- Referred for evaluation because obesity was detected by health care professionals
- Increased body size, weight, or abdominal girth.
- Symptoms related to the underlying medical condition causing obesity
- Symptomatic due to obesity-related morbidities

o Polydipsia, polyuria, nocturia (type 2 diabetes mellitus)
o Menstrual abnormalities, hirsutism (polycystic ovarian syndrome)
o Symptoms of obstructive sleep apnea (OSA), e.g., snoring, morning headaches, fatigue, difficulty in breathing during sleep, daytime sleepiness, behavioral disorders, poor school performance
o Symptoms related to orthopedic conditions such as hip pain (slipped capital femoral epiphysis), knee pain, bowing of legs, foot pain (flat foot), back pain.
o Hypertension and fatty liver related issues are usually asymptomatic.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON CAUSES

- Obesity is divided into common obesity or pathological (secondary) obesity

461

• Common obesity

○ Also known as primary obesity, nutritional obesity, or exogenous obesity.

○ Multifactorial trait due to the interplay of the following factors:

■ Polygenic inheritance (i.e., many different genes playing a role)
■ Environmental and behavioral factors:

□ Excessive food intake for the amount of energy required
□ Sedentary lifestyle (environmental and behavioral factors)
□ There is increasing recent evidence of contribution from metagenome of the gut microbiota

■ Developmental factors (in utero and early life influences such as excessive maternal weight gain during pregnancy and short duration of breastfeeding)

• Pathological obesity, or secondary obesity

○ Usually manifests as short and obese

○ Causes

■ Endocrinopathy

□ Cushing syndrome or Cushing disease
□ Hypothyroidism (usually overweight or mild obesity)
□ Hypopituitarism (usually overweight or mild obesity)

■ Genetic/syndromic

□ Prader–Willi syndrome
□ Other rare conditions, e.g., Bardet–Biedel syndrome, pseudohypoparathyroidism

■ Central nervous system (CNS) tumors

□ Craniopharyngioma

• Obesity can be associated with the following comorbidities

○ Metabolic

■ Glucose-related issues (impaired glucose tolerance, impaired fasting glucose, diabetes mellitus)
■ Dyslipidemia
■ Polycystic ovarian syndrome
■ Nonalcoholic fatty liver disease
■ Hypertension
■ Gallstones

○ Mechanical

■ OSA
■ Orthopedic disorders, e.g., slipped capital femoral epiphysis, genu varus, low back pain
■ Pseudotumor cerebri

○ Psychosocial issues

■ Bullying
■ Low self-esteem
■ Depression
■ Eating disorder

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

• The key tasks are

o To determine if the child is really obese.
o If the child is obese, to determine the cause (pathological causes of obesity versus common obesity due to overnutrition).
o To assess for the presence of or risk of obesity-related comorbidities.

# 1. Determine whether There Is obesity

• Take a history of the child's weight and height.
• Plot the current weight, height, and head circumference.
• Plot the past growth parameters to see the trend over time.
• Refer to the definitions of obesity at the start of this chapter.
• See whether the child fulfills any of the criteria for obesity.

# 2. History Taking: Determine the Cause of the Obesity (i.e., Differentiate between Pathological Causes of Obesity and Common Obesity due to Overnutrition)

• Get information about the onset of weight issue, the trend over time. Recent onset and rapid gain in weight is a red flag (may be pathological cause; may be common obesity).
• Get information about the diet (types of foods and drinks, calories, macronutrient composition, micronutrient composition, who is the cook, how often do they eat at home, how often do they eat out).
• Get information about physical activity.
• Assess if the calories consumed are adequate/excessive/inadequate for the daily energy needs.
• Get information about antenatal history, gestational age, birth weight, birth height, and newborn screening for hypothyroidism.
• Ask for associated symptoms.
• Do a systems review, including a developmental history.
• Get information about what treatment has been tried and what was the outcome.
• Get information about family history, e.g., weight issues (overweight, obesity), thyroid issues, other endocrine issues, lipid abnormality, heart disease, hypertension, sudden death.
• There are many possible symptoms. The most common symptoms would be a tall child who eats a lot of calories, has little physical activity, and the obesity may be long-standing. This is commonly seen with common obesity.
• The following scenarios are suggestive that the child is more likely to have a pathological cause of obesity:

o A child who is obese and whose height is <50th percentile
o A child who is obese and short for genetic potential (need to exclude syndromes, endocrinopathy)

1

- A child who is obese and has pubertal delay (need to exclude syndromes, endocrinopathy)
- A child who is obese and has developmental delay (need to exclude syndromes, hypothyroidism, growth hormone deficiency, panhypopituitarism)
- A child who has abnormal neurological symptoms/signs (need to exclude a CNS cause)
- A child who has abnormal vision (need to exclude a CNS tumor, genetic syndrome)
- A child who has headache and vomiting after waking up (may be due to CNS tumor, raised intracranial pressure, or complications of obesity like pseudotumor cerebri, OSA)
- Consanguineous parents increase the risk for a genetic cause of obesity (but such children can also have common obesity)
- Symptoms of hypothyroidism, Cushing syndrome, hypoglycemia

### 3. History Taking: Assess for the Presence of or Risk of Obesity-Related Comorbidities (Metabolic, Mechanical, Psychosocial)

- Are there symptoms indicative of diabetes mellitus, e.g., polydipsia and polyuria?
- Any previous blood glucose level or HbA1C level checked?
- Are there features suggestive of insulin resistance, e.g., acanthosis nigricans, and irregular menstruation suggestive of polycystic ovarian syndrome (PCOS)?
- Are there significant risk factors for diabetes mellitus, e.g., small for gestational age, large for gestational age, lack or short duration of breast-feeding, susceptible ethnicity (e.g., Asian, Hispanic)?
- Any history of abnormal lipid profile?
- Any history of hypertension?
- Any history of irregular menses or hirsutism if the patient is female (suggestive of PCOS)?
- Any history of abnormal liver panel or abnormal liver ultrasound (suggestive of nonalcoholic fatty liver disease)?
- Any history of upper quadrant abdominal pain? Episodes of fever and jaundice? Gallstones seen on ultrasound?
- Any symptom of snoring, morning headaches, fatigue, difficulty in breathing during sleep, daytime sleepiness, behavioral disorders, poor school performance (suggestive of OSA)?
- Any symptom of headache, especially upon waking up (suggestive of OSA, pseudotumor cerebri, CNS tumors, raised intracranial pressure)?
- Any symptom of back pain, hip pain, knee pain, foot pain, bowing of the legs (suggestive of orthopedic disorders such as mechanical lower back pain, slipped capital femoral epiphysis, genu varus, flat feet)?

# 4. Physical Examination

- Weigh the child.
- Measure the height.
- Measure the head circumference if the child is <6 years old.
- Plot the growth measurements; present ones and past ones too if available.
- Obtain the parents' heights; calculate mid-parental height and the target height range on the basis of mid-parental height (see Chapter 2).
- Measure vital signs.
- Do a head-to-toe examination.
- Possible findings include

- Tall child (commonly seen in common obesity)
- Short child (suggestive of a pathological cause, e.g., endocrinopathy, genetic syndrome)
- High blood pressure (may be stress or a complication of obesity or a sign of raised intracranial pressure)
- Dysmorphic (suggestive of a genetic cause)
- Acanthosis nigricans (suggestive of insulin resistance)
- Hirsutism in a female patient (suggestive of polycystic ovarian syndrome)
- Mouth breathing (suggestive of OSA)
- Enlarged tonsils (may increase the risk for OSA)
- Goiter (need to exclude thyroid issues)
- Central obesity (may be a sign of Cushing syndrome)
- Enlarged liver (suggestive of fatty liver/nonalcoholic liver disease)
- Murphy sign (cholecystitis)
- Striae (may be related to increase in weight, may be a sign of Cushing syndrome)
- Musculoskeletal-related findings (most likely related to complications of obesity; may be a cause of obesity as in it results in decreased activity)
- Depressed, withdrawn, angry, self-harm (most likely a complication of obesity; some endocrinopathies may predispose to depression and mood changes)
- Abnormal CNS findings

- Bitemporal hemianopia (need to exclude CNS tumor near optic chiasma)

- Visual loss (CNS tumor, some genetic syndromes)

- Slow reflexes (hypothyroidism)

![img-28.jpeg](img-28.jpeg)

5. Algorithm

# LOOK OUT FOR THESE RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Child's height is below 50th percentile
- Child's height is below the target height range (calculated on the basis of mid-parental height)
- Child has slow height velocity
- Rapid gain in weight
- Pubertal delay
- Developmental delay
- Neurological symptoms/signs
- Hypertension
- Abnormal vision
- Headache and vomiting after waking up
- Evidence of hypothyroidism, Cushing syndrome

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 12-year-old boy presents to you as his parents are concerned that he is too fat. He has no major medical history of note. He has been growing and developing appropriately, but noted to be fat since preschool. He often complains of dull headache, and is often sleepy in the daytime. He has poor performance in school, and teachers have remarked about his poor attention span. He does not have polydipsia or polyuria. His parents are also overweight; his mother had gestational diabetes when pregnant with him, and now she has type 2 diabetes on oral medication.

On examination, he is 158 cm tall (97th percentile) and weighs 80 kg (>97th percentile), and BMI was 32 kg/m² (>97th percentile). There is acanthosis nigricans on his neck and axillae. Blood pressure is 120/75 mmHg. He is in midpuberty, with pubic hair at Tanner 3 and genitalia Tanner 3, and testicular volume is 8 mL each side.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?
  - List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
  - List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
  - If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.
- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:
  - List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
  - List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
  - If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.
- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

1

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

  ◦ Obesity

• What is your diagnosis?

  ◦ Common obesity with features suggesting that significant comorbidities (increased risk for type 2 diabetes mellitus and OSA)

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

  ◦ Features supporting common obesity: tall and fat, not recent onset, both parents also overweight.

  ◦ Features supporting risk for type 2 diabetes mellitus: acanthosis nigricans, which is a sign of insulin resistance, and he is in puberty; mother had gestational diabetes and now has type 2 diabetes. Features supportive of OSA: daytime somnolence, attention deficit, headaches

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

  ◦ None

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

  ◦ No systemic review (i.e., run the risk of missing an important symptom)
  ◦ Incomplete physical examination findings (i.e., run the risk of missing an important physical abnormality)
  ◦ No growth chart with serial growth parameters plotted
  ◦ No information about parents' height and target height range

• What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

  ◦ No alternative diagnosis

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

  ◦ At risk for type 2 diabetes mellitus
  ◦ Symptoms suggestive of OSA

• What is your plan for this patient?

  ◦ Organize oral glucose tolerance test, or fasting, or random venous glucose test.
  ◦ Offer lipid screen, liver function test.
  ◦ Organize an overnight polysomnography; if indicative of OSA, to offer tonsillectomy and adenoidectomy and/or continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) therapy during sleep.
  ◦ Advice diet and exercise regimen.
  ◦ Enlist multidisciplinary team assistance in management, including dietitian, exercise therapist.

# Painful Joint, Painful Limb

Elizabeth Y. Ang • Denise L.M. Goh • Quah Thuan Chong

# DEFINITION OF PAINFUL JOINT, PAINFUL LIMB

Any joint or limb that is painful.

# SYMPTOMS OF PAINFUL JOINT, PAINFUL LIMB

- An older child can tell you or point to whichever part of the joint or limb is painful. If the pain is severe,

- There may be reduction in movement and/or reduction in function.
- There may be associated symptoms such as swelling, redness, warmth, stiffness, tenderness, altered skin, discoloration, etc.

- An infant or a younger child is often unable to verbalize or localize pain. In such children, the indicators of pain may be

- Reduced movement of the affected limb ("pseudoparalysis")
- Reduction in function, e.g., limp, refusal to walk or reach out for objects
- Unexplained crying
- Crying on movement
- Resistance to passive movement, e.g., when helping the child with dressing
- Noted to be stiff, clumsy, or slow in attainment of motor milestones
- Associated symptoms such as swelling, redness, warmth, tenderness, altered skin coloration, etc.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Pathophysiology

- A painful joint may be the result of pathology in the bone (specifically, the epiphysis or metaphysis), joint space (cartilage, synovium, synovial fluid), skin and soft tissues (tendons, ligaments, subcutaneous structures), or nerves supplying the area (as in reflex sympathetic dystrophy).
- A painful limb may be the result of pathology in the underlying bone, skin and soft tissue, or nerves.
- Sometimes, the child may experience "referred pain"; most commonly, the child indicates the pain is in the knee when the pathology is really in the hip.
- Often, the pathology affects several of these structures at one time, so it is useful to simultaneously think of the possible etiologies/diagnosis.

# 2. Common Causes/Diagnoses

- Trauma/overuse

- Fracture, dislocation, soft tissue injury, Osgood–Schlatter disease and related apophysitides, hypermobility syndrome, tendonitis, non-accidental injury, foreign body

469

• Orthopedic/mechanical

○ Avascular necrosis, slipped capital femoral epiphysis (SCFE)

• Infection-related

○ Septic arthritis, osteomyelitis, viral arthritis, cellulitis, soft tissue infection, myositis

• Inflammatory/autoimmune

○ Reactive arthritis, rheumatic fever, JIA, Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), Henoch–Schönlein purpura, arthritis associated with other systemic inflammatory diseases (e.g., inflammatory bowel disease), serum sickness

• Tumors (benign or malignant)

○ Leukemia, lymphoma

○ Neuroblastoma

○ Bone tumors (osteosarcoma, Ewing sarcoma, benign bone tumors)

○ Synovial hemangioma, pigmented villonodular synovitis (PVNS)

• Noninflammatory

○ Growing pains, fibromyalgia, reflex sympathetic dystrophy (RSD), conversion reaction/psychogenic pain, chronic fatigue syndrome

• Hematologic

○ Hemophilia with hemarthrosis

• Neurogenic

○ Peripheral neuropathy, e.g., Fabry disease

# 3. Manifestations of Important Causes/Diagnoses

• Trauma and/or traumatic arthritis

○ If the swollen joint is due to trauma, there is usually a clear history of significant injury immediately prior to the onset of joint pain and swelling.

○ Traumatic serosanguineous effusions can cause fever due to reaction to the blood within the joint.

○ Examination may also review tense swelling from a hemarthrosis with bruising.

○ The affected joint is usually very painful.

○ The affected child may deny trauma for fear of punishment.

○ Often, even if the trauma is mild and unrelated to the underlying etiology, the parent may still volunteer this information, when the diagnosis is really inflammatory arthritis or a malignant bone tumor (osteosarcoma, Ewing sarcoma). The examining doctor must decide – based on the history and examination – whether the history of trauma is related or not.

• Hypermobility syndrome

○ Benign condition in an otherwise normal child who has recurrent joint pains, usually in the lower limbs toward the end of the day and with physical activity.

○ The finding of hypermobile joints is usually an isolated finding.

○ Rarely, hypermobile joints may be associated with Ehlers–Danlos syndrome, Marfan syndrome, osteogenesis imperfecta,

homocystinuria, trisomy 21, and pseudoxanthoma elasticum. In such cases, look for other signs apart from hypermobility of the joint.

# • Non-accidental injury

• Always consider this in a child with multiple bruises (especially when not over bone edges) in one or several areas of limb or joint pain with documented fractures, especially if they are of varying ages (see Chapter 37).

# • Foreign body with/without infection

• A thorn, shard of glass, or other foreign body may cause a secondary infection or sterile synovitis.
• Children and families may not recall the activity resulting in the foreign body entering the joint.

# • Avascular necrosis

• The most common joint involved in avascular necrosis is the hip joint. Avascular necrosis of the femoral head is called Perthes disease.
• Affected children are usually 5–10 years of age.
• Onset of pain is usually insidious, may be preceded by trauma.
• Prolonged steroid use is a risk factor.
• Less than 10% are bilateral.
• Pain may be referred to the knee or be felt in the anterior thigh.
• The presentation may be as a painless limp.

# • Septic arthritis

• Most but not all children with septic arthritis or osteomyelitis appear ill.
• Typically, the child is unwell, and the affected joint is severely painful, swollen, warm, and red.
• Occasionally, the child with septic arthritis may be well and with just low-grade fevers.
• Tuberculous arthritis may have a more indolent course and be associated with fewer signs of joint inflammation.
• Diagnosis is made by aspirating the joint and sending synovial fluid for Gram stain, culture, and cell count. Blood cultures are positive in approximately 20% of affected patients.
• This is a medical emergency and prompt empirical treatment with parenteral antibiotics against the most common organisms (Staphylococcus, Streptococcus) is needed reduce the risk of joint destruction.

# • Osteomyelitis with/without sympathetic arthritis

• Occasionally, the affected child may appear well, though most are "sick" with fever and significant bony tenderness, which, in the early stages of disease, may not be accompanied by swelling, warmth, or erythema.
• Clues to osteomyelitis as the primary pathology include tenderness beyond the joint line.
• The child will be reluctant to move the affected limb.
• Sympathetic arthritis refers to inflammation in a joint as a reaction to osteomyelitis in the surrounding bone.

1

• Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) or a bone scan will yield changes much earlier than X-rays. Bone biopsy is ideal prior to starting parenteral antibiotics.

# • Viral arthritis

• The child has a single swollen joint without trauma, often during a viral or postviral infection.
• The child is usually well but may appear sick.
• Physical examination may reveal features of associated viral illness: rash, coryza, etc.
• The virus is almost never isolated from the joint, and such testing is usually unnecessary.
• Symptoms and signs of arthritis usually last 1–2 weeks, and should resolve within 6 weeks; otherwise, another etiology (e.g., JIA) should be considered.

# • Soft tissue infection: cellulitis, pyomyositis, necrotizing fasciitis

• Soft tissue infections in the limbs often cause fever, together with clear signs of swelling, tenderness, erythema, warmth, and can mimic arthritis when located near a joint, especially when a cut or insect bite (common portal of entry for offending bacteria) is not obvious.
• Pain and tenderness are often severe.
• Draining lymph nodes may be enlarged.

# • Myositis or myalgia

• Both may be clinically indistinguishable, though localized tenderness (usually to the calves) is seen more often with myositis.
• Only myositis causes demonstrable elevation of serum muscle enzymes, e.g., creatinine kinase (CK), aspartate transferase (AST), lactate dehydrogenase (LDH).
• Myositis is most often caused by viral illnesses, especially parainfluenza.
• Myositis may occur after intense and prolonged physical exertion.
• Myalgia is usually generalized and is a nonspecific finding of many viral illnesses, can follow trauma or physical exertion, and also features predominantly with certain bacterial infections, e.g., leptospirosis.

# • Reactive arthritis

• The affected child is generally well-appearing, with mild to moderate pain in the affected joint.
• The signs of arthritis are usually mild. When arthritis is in the hip, all that may be evident on examination is painful limitation to movement.
• This is a post infectious phenomenon following viral or bacterial illnesses without actual pathogens invading the joint. Common antecedent infectious agents are Streptococcus, Salmonella, Shigella, and Yersinia.
• “Toxic or transient synovitis” refers to a reactive arthritis in the hip, usually in boys, aged 6–10 years, following a respiratory infection.
• Post vaccination reactive arthritis has been most commonly described following MMR vaccination.

• When the symptoms and signs persist beyond 6 weeks, consider JIA.

# • Rheumatic fever

• This is more common in developing countries, and in children aged 5–15 years.
• Fever is usually constant.
• Associated arthritis is fleeting and flitting, usually affects large joints, and is typically exquisitely sensitive to aspirin.
• Jones criteria outline the associated manifestations.

# • JIA; subgroup: oligoarticular, initial presentation of polyarticular; enthesitis-related or psoriatic

• This diagnosis is made when there is inflammation of one or more joints for at least 6 weeks with other conditions excluded.
• Children usually have had JIA for weeks or months before parents become aware of it.
• Signs of chronicity (muscle wasting next to affected joint, longer leg where knee arthritis has been present for several months) are clues that the problem has been going on for a longer time.
• Pain may be associated with early morning stiffness, and should never wake the child from sleep.
• Family history of arthritis is usually absent.
• There is no definitive diagnostic test for JIA.
• Checking for rheumatoid factor, antinuclear antibody (ANA), and HLA B27 is useful only after the diagnosis of JIA has been made in order to subgroup the JIA.

# • JIA; subgroup: systemic

• Fever is usually high spiking at least once a day, returning to baseline in between.
• A salmon pink maculopapular rash may only be seen during fever spikes.
• Other features include hepatosplenomegaly and lymphadenopathy.
• Serositis may manifest as chest or abdominal pain.
• Joint manifestations may occur later in the course of disease and will be associated with physical findings of arthritis.

# • Henoch–Schönlein purpura (HSP)

• Most children have a low-grade fever and malaise.
• Child is often quite well, though may appear sick because of the degree of joint or abdominal pain.
• Usually the large joints like the knee and ankle are affected. Inflammation is periarticular and can cause significant pain.
• Joint symptoms may precede the HSP rash by a few days.
• The rash of HSP should be present (palpable purpura) for the diagnosis, and may be associated with angioedema.
• Ask for abdominal pain that suggests gut involvement. Check the urine for hematuria and proteinuria.

1

# • Serum sickness

- The affected child is usually quite miserable.
- Other findings include rash (urticarial, erythematous, occasionally target lesions).
- This is often drug-induced, but may be following a nonspecific infection as well.

# • Leukemia

- The affected child often looks miserable.
- Joint pain is often severe, affecting sleep, but with minimal signs of arthritis.
- When there is swelling, it may be periarticular or periosteal.
- There may be accompanying signs of hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy, bruising or petechiae, or pallor.
- A normal full blood count or peripheral blood film does not exclude leukemia.
- A bone marrow aspirate must be performed if leukemia is suspected.
- Other childhood malignancies that may invade the bone and bone marrow to cause pain include lymphoma and neuroblastoma.

# • Bone tumor, synovial tumor

- Nocturnal pain should be a red flag for malignancy.
- Initial presentation is often temporally related to mild trauma, e.g., when landing on the feet during a basketball game.
- The two most common malignant bone tumors are osteosarcoma and Ewing sarcoma. Synovial sarcoma is less common.
- Osteosarcomas occur most frequently in teenagers and young adults and affect the metaphysis, with the commonest location in the knee joint.
- Ewing sarcoma occurs mainly in the diaphysis.
- If pain is persistent, X-rays should be done and a referral made to an oncologist.
- Plain X-rays done early in the course of the tumors are often negative, or the changes are difficult to discern. So a high index of suspicion is necessary, including MRI scans. Comparing the affected limb with the opposite limb may help to detect subtle abnormalities.

# • Synovial hemangioma, pigmented villonodular synovitis (PVNS)

- These are both rare.
- Hemangiomas may present with painful joint swelling after minor trauma. PVNS may present with recurrent painful boggy swellings.
- Diagnosis should be suspected if synovial aspirate is dark brown (blood-stained) without a history of trauma or suggestion of coagulopathy.
- MRI is necessary for diagnosis, and these conditions are best managed by an orthopedic surgeon.

# • Growing pains ("benign limb pain of childhood")

- Classically deep aching or crampy pain in the shins, calves, or thighs in children, usually those aged 3–13 years.

- Pain usually occurs in the evening or at night and can wake the child up from sleep. However, the child is completely normal by the morning.
- Physical examination is completely normal.

# • Fibromyalgia syndrome

- Usually adolescent females.
- Diffuse musculoskeletal aches; may have subjective feeling of joint swelling.
- Often associated with sleep disturbances, headaches, irritable bowel syndrome.
- Normal physical examination apart from multiple tender points: occiput (suboccipital muscle insertion), trapezius muscles (upper border and midpoint of the muscles), neck (intertransverse spaces C5–C7), supraspinatus muscles (at the origin of the muscles, above the spine of the scapula), parasternal region (second costochondral junction), distal humerus (2 cm distal to the lateral epicondyles), lower back (upper outer quadrant of the buttocks), femoral greater trochanter (posterior prominence), knees (medial fat pad).

# • Reflex sympathetic dystrophy (RSD)

- This is a localized pain syndrome and additional signs of dysautonomia: skin of the affected limb has episodic cyanotic discoloration with a purple mottled appearance, may feel cool with slower capillary refill time.
- Swelling is common.
- Patient may seem indifferent to the problem even as he/she complains of pain ("la belle indifference").
- Patient is usually a teenage girl, with a history of minor trauma.
- When symptoms last for months, chronic features may be seen: skin and muscle atrophy, pigmentation, hyperhidrosis.

# • Psychogenic pain

- This is one form of somatization disorder.
- Joint or limb pain is often vague, but associated with significant functional impairment, including missed school and poor sleep.
- Usually no objective evidence of inflammation on examination or any investigation.
- More often than not, the patient has significant school or family stressors.

# • Chronic fatigue syndrome

- Generalized bone and joint pains often accompany children with chronic fatigue syndrome.
- Clues to this diagnosis include the presence of symptoms affecting multiple systems, including headaches, abdominal pain, and diarrhea.
- Missed school often accompanies this diagnosis as well.
- The physical examination is normal.

1

# • Hemarthrosis, coagulopathy

• By far, the commonest cause of hemarthrosis associated with bleeding diathesis is hemophilia. Other types of bleeding diatheses (e.g., thrombocytopenia) almost never cause hemarthrosis.
• The first presentation of hemophilia may be a swollen joint, usually present after minimal or no trauma.
• Hemarthroses are usually very painful as blood irritates the joint lining, and may be associated with signs of inflammation. Patients may report an unusual sensation in the joint even before the feel the pain.
• Treatment should be given as soon as possible to speed the recovery and prevent chronic arthropathy.

### CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

- It is often useful to consider ruling in/out the most common and most sinister causes of joint or limb pain when dealing with a child with limb or joint pain.
- When a child presents with a painful limb or joint(s), the important questions that need to be answered are

- Is it really pain?
- Is one joint/limb affected, or many joints/limbs are affected? Is it large or small joint?
- What is the nature of the pain?
- What other symptoms are there?
- Is the child well, or "sick?" By "sick" one means systemically unwell.

### 1. History Taking

- Is it really pain?

- Verify with the patient/parent what they mean by "pain" (see symptoms above).

- Is one joint/limb affected or many joints/limbs affected?

- Verify with the patient/parent where is the pain? How many sites?
- Is it mainly the joint? Or does it involve the limb predominantly without affecting the joints much?
- If more than one limb or joint is affected, is it simultaneously or over a course of time?
- Have a quick glance at all the joints and limbs to see which joint/limb are obviously abnormal/affected.

- If the disease is obvious, you may easily see that more than one joint is involved.
- If this is not obvious, take a quick visual surveillance at the patient early in the encounter to see how many joints are involved before going through the full history and examination. This allows you to ask the right questions and think about the correct differential diagnoses.

1

# • What is the nature of the pain?

○ How long has the pain been there? Is it acute pain or chronic pain?
○ What is the pattern of the pain?

■ Episodic or persistent?
■ Worsening? Waxing and waning? Getting better?
■ Pain that wakes the child from sleep (nocturnal pain) is significant, usually for malignancy.

○ How severe is the pain?

○ Is the pain present on movement, on palpation, and/or at rest?
○ Determine if there is any aggravating factor, e.g., movement (usually implies a mechanical reason for the pain), rest (usually implies an inflammatory cause).
○ Determine if there are relieving factors, e.g., rest, assuming a certain position, medication.
○ Determine if there was a preceding event:

■ Trauma (fracture, dislocation, soft tissue injury, injuries). Often, even if the trauma is mild and unrelated to the underlying etiology, the parent may still volunteer this information. The examining doctor must decide – based on the history and exam – whether the history of trauma is related or not
■ Infection (cellulitis, soft tissue infection, septic arthritis, viral arthritis, reactive arthritis, osteomyelitis)
■ Inactivity
■ Increased activity preceding the pain
■ Any other trigger

# • Is the child well, or "sick?" By "sick" one means systemically unwell.

○ A "sick" child in the context of bone or joint pain refers to a child with

■ A high or prolonged fever
■ Severe pain that significantly affects function or wakes the child at night

# • Is there early morning stiffness?

○ Rarely, the child or caregiver may volunteer "early morning stiffness" (significant if lasting more than 20 min) or the need to move the joint repeatedly before achieving fully mobility. This is seen with chronic inflammatory arthritides.

# • What other symptoms are there?

# • Do a systems review

○ Acute fever suggests a joint, bone, or soft tissue infection; may also be present in SLE, inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), systemic JIA, leukemia, and other malignancies; fever with other infectious symptoms 1–2 weeks prior is consistent with reactive arthritis.
○ Photosensitive rash, mouth ulcers, lethargy, and fever may suggest SLE.

• Ask about growth history, developmental history, travel history, immunization history, family history.

## 2. Physical Examination

- General inspection

  - Does the child look "sick?," i.e. high fever, "toxic" and/or unwell, lethargic, in severe pain?

- Examine all the joints and limbs.

  - Determine the sites that are affected/painful.

    - Which limb(s)?
    - Are the joint(s) involved?

  - Determine if there is swelling, warmth, tenderness, crepitus, and/or joint effusion.

    - Swelling

      - Fluid (transudate, exudate, blood)
      - Synovium (thickening, tumor)
      - Bone (callus, tumor, malignancy)
      - Soft tissue (infection, inflammation, injury)

    - Warmth: infection, inflammation
    - Tenderness: infection, inflammation, injuries
    - Crepitus: joint degeneration, facture, soft tissue gas
    - Joint effusion: transudate, exudate, blood

- Look for evidence of chronic joint or limb pain.

  - Limb length discrepancy
  - Muscle wasting of affected limb
  - Contracture or flexion deformity of affected joint

- Do a head-to-toe examination including vital signs, growth, development.
