3. Algorithm

Continued from previous page

![img-30.jpeg](img-30.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Severe pain that significantly affects function or wakes the child up at night
- High fever
- Prolonged fever
- Sick looking
- "Toxic" and/or unwell
- Lethargic
- Hemodynamic instability

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 14-year-old boy presents with 3 days of left knee and ankle pain. He first felt the pain in his left knee at home as he was walking from his room to the bathroom. Thereafter, the pain was felt around his left ankle. The pain was worse on walking and better at rest. He is still able to walk but with a limp. In the last few days, the pain has not increased, neither is it better with ibuprofen.

One day before the joint pain, he developed a fever associated with rhinorrhea and one episode of diarrhea. He had already seen a doctor on the first day of knee pain where an X-ray of the knee was done and it was normal.

Specifically, he has no myalgia, or back or other joint pain.

On examination, he is well-appearing but febrile. His temperature is 39.2°C, heart rate 102/min, respiratory rate 16/min, blood pressure 112/75 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 100%. His height is on the 25th percentile. His weight is on the 50th percentile. He is not pale or jaundiced.

Musculoskeletal examination reveals no limb-length discrepancy or muscle wasting. There is a small left knee effusion. The left knee is slightly warm but without erythema, tenderness, or limitation to the range of motion. There is significant tenderness over the left lateral malleolus. The boy yells when you press around the lateral malleolus. There is no swelling, erythema, or warmth. There is no tenderness along the ankle joint line. It is difficult to test the range of motion of that ankle because of pain over the lateral malleolus.

His throat, nose, and ears are normal. His neck is supple. Heart rate is regular. On auscultation, S1 S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds. Auscultation of the lungs reveals vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft and nontender without hepatosplenomegaly or other masses. He has no rashes. Neurologic examination is normal.

# Self-Reflection Questions

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Painful joint

- What is your diagnosis?

- Left fibula osteomyelitis with sympathetic left knee arthritis

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Exquisite bony tenderness over left distal fibula (lateral malleolus) is the most important diagnostic physical finding.
- Acute onset of symptoms with associated high fever
- Absence of a "hot" joint in spite of the knee effusion, i.e., no warmth and no significant tenderness that limits range of motion that would be expected in a septic arthritis.
- No physical signs to suggest chronicity, i.e., good health previously, good growth and development, and no limb length discrepancy or muscle wasting.
- Preceding rhinorrhea and diarrhea happen too soon prior to symptoms so not a reactive arthritis/transient synovitis. (Expect the antecedent illness to be at least 1 week prior.)
- Absence of trauma.
- Age of boy does not suggest a congenital coagulopathy.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Absence of swelling, warmth, or erythema at the fibula may lull the examiner into thinking less about infection, as inflammation in deep-seated structures like the bones may be hard to detect early on. The only clue may be pain.
- However, for a superficial bone like the fibula, one may expect these physical findings fairly early.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Swelling, warmth, or erythema around the affected part of the fibula.

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Viral arthritis
- Systemic JIA

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Viral arthritis, Systemic JIA: Acute onset fever and joint pain

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Viral arthritis

- Degree of pain is more than expected for viral arthritis.

■ Physical findings in the ankle are localized and more suggestive of bony pathology than joint pathology. If it was a joint inflammation, we expect joint line tenderness, or more diffuse ankle swelling.

○ Systemic JIA

■ Physical findings in the ankle are localized and more suggestive of bony pathology than joint pathology. If it was a joint inflammation, we expect joint line tenderness, or more diffuse ankle swelling.

■ Absence of other findings expected in systemic JIA: lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, evanescent rash.

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Left fibula osteomyelitis with sympathetic left knee arthritis

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ None at present, but if untreated for any longer, subperiosteal abscess, pyomyositis and chronic osteomyelitis may complicate acute osteomyelitis.

• What is your plan for this patient?

○ Blood culture, ultrasound, or MRI to look for associated complications.

○ Urgent referral to an orthopedic surgeon for surgical drainage to send for Gram stain, culture, and sensitivities before starting antibiotics.

○ Initial antibiotics should cover for the most likely organism, which would be Staphylococcus or Streptococcus. In proven cases of osteomyelitis, antibiotics should be given for at least 4–6 weeks.

# Pallor

# DEFINITION OF PALLOR

- Pallor is defined as paleness of the skin or loss of skin color.
- Pallor may be due to

- Anemia
- Nonhematologic causes, such as,

- Peripheral vasoconstriction, e.g., shock, hypoglycemia, cold environment, hypothermia
- Decreased pigmentation, e.g., fair complexion, hypopigmentation

- This chapter will concentrate on pallor due to anemia and will not discuss the nonhematological causes.

# SYMPTOMS OF PALLOR

- Paleness of skin
- Paleness of mucous membranes such as conjunctiva, tongue, nail beds, hand creases
- Other symptoms associated with anemia, e.g., lethargy, decreased effort tolerance, poor feeding, shortness of breath, palpitations, dizziness, syncope

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

Anemia can be caused by these basic pathophysiologic mechanisms:

- Only involving red blood cells (RBCs) (single cell line)

- Blood loss
- Increased RBC destruction (hemolysis)

- Genetic causes
- Acquired causes

- Decreased RBC production

- Genetic causes
- Acquired causes

- Involving RBCs and at least one other cell line

- Bone marrow replacement
- Bone marrow failure

- Genetic causes
- Acquired causes

- Peripheral destruction

# 1. Involving Only RBCs

- Blood loss

- Acute blood loss does not result in anemia.

485

○ Anemia occurs when there is subsequent retention of fluid and/or inadequate marrow response to anemia.

○ Sites of potential blood loss are

- Gastrointestinal (GI) blood loss, e.g., gastritis, gastric/duodenal ulcer, esophageal varices, Meckel diverticulum, intestinal parasitic infection (hookworms), cow's milk protein allergy, inflammatory bowel disease
- Menstrual blood loss
- Physical trauma with internal and/or external bleeding
- Hematuria (rarely causes significant anemia)
- Other

○ Please note that the lack of obvious bleeding does not exclude the possibility of occult blood loss, e.g., GI bleed.

• Increased RBC destruction (hemolysis)

○ Genetic causes (intrinsic) causes

- Red cell enzyme deficiency – glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency, pyruvate kinase deficiency
- Membrane defect – hereditary spherocytosis, hereditary elliptocytosis
- Hemoglobinopathy – thalassemias, sickle cell anemia
- Congenital dyserythropoietic anemia (CDA)

○ Acquired (extrinsic) causes

- Autoimmune – primary autoimmune hemolytic anemia or secondary due to systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)
- Drugs, e.g., penicillins, methyldopa, cephalosporins, fludarabine
- Infections, e.g., mycoplasma, Epstein–Barr virus (EBV) malaria
- Transfusion, i.e., hemolytic transfusion reactions (due to alloantibodies)
- Microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (hemolytic uremic syndrome)
- Mechanical heart valves
- Hypersplenism

○ Manifestations of hemolysis

- Pallor
- Jaundice
- Dark-colored urine
- Red-colored urine if there is intravascular hemolysis (urine is red because of hemoglobinuria

• Decreased RBC production

○ Genetic causes

- Pure red cell aplasia – Diamond–Blackfan anemia

○ Acquired causes

- Nutritional – iron, vitamin B12, or folic acid deficiency
- Infections, e.g., parvovirus
- Drugs
- Lead poisoning
- Transient erythroblastopenia of childhood (TEC)

■ Chronic illness associated with impaired erythropoietin production – chronic kidney disease, chronic inflammation

# 2. Involving RBCs and At Least One Other Cell Line

■ Bone marrow replacement

○ Hematological malignancies (leukemia, lymphoma)
○ Metastatic malignancies (neuroblastoma)
○ Storage diseases

■ Bone marrow failure

○ Genetic causes (Fanconi anemia, dyskeratosis congenita)
○ Acquired causes (e.g., aplastic anemia – may be idiopathic, drugs, infection-associated)

■ Peripheral destruction

○ Autoimmune causes (e.g., SLE)
○ Severe hypersplenism due to portal hypertension

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Initial Impression

■ Initial impression

○ Assess appearance
○ Assess work of breathing
○ Assess circulation to skin (color)

■ The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

○ If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

■ Check pulse

□ Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
□ Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

■ When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

○ If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

■ Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
■ The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
■ If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
■ If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

1

### 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- History taking

- What does the patient/parent mean by pallor?
- Is the patient's pallor due to anemia?

- Patients who have decreased pigmentation have pale skin but have bright pink mucus membranes (subconjunctiva), tongue, nail beds, and palmar creases.
- Patients who have anemia have pale skin and pale mucus membranes (subconjunctiva), tongue, nail beds, and palmar creases.

- When was the onset?

- Iron deficiency is common in toddlers and menstruating adolescent girls.
- Early onset in infancy is suggestive of congenital cause.

- Was it sudden or gradual?
- Is this the first episode or is it recurrent?
- Assess the severity of the anemia and its impact.

- Lethargy, decreased effort tolerance, shortness of breath, palpitations, dizziness, syncope, poor feeding

- Is it only the RBCs? Or are other cell lines involved?

- Any symptom to suggest petechiae? Mucocutaneous bleeding?
- Any recurrent or serious infection, prolonged fever, or recurrent fever?

- If only red blood cell line is involved, look for the etiology and complications.

- Ask for the associated symptoms.
- Determine if there is jaundice or dark colored urine.
- Look for sites of potential blood loss.
- Take a detailed dietary history, including milk intake, time of weaning, intake of iron-rich foods (red meat, liver, green leafy vegetables), intake of foods rich in folate and vitamin B12.
- Ask about supplementation with iron, folate, vitamin B12.
- Ask about antenatal history, perinatal history, newborn screening, ethnicity, growth, development.
- Ask about fever, drug history, transfusion history, travel history.
- Take a family history.
- Do a systemic review.
- There are many possible symptoms. The list below covers the interpretation of the more common ones.
- Jaundice with or without dark urine:

- Jaundice suggests hemolytic anemia. Note that mild hemolysis may give rise to a lower level of hyperbilirubinemia, which is not obvious on physical examination.
- Dark urine may be due to bilirubin.
- Red urine may be due to hematuria or hemoglobinuria.

- Prematurity – preterm infants are more prone to anemia.

- Age at onset – iron deficiency is common in toddlers and menstruating adolescent girls; early onset in infancy is suggestive of congenital cause.
- Gender – G6PD deficiency more common in males.
- G6PD status – Was it ever tested? What was the result?
- Ethnic groups:

  - β-Thalassemia mutations are common in patients of southern Chinese, Malay, Indian, Indonesian, Thai, and Mediterranean descents.
  - α-Thalassemia mutations are common in the Chinese.
  - HbE mutations are common in Southeast Asians, e.g., Malay, Thai, or Indonesian descent.
  - Sickle cell anemia is seen in patients of African descent.

- Blood loss (menstrual, GI, kidneys, external bleeding)
- Abnormal stool color (red, dark red, black)
- Cow's milk intake is associated with increased risk of cow's milk allergy, iron loss through gut inflammation.
- White meat-based diet, vegetarian diet are low in iron.
- Vegan diet is associated with iron and vitamin B12 deficiencies.
- Symptom of pica (seen in iron deficiency)
- Malabsorption symptoms (at risk for iron, vitamin B12 deficiencies)
- Chronic illness (renal disease, autoimmune disease, etc.) (at risk for iron deficiency, vitamin B12 deficiency, decreased marrow production)
- Recent infection (EBV, mycoplasma, malaria, parvovirus, TEC) (decreased marrow production, hemolysis)
- Exposure to fava beans, naphthalene, moth balls (causes of hemolysis in G6PD-deficient patients)
- Exposure to lead (paint, toys) (decreased marrow production)
- Exposure to drugs or alternative medicine or supplements or intravenous transfusion (hemolysis, decreased marrow production)
- Developmental delay is associated with iron/vitamin B12/folate deficiency, Fanconi anemia.
- Failure to thrive – iron/vitamin B12/folate deficiency can affect growth; Fanconi anemia associated with short stature and slower growth.
- Travel history to malaria prone areas; with/without malaria prophylaxis.
- Positive family history:

  - Consanguinity (more likely to give rise to autosomal recessive diseases)
  - Family history of thalassemia and other anemia
  - Family history of jaundice, gallstones, splenectomy – suggestive of congenital hemolytic anemia, e.g., hereditary spherocytosis (autosomal dominant)

- If more than the red blood cell line is involved, look for the etiology and complication.

- Ask for the associated symptoms.

1

- Take a more detailed history about mucocutaneous bleeding (see Chapter 14).

- Take a more detailed history about the fevers (see Chapter 29).

- Ask about loss of appetite, loss of weight.

- Ask about bone pain.

- Ask about lumps and bumps.

- Ask about exposure to drugs, infections.

- Ask about growth, development.

- Ask about congenital malformations.

- Ask about family history.

- Do a systemic review.

- There are many possible symptoms. The list below covers the interpretation of the more common ones.

- Loss of appetite, loss of weight – suggestive of malignancy, autoimmune cause

- Bone pain, lumps, and bumps – suggestive of malignancy, autoimmune cause

- Joint pain, joint swelling – suggestive of autoimmune cause, possible malignancy

- Exposure to drugs, infection (post-hepatitis)-acquired bone marrow failure

- Developmental delay, failure to thrive, short stature, congenital malformations – congenital bone marrow failure syndromes (Fanconi anemia, dyskeratosis congenita), congenital bone marrow replacement (storage conditions) may be associated with these features.

- Positive family history

- Consanguinity (more likely to give rise to autosomal recessive diseases)

- Similarly affected individual (more likely genetic)

- Physical examination

- A head-to-toe examination is needed.

- The following signs may help you determine the etiology and complication:

- Hemodynamic instability can be due to hypovolemia (bleeding), cardiac (severe anemia causing high output cardiac failure), distributive (sepsis causing shock).

- Altered conscious state – shock, bleed in the brain, hydrocephalus, space occupying lesions,

- Short stature/failure to thrive can be due to chronic anemia, chronic disease, Fanconi anemia, and other syndromes.

- Any dysmorphism, limb/thumb deformity – Fanconi anemia, Diamond–Blackfan anemia, other genetic causes

- Thalassemic facies – frontal bossing, maxillary prominence, dental malocclusion

- Jaundice – suggestive of hemolysis

- **Pallor** – can be due to poor peripheral perfusion, shock, anemia, decreased pigmentation.
- **Degree of anemia** – conjunctival pallor (Hb < 10 g/dL), palmar crease pallor (Hb < 7 g/dL), and eyeball bruit (Hb < 5 g/dL)
- **Glossitis, angular stomatitis** – vitamin B12 deficiency, iron deficiency
- **Koilonychia** – iron deficiency
- **Evidence of internal or external bleeding**
- **Bruises/petechiae (thrombocytopenia)** – bone marrow replacement, bone marrow failure syndrome, autoimmune cause, e.g., SLE
- **Other skin and musculoskeletal signs** – features of SLE, dyskeratosis congenita; any lump suggestive of metastatic malignancy, e.g., neuroblastoma
- **Hepatomegaly only** – heart failure, infections (EBV, malaria), autoimmune (SLE)
- **Hepatosplenomegaly** – hematological malignancy, hemolytic anemia (thalassemia, hereditary spherocytosis), infections (EBV, malaria), autoimmune (SLE)
- **Other intra-abdominal mass** – neuroblastoma
- **Lymphadenopathy** – hematological malignancy, EBV infection, SLE
- **Cardiovascular**

- Raised jugular venous pressure (heart failure)
- Systolic flow (hemic) murmur (Hb < 8 g/dL)
- Mechanical valve in situ

- **Lungs** – crepitations may be seen in heart failure, lung infection

- **Other signs of infection** – may be the cause of the pallor or the consequence of neutropenia

1

# 4. Algorithm

![img-31.jpeg](img-31.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Hemodynamic instability (tachycarcia, hypotension)
- Severe anemia
- Symptoms and signs of heart failure
- Symptoms and signs of malignancy (prolonged fever, bone pain, bleeding, bruises, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy)
- Symptoms suggestive of bleeding
- Symptoms suggestive of SLE

1

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 5-month-old Indian baby boy is brought to the clinic for the main complaint of poor feeding. He is the first child of nonconsanguineous parents and was born at full term with a birth weight of 3.5 kg. His G6PD screen done at birth was normal. He had mild neonatal jaundice at day 5 of life, not requiring phototherapy. He is fully breastfed and has been putting on weight well till the past one month. Mother notes that he is taking longer to feed and often needs to rest and appears breathless while feeding. He is also less active and appears pale. He has no fever and no rash.

On examination, he is alert and comfortable at rest. His pulse rate is 170/min and blood pressure 80/55 mmHg. Respiratory rate is 48/min. He is afebrile. His weight is 5 kg. He has conjunctival and palmar crease pallor. There is no obvious jaundice. He is not dysmorphic and has no limb deformities. Cardiovascular examination reveals that apex beat is not displaced, but there is a soft systolic murmur heard. Auscultation of his lungs reveals normal breath sounds bilaterally with no crepitations or wheeze. His liver is palpable 3 cm below the right costal margin and soft in consistency. His spleen is just palpable. There is no lymphadenopathy. He has no bruises.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Pallor due to anemia

- What is your diagnosis?

- Thalassemia major
- Symptomatic of severe anemia

1

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Onset of anemia at 5 months of age. This is when the production of fetal hemoglobin decreases and in β-thalassemia major, the baby does not produce the β globin chains needed for HbA.
- Presence of hepatosplenomegaly. This is due to extramedullary hematopoiesis, which is seen in thalassemia major.
- Indian descent – β-thalassemia mutations are seen in the Indian population.
- Symptomatic of severe anemia – poor feeding, weight may be a bit low for his age.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- None

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- Growth parameters should have been plotted
- No information about development
- No information provided on family history of thalassemia or anemia
- No information about whether parents are consanguineous
- No history provided on whether the mom had antenatal follow-up and if she was flagged to have anemia or thalassemia minor
- No information about maternal diet; vegetarian diet is common amongst the Indian race No information about whether there is evidence of blood loss or bleeding
- No information about whether the baby was given iron supplements (American Academy of Pediatrics recommends iron supplementation by 4 months of age in totally breastfed child)
- No information about whether there was any malabsorption symptom
- No information about any preceding infection (EBV, mycoplasma, malaria, parvovirus, TEC)
- No information about the exposure to drugs or alternative medicine

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

- Iron-deficiency anemia

- Acute leukemia

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Iron-deficiency anemia

- Fully breastfed; iron deficiency can be seen in fully breastfed infants

- Not on iron supplements

- Acute leukemia

- Anemia

- Hepatosplenomegaly

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Iron-deficiency anemia

- Hepatosplenomegaly is not seen in iron deficiency.

- Acute leukemia

- Only one cell line involved (anemia).

- Leukemia at this age is rare and this child is otherwise well.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Iron deficiency

- There is no history regarding any possible blood loss, e.g., blood in stools, which can lead to iron deficiency. Occasionally, blood in stools can be due

to cow's milk protein allergy, which can lead to iron deficiency. Even though the baby is breastfed, the mother may be consuming cow's milk and the baby may still manifest cow's milk allergy symptoms.

- Iron studies will show an iron-deficiency picture with low ferritin, low iron, low iron saturation, high TIBC (total iron binding capacity)/transferrin.

○ Acute leukemia

- History of fever, bruises, or bleeding
- Lymphadenopathy
- Other cell line involved: neutropenia, thrombocytopenia
- Presence of blasts in peripheral blood film

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

○ Thalassemia major

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

○ Symptomatic anemia with poor feeding and poor growth (only gained 1.5 kg in 5 months)
○ Tachycardia, a potential sign of symptomatic anemia; no symptoms or signs of heart failure yet

- What is your plan for this patient?

○ Confirmatory investigations:

- Full blood count
- Peripheral blood film
- Hemoglobin electrophoresis
- Iron studies
- Blood for group and cross-match
- Blood for thalassemia genotyping if thalassemia major is confirmed by Hb electrophoresis

○ Review Hb. Most likely, he will need a transfusion of packed RBCs (leukocyte-reduced). Leukocyte reduction helps to decrease the risk of CMV (cytomegalovirus) transmission and leukocyte-related febrile transfusion reactions. It is also important to reduce HLA sensitization, which may be important if the child needs a hemopoetic stem cell transplant.

○ Refer to a hematologist.
○ Genetic counseling.

# Palpitations

Terence Lim Chee Wen • Denise L.M. Goh

# DEFINITION OF PALPITATIONS

Palpitations are defined as the sensation of forceful pulsation felt in the chest or lower neck, and are usually associated with an increase in force or frequency of the heartbeat. They may or may not be associated with irregularity in rhythm.

# SYMPTOMS OF PALPITATIONS

- Symptoms may include

- Sudden awareness of heart pulsation; most people, including children, are unaware of their heartbeats, so any sudden awareness of heart pulsation should be taken seriously.
- Prominent cardiac pulsations felt in the chest or lower neck.
- Sensation of fast heart beats (tachycardia).
- Sensation of skipped/missed beats.
- Preverbal children and young children are not usually able to describe the feeling of palpitations well.

- Palpitations may be associated with symptoms of low cardiac output such as

- Slight sensation of dyspnea
- Giddiness, near fainting, syncope, loss of consciousness
- Lethargy
- Decreased effort tolerance
- Chest pain not a frequent association with palpitation in the pediatric age group

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

- Pathophysiology

- Cardiac causes
- Extracardiac causes
- Physiologic responses

- See Table 45-1 for more details

496

TABLE 45-1

PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES OF PALPATIONS

|  Pathophysiological Causes | Clinical Manifestations | ECG Findings  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  **Cardiac Cause of Arrhythmia**  |   |   |
|  - Primary rhythm disorders (e.g., ectopic beats, non-physiologic supra-ventricular tachycardia such as re-entry or ectopic tachycardia) - Primary disorders of cardiac structure or function - Disorders of the pericardium such as pericarditis and pericardial effusions | - Primary rhythm disorders usually present as isolated palpitations, which abruptly stop and start. Physical examination is otherwise normal. - Palpitations as the sole presentation of primary disorders of cardiac structure or disorders of pericardium are unusual. More common is that they occur concurrently with other symptoms such as breathlessness or lethargy. Hence, ask for other associated symptoms. | - May be normal or abnormal - Abnormalities that may be present include the presence of pre-excitation (short PR interval with delta wave), atrial or ventricular ectopic beats, changes seen in pericarditis, changes seen in pericardial effusion  |
|  **Extracardiac Causes of Arrhythmia**  |   |   |
|  - Sympathetic overdrive: hyperthyroid states, certain drugs (e.g., salbutamol), caffeine, sympathomimetic poisoning, pheochromocytoma - Symptomatic severe anemia - Decreased intravascular volume states or other causes of shock - Significant electrolyte or acid-base anomalies | - History of weight loss, hyperanxiety, drug use, poisons, caffeine - Look for signs and symptoms of anemia (see Chapter 44) - Look for signs and symptoms of low blood pressure, dehydration (see Chapters 9 and 19) - A head-to-toe examination may show signs of the underlying cause | - Sinus tachycardia - ECG may show changes if there are significant changes in blood potassium or sodium level  |
|  **Physiologic Responses**  |   |   |
|  - Fever, anxiety, stress, fear, pain, exercise - Normal heart rate for age | - Usually, circumstances are obvious; no abrupt start or stopping. Physical examination is consistent with the trigger - Heart rate is appropriate for age, e.g., heart rate of 120 in a newborn | - Sinus tachycardia - Normal  |

1

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If unconscious and not breathing/gasping only:

- Check pulse

- Pulse absent: Commence cardiac compressions and assisted ventilation with bag valve mask and follow the pediatric cardiac arrest algorithm.
- Pulse present: Commence bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen to support breathing.

- When there is return of spontaneous circulation or when the breathing is adequately supported, perform primary assessment.

- If there are no signs of a potentially life-threatening situation, perform primary assessment.

# 2. Perform Primary Assessment

- Assess ABCDE (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure).
- The primary assessment evaluates the respiratory, circulatory, and neurologic function.
- If the assessment indicates need for immediate intervention, start appropriate treatment.
- If the assessment does not indicate need for immediate intervention, perform secondary assessment.

# 3. Perform Secondary Assessment

- History taking

- Is the patient still having the sensation of palpitations? If yes, then do an ECG immediately.
- What was the sensation?
- Nature of the start and stop of the sensation

- Sudden start and stop is suggestive of a primary arrhythmia.
- What was the patient doing before the start of the sensation?

- Ask for the associated symptoms.
- Ask for the age.
- Carry out a systems review, including but not limited to the following symptoms:

- Any symptom of low cardiac output symptoms (giddiness, near faint, syncope, loss of consciousness significant lethargy, decreased effort tolerance)?

- Any breathlessness?

- Any chest pain? Think of pericarditis.
- Any weight loss?
- Any seizure?
- Any risk factor for or symptom of dehydration?
- Any recent infection?
- Any ongoing fever?
- Any history of heart problems, cardiac surgery, or intervention?
- Any mood changes? Mental health issues?
- Growth and development.

o Drug history: medications, illicit drugs, poisons, caffeine intake

o Family history: arrhythmia, heart problems, sudden death, seizures thyroid issues, anemia

• Physical examination

o Do a full physical examination, especially a complete cardiovascular examination.

o Look especially for

- Growth parameters
- Fever
- Hydration status
- Anemia
- Thyroid eye signs
- Goiter
- Tremors
- Signs of heart failure (raised jugular venous pressure, hepatomegaly, pitting edema, tachypnea, crepitations in the lungs, apex beat)
- Rate and rhythm of the pulse rate
- Any heart murmur
- Any abdominal mass
- Mental state/mood

# 4. Role of 12 Lead ECG

• ECG during the palpitation episode itself is the most important investigation. Features to take note include rate, rhythm, axis, P waves, QRS complex, ectopic beats (ventricular, atrial, or junctional), and features of pre-excitation (delta wave). In particular, note the P to QRS relationship; if P waves are not clearly seen, re-entry type supraventricular tachycardia must be considered.

• For resting ECGs done when the patient is asymptomatic, look in particular for the presence of any delta-wave.

1

![img-32.jpeg](img-32.jpeg)

5. Algorithm

![img-33.jpeg](img-33.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Palpitations associated with giddiness, near faint, syncope, loss of consciousness, lethargy, decreased effort tolerance, chest pain or breathlessness
- Abnormal physical findings particularly on auscultation, pulses, or systemic perfusion
- Abnormal ECG other than sinus tachycardia, e.g., supraventricular tachycardia, ventricular tachycardia, delta waves, Brugada ECG pattern, prolonged QT interval
- History of sudden death in family
- History of seizures in family
- History of cardiac surgery or intervention

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 12-year-old boy was brought to the emergency department complaining of palpitations, which were worse when lying flat. There was no significant past medical or family history of note.

He was well until a fortnight back when he started complaining of easily fatigue, poor exercise tolerance, and reduced appetite. This has gotten worse over the last week. He has breathlessness, chest discomfort, and palpitations, which were worse when lying flat. Parents note that he has lost 5 kg over 1 month. On examination, he was a well-thrived teenager but markedly pale. At rest, his respiratory rate was 31/min, heart rate 102/min, and blood pressure 121/82 mmHg. He was afebrile. Apex beat was not well felt. Poor air entry was noted bilaterally, and the heart sounds were poorly heard. The liver was 3 cm below the right costal margin. There was no pitting edema.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

o Palpitations associated with symptoms suggestive of low cardiac output

• What is your diagnosis?

o Pericardial and pleural effusion; underlying cause may be a malignancy, e.g., lymphoma

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

o Recent onset of easy fatigue, poor exercise tolerance, and reduced appetite.

o Progressive worsening of symptoms.

o Breathlessness, chest discomfort, and palpitations, which were worse when lying flat.

o Apex beat was not well felt.

o Poor air entry was noted bilaterally and heart sounds were also poorly heard.

o The liver was 3 cm below the right costal margin.

o Loss of weight, pale.

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis

o None

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

o No mention of what the patient meant by palpitations

o No mention of whether the pulse was regular or irregular and what the pulse volume was like

o No mention of jugular venous pressure

o No mention of whether he has had enlarged lymph nodes or splenomegaly or petechiae

o No ECG provided/done

o No mention of vaccination status especially to BCG; no mention of exposure to tuberculosis

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

o Myocarditis

o Tuberculosis (TB) pericarditis

• For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

o Myocarditis: Recent onset of easy fatigue, poor exercise tolerance, and reduced appetite. Progressive worsening of symptoms. Breathlessness, chest discomfort, and palpitations, which were worse when lying flat. Has evidence of low cardiac output, apex beat not well felt, liver is enlarged.

o TB pericarditis: Recent onset of easy fatigue, poor exercise tolerance, and reduced appetite. Progressive worsening of symptoms. Breathlessness, chest discomfort, and palpitations, which were worse when lying flat. Has evidence of low cardiac output, apex beat not well felt, liver is enlarged, can have large pleural effusions; may be pale.

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o Myocarditis: not usually anemic/pale, usually does not have such large pleural effusion.

o TB pericarditis: rare.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- Information about recent infection prior to onset of symptoms
- Information of BCG vaccination, TB exposure

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

- Pericardial and pleural effusion; underlying cause may be a malignancy, e.g., lymphoma

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

- Palpitations associated with lethargy, decreased effort tolerance, chest pain, and breathlessness
- Abnormal physical findings

- What is your plan for this patient?

- Admit to high dependency or ICU
- Urgent referral to a cardiologist
- Needs CXR, ECG, and echo
- Full blood count and peripheral blood film
- May need a CT scan of the thorax

# Rash with Fever

Chan Poh Chong • Denise L.M. Goh • Hugo P.S. Van Bever

# DEFINITION OF RASH WITH FEVER

- "Rash with fever" is defined as a rash that occurs before, concomitant with, or after a fever.

# SYMPTOMS OF RASH WITH FEVER

- Fever is frequently defined as temperature >37.5°C (see Chapter 28 for more information about definition of fever).
- The most common complaints suggestive of a rash are

- Change in color, e.g., blue, red, white, brown, black, purple, green
- Lesion(s) that is not palpable

- Macule – flat smooth area of color change less than 1.5 cm in diameter
- Patch – flat smooth area of color change larger than 1.5 cm in diameter
- Petechiae – pinpoint areas of nonblanchable erythema due to leakage of blood into the skin from capillaries
- Ecchymosis – larger nonblanchable macules due to leakage of blood into the skin; commonly known as "bruise"

- Lesion(s) that is palpable

- Papule – small raised lesion less than 0.5 cm in diameter
- Nodule – large raised lesion more than 0.5 cm in diameter
- Plaque – broad, raised lesion (broader than high)
- Vesicle – small fluid-filled blister less than 0.5 cm in diameter
- Bulla – large fluid-filled blister larger than 1 cm in diameter
- Pustule – vesicle filled with pus
- Wheal – elevated edematous papule or plaque
- Scale – accumulation of dried layers of squamous cells
- Palpable purpura – nonblanchable but palpable papules or plaques due to leakage of blood into the skin
- Lichenification – linear thickening of the skin as a consequence of scarring
- Excoriation – interruption of the epidermis, presenting as red-wet, sharp delimited lesions

- Please see the pictures below for examples of rashes.

- The rash may be associated with these symptoms:

- May be localized or generalized
- May be itchy or not itchy
- May be painful or not painful

505

○ May be blanching or not blanching
○ May be palpable or not palpable

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Virus

- Causes

- Human herpes virus 6
- Human parvovirus B19
- Varicella
- Enterovirus
- Epstein-Barr virus (EBV), cytomegalovirus (CMV)
- Dengue
- Measles
- Rubella
- Nonspecific viruses

- Manifestations

- Fever may precede or is concurrent with the rash.
- Rash may be maculopapular, vesicular, petechiae, etc.
- May have other features that are unique to that particular infection, e.g., mouth ulcers, hepato/splenomegaly, and lymphadenopathy.

## 2. Bacteria

- Causes

- Staphylococcus
- Streptococcus
- Meningococcus
- Treponema pallidum
- Borrelia burgdorferi
- Rickettsia rickettsii

- Manifestations

- Staphylococcus

- Cellulitis
- Impetigo
- Staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome (SSSS)
- Toxic shock syndrome (TSS)
- Necrotizing fasciitis

- Streptococcus

- Scarlet fever
- TSS
- Necrotizing fasciitis

- Meningococcus

- Septic shock with disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) and purpura

- Treponema pallidum

- Secondary syphilis (late teenage and above)

- Borrelia burgdorferi

- Lyme disease (tick-borne disease in endemic areas)

- Rickettsia rickettsii

- Rocky Mountain spotted fever (tick-borne disease in endemic areas)

## 3. Mycoplasma

- Causes

- Mycoplasma pneumoniae

- Manifestations

- Constitutional symptoms, nonspecific erythematous maculopapular eruptions, erythema multiforme, Stevens-Johnson syndrome

## 4. Immune-mediated

- Causes

- Drugs
- Viral or post-viral
- Autoimmune
- Idiopathic

- Manifestations: See Table 46-1

TABLE 46-1

MANIFESTATIONS OF IMMUNE-MEDIATED CAUSES

|  Immune-Mediated Causes | Manifestations  |
| --- | --- |
|  Drugs | Toxic epidermal necrolysis Drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS) syndrome Stevens-Johnson syndrome Serum sickness Erythema multiforme (EM) Erythema nodosum (EN)  |
|  Viral or post-viral | EM EN Urticaria  |
|  Autoimmune/inflammatory | Kawasaki disease Systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis Systemic lupus erythematosus Inflammatory bowel disease (most common rash of inflammatory bowel disease is EN)  |
|  Idiopathic | EM EN Urticaria  |

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History

- Determine the timing and occurrence of fever with rash.

- Duration of the fever.
- When was the rash first noted?

- Ask about the characteristic of the rash.

- What does it look like?
- Which part of the body is the rash located?
- Is it itchy?
- Is it painful?
- Does it blanch?
- Is it palpable?

- Ask about the progression of the rash.
- Is the child clinically well or unwell?
- Ask about associated symptoms, e.g., myalgia, arthralgia, upper respiratory tract signs
- Exposure and contact history
- Immunization history
- Travel history
- Drug history, alternative medicine/supplement intake
- Systemic review (including growth and development)
- Family history

## 2. Physical Examination

- Does the child look well or sick?
- If the child looks sick, check the vital signs.

  - Warning signs are high fever, tachycardia, tachypnea, hypotension, pallor, and cyanosis.
  - These may indicate the presence of sepsis, severe inflammation/autoimmune reaction.

- Note the location and distribution (localized or generalized).
- Note the symmetry/asymmetry, color of the rash.
- Assess the morphology of the rash; touch to determine if the rash is palpable.
- Look to see if there is any punctum, e.g., insect/tick bite marks.
- Look to see if there are signs of scratching.
- Look to see if there are signs of bacterial infection.
- Look at the scalp, oral mucosa, nails.
- Look at the genitalia and anus.
- Examine the rest of the body; look for signs of systemic illness and complications, e.g., lymphadenopathy, hepatomegaly and/or splenomegaly, dehydration.
- Look at the growth and development.

## 3. Algorithm

- Is this rash a medical emergency?

  - Are the vital signs stable?
  - Is the child toxic looking?
  - Decide on the need for admission and/or isolation.

- What is the cause of the rash and fever?

  - See Table 46-2 for the differential diagnosis.

TABLE 46-2

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF RASH ASSOCIATED WITH FEVER

|  Rash Morphology | Causes  |
| --- | --- |
|  Maculopapular/plaques | **Generalized** - Exanthem subitum, dengue fever, chikungunya, zika, EBV, CMV, measles, rubella, parvovirus infection, other viral exanthem - Scarlet fever - Mycoplasma - Drug reactions, drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS syndrome), Stevens-Johnson syndrome - Inflammatory/autoimmune conditions, e.g., Kawasaki disease, SJIA systemic lupus erythematosus - Secondary syphilis - *Borrelia burgdorferi* - *Rickettsia rickettsii***Localized** - Cellulitis - Necrotizing fasciitis - Erythema multiforme  |

TABLE 46-2

DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF RASH ASSOCIATED WITH FEVER—cont'd

|  Rash Morphology | Causes  |
| --- | --- |
|  Papular/nodular/pustular | - Erythema nodosum (it more commonly presents as tender nodules) - Folliculitis, impetigo - Insect bites - Abscess - Drug reaction  |
|  Vesicular/bullous | - Stevens–Johnson syndrome - Bullous impetigo - Varicella zoster infection - Injuries, e.g., burn/scalds  |
|  Peeling/desquamation | - Toxic epidermal necrolysis - Toxic shock syndrome - Staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome (SSSS) - Steven–Johnsons syndrome - Kawasaki disease (convalescent phase) - Postscarlet fever  |
|  Scales | - Atopic dermatitis that is infected - Dermatomyositis  |
|  Wheal | - Viral-induced urticarial - Serum sickness - Idiopathic  |
|  Petechiae/ecchymosis | **Platelet disorder** - **Destruction/consumption/sequestration of platelet**     - Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) or ITP (immune thrombocytopenic purpura)     - Infection: viral-induced thrombocytopenia (e.g., dengue fever), sepsis, meningococcemia, *Rickettsia rickettsii*     - Drugs, e.g., cephalosporins     - Disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC)     - Hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS)     - Thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP) - **Lack of platelet production**     - Bone marrow failure: aplastic anemia, Fanconi anemia, drugs, viral infections     - Bone marrow replacement: leukemia, lymphoma**Coagulopathy** - DIC  |
|  Palpable purpura | - Small vessel vasculitis, e.g., Henoch–Schönlein purpura - Other vasculitis  |

- Any complication or red flag?

- Poor feeding
- Dehydration
- Spreading bacterial infection
- Extensive desquamation and mucous membrane involvement

1

- Risk of or the presence of massive fluid loss from skin lesions
- Bleeding, petechiae, purpura
- Risk of bleeding

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Hemodynamic instability
- Toxic, septic looking
- Shock (distributive, hypovolemic)
- Poor feeding
- Dehydration
- Spreading bacterial infection
- Extensive desquamation and mucous membrane involvement
- Risk of or the presence of massive fluid loss from skin lesions
- Bleeding, petechiae, purpura
- Risk of bleeding

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A previously well 5-year-old boy presents to you for the problem of fever with chills, sore throat, and headache for 3 days. His fever was hitting 39–40°C. He was lethargic and occasionally irritable, with poor appetite. He also developed a rash on his trunk and limbs, which was not pruritic or painful.

He described his headache as pain “all over his head” of grade 6 out of 10, but there were no visual problems or vomiting. His sore throat was still bearable and he could still drink and eat soft foods. There was minimal cough but no rhinorrhea. There were also no other gastrointestinal or urinary symptoms on systemic review.

He was seen the day before at a general practitioner and was given paracetamol and amoxicillin. His mother had not started the antibiotics for him yet.

He is currently attending kindergarten, and there were always some other children in the center with fever, rash, and ulcers in the mouth, but there were no reported epidemics. His family members consisting of his parents and a younger sister are all well. They travelled to Malaysia 3 weeks ago to visit some relatives who were well.

He had all his vaccination updated. There was no recent immunization.

On examination, his temperature is 39.2°C, heart rate 120/min, respiratory rate 20/min, blood pressure 100/75 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 99%. He is lethargic but not toxic and is still able to respond appropriately.

His height and weight are on the 25th percentile. He is not pale, cyanosed, or jaundiced. There is no conjunctivitis, and pupils are equal and reactive. Fundoscopy is normal. His throat is noted to be slightly red with tonsillar enlargement bilaterally, but no exudates. There are some

petechiae on his hard palate. His tongue is red with some white coating over it. His nose and ears are normal. There are some mildly tender subcentimeter anterior cervical lymph nodes bilaterally. There is a generalized erythematous maculopapular rash on his trunk and all four limbs, which felt slightly rough. Heart rate is regular. On auscultation, S1 S2 are heard. There are no additional heart sounds. On auscultation of the lungs, there are vesicular breath sounds. The abdomen is soft and nontender. There is no hepatosplenomegaly. Bowel sounds are normal. His peripheries are warm. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds. No edema is noted in his eyes, feet, or hands. His genitalia are normal. Musculoskeletal examination is normal. Examination of his neurological system does not reveal any neck stiffness or other deficit.

A urine dipstick showed no abnormalities.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Fever with sore throat and rash exanthema – likely infection related

- What is your diagnosis?

- Scarlet fever

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Fever with sore throat in the absence of rhinorrhea or significant cough
- Truncal and limb exanthem with sandpapery feel
- Tender lymphadenopathy
- White strawberry tongue
- Acute illness (good health previously, good growth, normal development)
- No medications except paracetamol; mother did not administer amoxicillin

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

- Absence of tonsillar exudates

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

No peeling or desquamation of extremities
- No information about whether there is perioral pallor

- What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

Early Kawasaki disease
Infectious mononucleosis syndrome (IMS)

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

- Kawasaki disease – fever, irritability, strawberry tongue, rash, lymphadenopathy
IMS - fever, tonsillar enlargement, rash, lymphadenopathy

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

- Kawasaki disease – tonsillar enlargement; no conjunctivitis, erythema of palms and soles or swollen extremities
IMS - sandpapery truncal rash, no hepatosplenomegaly, no tonsillar exudates, no eyelid swelling

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

None

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

Scarlet fever

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

No red flags.
- But also no information provided on whether he is dehydrated.
Lethargic.
He is potentially at risk of dehydration, as he is not feeding/drinking well.

• What is your plan for this patient?

- Do a throat swab for streptococcus (Group A beta-hemolytic strep) rapid strep antigen test or culture.
Full blood count for high white count and neutrophilia.
- Start oral Penicillin V or amoxicillin.
- Ensure good oral rehydration.
- Symptomatic fever and pain relief.
- This boy should avoid close contact with other children until after he has received appropriate antimicrobial therapy for at least 24 hours. No school or child care attendance during this period.

# Rash without Fever

Elizabeth Tham • Denise L.M. Goh

• Hugo P.S. Van Bever

# DEFINITION OF RASH

- A rash is a general description of a change in the appearance of the skin. It is a very common pediatric complaint affecting children of all age groups.
- Rashes without fever in children have different etiologies from rashes that are associated with fever (see Chapter 46 for the latter).

# SYMPTOMS OF RASH

- The most common complaints suggestive of a rash are

  - Change in color, e.g., blue, red, white, brown, black, purple, green
  - Lesion(s) that is not palpable

    - Macule – flat smooth area of color change less than 1.5 cm in diameter
    - Patch – flat smooth area of color change larger than 1.5 cm in diameter
    - Petechiae – pinpoint areas of nonblanchable erythema due to leakage of blood into the skin from capillaries
    - Ecchymosis – larger nonblanchable macules due to leakage of blood into the skin; commonly known as “bruise”

  - Lesion(s) that is palpable

    - Papule – small raised lesion less than 0.5 cm in diameter
    - Nodule – large raised lesion more than 0.5 cm in diameter
    - Plaque – broad, raised lesion (broader than high)
    - Vesicle – small fluid-filled blister less than 0.5 cm in diameter
    - Bulla – large fluid-filled blister larger than 1 cm in diameter
    - Pustule – vesicle filled with pus
    - Wheal – elevated edematous papule or plaque
    - Scale – accumulation of dried layers of squamous cells
    - Palpable purpura – nonblanchable but palpable papules or plaques due to leakage of blood into the skin
    - Lichenification – linear thickening of the skin as a consequence of scarring
    - Excoriation – interruption of the epidermis, presenting as red-wet, sharp delimited lesions

  - Please see Chapter 46 for examples of rashes.

- The rash may be associated with these symptoms:

  - May be localized or generalized
  - May be itchy or not itchy
  - May be painful or not painful

517

- May be blanching or not blanching
- May be palpable or not palpable

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Rash that Is Congenital or Appeared during the Neonatal Period

- Causes of localized rash

- Physiological: acne neonatorum, erythema toxicum, transient neonatal pustular melanosis, postdate peeling, milia, Mongolian blue spot

- Infection:

- Bacterial: folliculitis, impetigo (Staphylococcus aureus)
- Fungal: candidiasis

- Inflammation: seborrheic dermatitis, diaper dermatitis, heat rash (also known as miliaria)
- Traumatic: sucking blister, birth injury, non-accidental injury
- Malformation/genetic: incontinentia pigmenti, Sturge-Weber syndrome, café au lait spots, hemangioma, nevus sebaceous, cutis aplasia

- Causes of generalized rash

- Physiological: erythema toxicum, transient neonatal pustular melanosis, post-term peeling, cutis marmorata
- Infection:

- Intrauterine infections, e.g., toxoplasmosis, rubella, cytomegalovirus, herpes simplex
- Viral: neonatal varicella, neonatal herpes simplex infection
- Bacterial: folliculitis, impetigo (S. aureus)

- Autoimmune: maternal immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP)
- Malformation/genetic: café au lait spots, epidermolysis bullosa, bleeding disorder, platelet disorder, connective tissue disorder, albinism
- Iatrogenic: phototherapy-related rash
- Malignancy: congenital leukemia

# 2. Rash that Appeared during Infancy, Childhood, or Adolescence

- Causes

- Infection:

- Viral: molluscum contagiosum, warts, viral exanthem
- Bacterial: folliculitis, (bullous) impetigo, cellulitis, toxic epidermal necrolysis
- Fungal: tinea capitis, tinea corporis, tinea vesicolor, candidiasis
- Parasitic: scabies

- Traumatic: accidental injury, non-accidental injury

- Inflammation: insect bites, diaper dermatitis, erythema nodosum, contact dermatitis, heat rash, sunburn, acne
- Autoimmune/inflammatory: Henoch–Schönlein purpura (HSP), systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), cutaneous lupus, dermatomyositis, morphea (localized scleroderma), Gianotti–Crosti syndrome, Kawasaki disease, vitiligo, idiopathic thrombocytopenic purpura, psoriasis, pityriasis rosea
- Allergy: atopic dermatitis, urticaria (due to viruses, food allergy, anaphylaxis), Stevens–Johnson syndrome, drug allergies
- Malformation/genetic: neurocutaneous syndromes, albinism, blood disorders, platelet disorders, connective tissue disorders
- Malignancy: leukemia, neuroblastoma
- Physiological: keratosis pilaris

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History Taking

- Ask about the characteristic of the rash

- What does it look like?
- Which part of the body is the rash located?
- Is it itchy?
- Is it painful?
- Does it blanch?
- Is it palpable?

- When was the rash first noted?

- Ask about the progression of the rash.

- Is the child clinically well or unwell?

- Ask about associated symptoms.

- Do a systemic review, including growth and development.

- Ask about family history.

# 2. Physical Examination

- Does the child look well or sick?
- If the child looks sick, check the vital signs.
- Note the location and distribution (localized or generalized).
- Note the symmetry/asymmetry, color of the rash.
- Assess the morphology of the rash; touch to determine if the rash is palpable.
- Look to see if there are signs of scratching.
- Look to see if there are signs of bacterial infection.
- Look at the scalp, oral mucosa, nails.
- Look at the genitalia and anus.
- Examine the rest of the body for signs of other systemic illness.
- Look at the growth and development.

# 3. Algorithm for Localized Rash that Was Present at Birth or Started during the Neonatal Period

|  Morphology and Distribution | Associated Symptoms | Cause  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  **Limbs**  |   |   |
|  Vesicle or bulla | This is a diagnosis by exclusion Baby is well Found on parts of the body that the baby can put into the mouth | Sucking blisters  |
|  Mottling, blanching, reticular pattern | Associated with a cold environment | Cutis marmorata  |
|  **Scalp**  |   |   |
|  Many plaques • Greasy • May have an erythematous base | May also be seen on the eyebrows, ears, neck, and in the diaper area | Seborrheic dermatitis  |
|  Single plaque • Well-circumscribed • Oval or linear • Waxy • Yellow or light brown in color | May be present at birth or develop soon after birth | Nevus sebaceous  |
|  Well-circumscribed area where there is a lack of skin | Usually single lesion but may have more than one | Cutis aplasia  |
|  **Face**  |   |   |
|  Papules and/or pustules • Associated with erythema • Usually found on the face (especially the cheeks) | Usually not born with it Develops it in the first few weeks of life Can sometimes also be found in the scalp | Acne neonatorum  |
|  Papules (usually 1–2 mm in diameter) • Whitish yellow in color • Usually found on the nose, cheeks, chin, and forehead |  | Milia  |
|  Plaques • Greasy • May have an erythematous base | May also be seen in the scalp, neck, and in the diaper area | Seborrheic dermatitis  |
|  Petechiae and/or purpura | Look for bleeding elsewhere on the body (see Chapter 14) | Birth injury Platelet disorder Coagulation defect Blood vessel disorder Accidental/non-accidental injury  |

|  Morphology and Distribution | Associated Symptoms | Cause  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Macule or patch (stork bite mark, salmon patch) • Blanchable • Not over the distribution of the fifth cranial nerve | May be single or multiple | Hemangioma  |
|  Macule or patch • May be blanchable • Found over the distribution of the fifth cranial nerve | May be associated with intracranial hemangioma May be associated with glaucoma | Hemangioma; may be only cutaneous or associated with Sturge–Weber syndrome  |
|  **Groin**  |   |   |
|  Papules and/or pustules and/or plaques • Erythema • Skin folds are usually spared | May not be well demarcated | Diaper dermatitis  |
|  Papules and/or pustules and/or plaques • Erythema • Can be found in the skin folds | Well-dermarcated Satellite nodules common | Candidiasis  |
|  Plaques • Greasy; may have an erythematous base | May also be seen in the scalp, eyebrows, ears, and neck area | Seborrheic dermatitis  |
|  **Any Part of the Body**  |   |   |
|  Papules, pustules, vesicles, and/or bullae • Erythema • More likely to be localized but can be generalized | Baby may look unwell with systemic signs of infection | Folliculitis Impetigo  |
|  Papules, pustules • Erythema • Usually found over the face and/or body | Usually spares the palms and soles and oral mucosal surfaces Baby is usually well | Erythema toxicum  |
|  Papules, pustules, macules • Usually pigmented • Usually not erythematous • Usually found over the face and/or body | May affect the palms and soles | Transient neonatal pustular melanosis  |
|  Vesicles and/or bullae | Evolve over time to wart-like lesions, then to hyperpigmented macules, then to hypopigmentation | Incontinentia pigmenti  |

|  Morphology and Distribution | Associated Symptoms | Cause  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Petechiae and/or purpura | See Chapter 14 (Bruising and Bleeding) | Birth injury Platelet disorder Coagulation defect Blood vessel disorder Accidental/non-accidental injury  |
|  Pink or red macule, patch or plaque | Blanchable | Hemangioma (stork bite mark, salmon patch)  |
|  Blue-colored macule/patch | Usually over the buttock area but can be found in any part of the body | Mongolian blue spots  |
|  Brown colored macule or patch | Note that most children with multiple café au lait spots (CALs) evolve over time to become neurofibromatosis (NF-1) NF-1 signs and symptoms come on with age (until 20 years of age) | Moles/nevi CALs  |
|  White/hypopigmented macule or patch |  | Albinism May be isolated or associated with other conditions such as tuberous sclerosis  |
|  Skin that is peeling | Can be localized or generalized Occur more often in postdate babies but can also occur in term babies | Physiological peeling  |

#### 4. Algorithm for a Generalized Rash that Was Present at Birth or Started During the Neonatal Period

|  Rash Morphology | Cause  |
| --- | --- |
|  Maculopapular | • Phototherapy rash • Congenital syphilis  |
|  Papular/pustular | • Erythema toxicum • Transient neonatal pustular melanosis • Folliculitis • Impetigo  |
|  Vesicular/bullous | • Epidermolysis bullosa • Neonatal varicella • Neonatal herpes simplex infection • Impetigo  |

|  Rash Morphology | Cause  |
| --- | --- |
|  Peeling/desquamation | • Post-term gestation  |
|  Scaly | • Seborrheic dermatitis  |
|  Petechiae/purpura | • Maternal immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP) • Intrauterine infections, e.g., congenital rubella syndrome, CMV, toxoplasmosis • Platelet disorder • Coagulation defect • Blood vessel disorder • Accidental/non-accidental injury • See Chapter 14  |
|  Pigmentary changes | • Café au lait spots (may be isolated or associated with NF-1) • Hypopigmented macules (may be isolated or associated with tuberous sclerosis) • Albinism  |
|  Mottling | • Cutis marmorata  |

### 5. Algorithm for Rashes that Appear During Infancy, Childhood, or Adolescence

|  Rash Morphology | Cause  |
| --- | --- |
|  Maculopapular/plaques | **Generalized** • Drug reactions • Autoimmune/inflammatory conditions, e.g., Kawasaki disease, systemic lupus erythematosus, dermatomyositis • Viral exanthem **Localized** • Cellulitis • Drug reactions • Heat rash (miliaria) • Dermatitis (contact, atopic, diaper) • Scabies • Insect bite • Nevus sebaceous  |
|  Papular/nodular/pustular | • Candidiasis • Folliculitis, impetigo • Molluscum contagiosum • Warts • Scabies • Insect bites • Acne keratosis pilaris • Gianotti–Crosti syndrome • Erythema nodosum • Leukemia, lymphoma, neuroblastoma  |
|  Vesicular/bullous | • Stevens–Johnson syndrome • Bullous impetigo • Injuries, e.g., burn/scalds • Drug reaction  |

Continued

|  Rash Morphology | Cause  |
| --- | --- |
|  Peeling/desquamation | - Toxic epidermal necrolysis - Sunburn - Kawasaki disease (convalescent phase) - Postscarlet fever  |
|  Scale | - Fungal infection (tinea capitis, corporis, vesicolor) - Seborrheic dermatitis - Atopic dermatitis - Psoriasis - Pityriasis rosea - Dermatomyositis  |
|  Wheal | - Anaphylaxis - Food allergy - Viral-induced urticaria - Physical/cold/vibratory/cholinergic - Insect bite - Idiopathic  |
|  Petechiae/purpura | - Platelet disorder - Coagulation defect - Blood vessel disorder (including vasculitides) - Accidental/non-accidental injury - See Chapter 14  |
|  Pigmentary changes | - Hyperpigmentation - Postinflammation - Moles/nevus - Acanthosis nigricans - Adrenal insufficiency - Drugs - Café au lait spots (may be isolated or associated with NF-1, etc.) - Hypopigmentation - Postinflammation - Vitiligo - Associated with tuberous sclerosis, vitiligo, incontinentia pigmenti, etc. - Idiopathic  |
|  Palpable purpura | - Small vessel vasculitis, e.g., HSP  |
|  Other | - Erythema, induration and shiny appearance of skin, tightening of skin suggests morphea (localized scleroderma) - Mottling due to peripheral vasoconstriction  |

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Hemodynamic instability
- Ill-looking
- Shock (distributive, hypovolemic)
- Poor feeding
- Dehydration
- Extensive desquamation and mucous membrane involvement
- Risk of or the presence of massive fluid loss from skin lesions
- Bleeding, petechiae, purpura
- Risk of bleeding
- Systemic symptoms, e.g., fever, weight loss, lethargy, joint pain/swelling, hemodynamic instability
- Suspicious of non-accidental injury (see Chapter 37)
- Other congenital anomalies, e.g., dysmorphic features, cardiac murmur, hepatosplenomegaly

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 2-year-old boy presents to the clinic with a generalized scaly pruritic rash, which had been present since early infancy.

His mother recalled that he had yellowish scaly rashes over his scalp in the first few months of life, which gradually resolved. This current generalized rash developed over his neck, cubital, and popliteal fossae, and trunk appeared during his 2nd month of life. He also had rashes over his perineal region, which did not involve the folds. The rashes waxed and waned with the application of many topical creams obtained from various physicians but had not fully resolved. He never had any fever and was growing and feeding appropriately. There was a strong family history of allergic rhinitis and asthma in both parents as well as his older sister, aged 6. His vaccinations were up to date and his developmental milestones were appropriate for his age.

On examination, his temperature is 36.5°C, heart rate 120/min, respiratory rate 25/min, blood pressure 85/50 mmHg, and oxygen saturation on room air 98%. His height, weight, and occipital-frontal circumference are on the 50th percentile for age. He is not pale or jaundiced. Cardiovascular, respiratory, and gastrointestinal systems are normal. His nails are normal in appearance. There are small mobile lymph nodes palpable in the cervical, inguinal, and axillary regions. Capillary refill is less than 2 seconds. The rash is shown in the picture.
