# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

- Chronic dry scaly rash affecting flexures; no fever

- What is your diagnosis?

- Atopic dermatitis

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

- Typical morphology and distribution of rash
- Itchy rash
- Strong family history of atopy

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

  - Rashes over his perineal region, which did not involve the folds. This is more likely to be contact/diaper dermatitis.

- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

  - None

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

  - No good alternative diagnosis

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

  - No. There is no evidence of superimposed bacterial infection (purulent discharge, yellowish crusting, fever, erythema, and induration). There is also no evidence of eczema herpeticum (blisters or punched out ulcers at affected areas).

- What is your plan for this patient?

  - Intensive moisturizing

  - Topical antiseptics such as bleach baths

  - Topical steroids for acutely inflamed areas

  - Wet wraps

  - Skin testing for possible allergic triggers

  - Eliminate triggers after identification of allergic triggers

  - Consider giving antihistamine to decrease the itch if there is a lot of scratching, especially at night

# Sleep-Related Issues

Daniel Goh

# DEFINITION OF SLEEP

- Sleep is not simply the opposite of wakefulness, and it is not a passive state.
- It is an active phase where many vital bodily functions take place.

# NORMAL SLEEP ACROSS THE AGES

- Sleep patterns develop and evolve over the first years of life, and it is during the first years of life that sleep habits are established.
- Normal sleep alternates between rapid eye movement (REM) and nonrapid eye movement (NREM) sleep, in cycles between four and six times per night (see Fig. 48-1).
- Each cycle lasts about 50 min in infancy and increases to between 90 and 100 min in older children and adulthood.
- The proportion of REM sleep increases, whereas NREM sleep decreases through the night.
- REM sleep is the time when new information is processed and stored into our memory banks. It plays a role in the reorganization of brain processes that handle the flow, structure, and storage of information. It is also the phase when most dreams occur. Breathing is more rapid and irregular during REM sleep, and the heart rate and blood pressure also increase to near-waking levels. Core temperature is not well regulated during this time and tends towards the ambient temperature. Muscles become paralyzed and unresponsive during REM sleep.
- NREM sleep can be divided into Stages 1–4. The function of NREM sleep is somatic and restores the body. Growth hormones peak during deep NREM sleep. For optimal growth, children not only have to get enough sleep, but they also have got to get enough deep NREM sleep. Muscles become more relaxed during NREM sleep.
- There is no exact number of hours of sleep that every child should get, but the approximate number of hours in each age group has been described. There are significant individual variations in sleep needs and tolerance for sleep inadequacy. The adequate amount of sleep for an individual is that which enables the child to be well rested and function optionally.

# THE EFFECTS OF INADEQUATE OR POOR SLEEP

- Sleep is vital for our bodies and mind to function optimally; it is also essential for good health.

528

![img-57.jpeg](img-57.jpeg)

![img-58.jpeg](img-58.jpeg)

FIGURE 48-1

Histogram of normal sleep in child, young adult. Reference: Am Fam Physician 2001;63:277–84.

- Sleep deprivation has been associated with increased risk of hypertension, diabetes, obesity, impaired immunity, as well as many other chronic ailments. In childhood, where there is rapid growth and development, sleep deprivation can impair these processes and also lead to chronic health problems in the long term.

# SYMPTOMS OF SLEEP PROBLEMS

- Irregularity of sleep

- Day-night reversal
- Unpredictable timing and duration of sleep

- Bedtime problems

- Refusal/resistance to going to bed

- Inability to fall asleep on his/her own
- Taking a long time to fall asleep

# • Awakenings during the night

- Inability to sleep through the night
- Frequent awakenings but can fall back to sleep on his/her own
- Frequent awakenings and needs help/intervention to go back to sleep

# • Early awakening

# • Abnormal noise or movement during sleep

- Snoring
- Choking sound
- Stops breathing
- Teeth grinding
- Talking in the sleep
- Screaming
- Sleep walking
- Moving the legs
- Jerks
- Head banging
- Body rocking

# • Excessive daytime sleepiness

- Problem waking up in the morning
- Falls asleep easily during the day
- Problem staying awake during the day
- Needs to sleep during the day

# • Sleeps too little

# • Headache

- That wakes patient up from sleep.
- That is present on awakening (after a night's sleep or a nap).

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

# Sleep problems may be

- Primary, i.e., related to the brain's development of normal sleep pattern
- Secondary

- Environmental causes, e.g., the caregiver's influences, school demands, screen time, caffeine intake
- Medical causes, e.g.

- Airway abnormality or craniofacial abnormality causing obstructive sleep apnea (OSA)
- Gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD) affecting sleep
- Seizures during sleep
- Secondary causes of headache affecting sleep (raised intracranial pressure, etc.)
- Stress, depression

# COMMON DIAGNOSES

- The common diagnoses and their salient features are shown in Table 48-1.
- Information regarding obstructive sleep apnea is detailed in the section after the table.

TABLE 48-1

COMMON DIAGNOSIS AND THEIR SALIENT FEATURES

|  Diagnosis | Salient Features | Notes  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Day-night reversal | Awake during the night, sleeps during the day. | Common in first few weeks of life; should resolve after that  |
|  Sleep pattern irregularity | Unpredictable timing and/or duration of sleep | Sleep/wake cycles may take 6–12 weeks to be established in the newborn  |
|  Nonconsolidated sleep | Inability to sleep through the night; will wake up but able to fall back to sleep on his/her own | Sleep consolidation is usually achieved by 9 months  |
|  Sleep-onset association disorder | Needs the caregiver's intervention to initiate sleep Inability to sleep through the night; can arouse four to six times a night After arousing, some may be able to fall back to sleep on his/her own, whereas others need caregiver intervention to fall back to sleep | Inability to self-soothe Often requires rocking or feeding or being held to get to sleep  |
|  Rhythmic movement disorders | Head banging, body rocking | These are self-soothing behaviors; usually at sleep onset or after night-time arousals; more common in boys (4:1 ratio)  |
|  Bedtime resistance | Refuses to go to sleep or to sleep on his/her own; may need a parent to accompany to sleep | Common in infants and toddlers  |
|  Nightmares | Frightening dreams that usually awake the child | Usually occurs in the latter half of the night where REM sleep predominates Recollection of dream content No confusion or disorientation  |
|  Night terrors | Sudden distressing, often frightening and dramatic events | Usually occurs in first few hours of sleep where NREM sleep predominates No recollection of events Often positive family history  |
|  Sleepwalking | Ambulates but unaware May also have night terrors due to common genetic predisposition | No recollection of events Often positive family history  |
|  Obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) | Often presents with snoring, restlessness in sleep, increased effort in breathing or observed cessation of respiration during sleep (apnea) | History alone cannot diagnose the condition. The polysomnography (sleep study) is the gold standard for diagnosis and evaluation of severity  |

Continued

TABLE 48-1

COMMON DIAGNOSIS AND THEIR SALIENT FEATURES—Cont'd

|  Diagnosis | Salient Features | Notes  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Irregular sleep/ wake schedules | Usually a result of school demands; bedtimes and wake times differ during school and nonschool days |   |
|  Poor sleep hygiene | Practices that increase night-time arousals and difficulty sleeping: screen time near bed time, e.g., watching television or playing computer games, napping late in evening, caffeine intake |   |
|  Bruxism | Repetitive grinding and clenching of teeth during sleep |   |
|  Inadequate sleep | Often contributed by academic and extracurricular activities; usually late-night activities and late bedtime coupled with early school start time results in inadequate sleep | Sleeps less than recommended hours Delayed shift in circadian rhythms in adolescence contributes to the later sleep onset and risk for inadequate duration of sleep May have other causes, e.g., stress, depression  |
|  Delayed sleep phase syndrome | Shift in sleep—wake schedules that interfere with school or work demands | Contributed by circadian rhythm shift at puberty Usually no problems maintaining sleep  |
|  Insomnia | Difficulty initiating and/or maintaining sleep | Insomnia can be primary or secondary, e.g., stress, depression, caffeine  |
|  Restless leg syndrome | Uncomfortable sensations in legs, resulting in motor restlessness, especially at night | Diagnosed on clinical history Often coexists with PLMD  |
|  Periodic leg movement disorder (PLMD) | Periodic, episodic, and repetitive limb movements during sleep associated with partial arousal or awakening | Objectively quantified by a polysomnography  |
|  Narcolepsy | Excessive (uncontrollable and overwhelming) daytime sleepiness with or without cataplexy, hypnagogic hallucination, and sleep paralysis | Diagnostic tests: polysomnography and multiple sleep latency test (MSLT)  |
|  Headache that is related to sleep | A headache that wakes a patient up from sleep A headache that is present upon awakening from sleep or a nap | These are symptoms highly suggestive of a secondary cause of headache; possible differentials include inadequate sleep, OSA, raised intracranial pressure (see Chapter 34)  |

# • Obstructive Sleep Apnea Hypopnea Syndrome in Childhood

• Obstructive sleep apnea hypopnea syndrome (OSAHS) is a common sleep-related breathing disorder in childhood. It is characterized by episodic complete obstruction (apnea) or prolonged partial obstruction (hypopnea) of the upper airway during sleep.
• In children because of the presence partial obstruction (hypopnea), the term OSAHS is often, and more appropriately, used instead of OSA.
• The peak age is between 3 and 8 years old and coincides with the peak of adenotonsillar hypertrophy.
• Other risk factors for OSAHS include obesity, hypotonia, craniofacial abnormalities (e.g., micrognathia, midfacial hypoplasia), reduction in central respiratory drive (e.g., Arnold–Chiari malformation, brainstem injury).
• OSAHS in children has very different characteristics from OSA in adults (see Table 48-2).

TABLE 48-2

DIFFERENCES BETWEEN CHILDHOOD AND ADULT OSA

|  Parameter | Childhood OSAHS | Adult OSA  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Peak age | 3–8 years | 30–60 years  |
|  Gender | Male = female | Male >> female + postmenopausal female  |
|  Body weight | Normal, obese, or failure to thrive | Usually obese  |
|  Common causative factor | Adenotonsillar hypertrophy | Obesity  |
|  Estimated prevalence | 1%–2% | 2%–4%  |
|  Polysomnography features: |  |   |
|  • Apnea Index (abnormal) | >1/hour | >10/hour  |
|  • Abnormalities | More often prolonged partial obstruction | Recurrent complete obstruction episodes  |
|  • Sleep architecture | Usually normal | Often abnormal  |
|  • Daytime sleepiness | Uncommon | Common  |
|  Usual treatment modality | Tonsillectomy and adenoidectomy (T&A) if there is adenotonsillar hypertrophy; may need CPAP if T&A does not help | Continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP)  |

# 1. Evaluate Sleep in Routine Checkups; Use a Sleep Screening Algorithm

- It is important to include sleep assessment in all routine pediatric consultations.
- A simple tool that can be used to screen for sleep problems is the BEARS sleep screening algorithm (Table 48-3).

# 2. History and Physical Examination

- Take a detailed history of the sleep issues.

- What exactly is the "issue"?
- What are the signs and symptoms?
- Ask about sleep habits, sleep patterns.

- Ask about psychosocial history, family history, caregiver history, family's expectations about sleep.

- What is the impact on the child's growth and development?
- What impact is it having on the child's function, e.g., ability to play with other children, school performance?
- What impact is it having on the child's family, e.g., does it affect the caregiver's ability to get a full night's sleep?

TABLE 48-3

BEARS SLEEP SCREENING ALGORITHM

|   | **Toddler/Preschool (2–5 years)** | **School-Aged (6–12 years)** | **Adolescent (13–18 years)**  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  1. Bedtime problems | Does your child have any problem going to bed? Falling asleep? | Does your child have any problem at bedtime? (P) Do you have any problem going to bed? (C) | Do you have any problem falling asleep at bedtime? (C)  |
|  2. Excessive daytime sleepiness | Does your child seem overtired or sleepy a lot during the day? Does your child still take naps? | Does your child have difficulty waking in the morning, seem sleepy during the day, or take naps? (P) Do you feel tired a lot? (C) | Do you feel sleepy a lot during the day? In school? While driving? (C)  |
|  3. Awakenings during the night | Does your child wake up a lot at night? | Does your child seem to wake up a lot at night? Any sleepwalking or nightmare? (P) Do you wake up a lot at night? Have trouble getting back to sleep? (C) | Do you wake up a lot at night? Have trouble getting back to sleep? (C)  |
|  4. Regularity and duration of sleep | Does your child have a regular bedtime and wake time? What are they? | What time does your child go to bed and get up on school days? Weekends? Do you think he/she is getting enough sleep? (P) | What time do you usually go to bed on school nights? Weekends? How much sleep do you usually get? (C)  |
|  5. Snoring | Does your child snore a lot or have difficulty breathing at night? | Does your child have loud or nightly snoring or any breathing difficulty at night? (P) | Does your teenager snore loudly or nightly? (P)  |

Notes: C, child-directed question; P, parent-directed question.

Source: Jodi A. Mindell and Judith A. Owens. "A Clinical Guide to Pediatric Sleep: Diagnosis and Management of Sleep Problems"; Lippincott Williams & Wilkins.

• Do a systemic review.
• Ask about medication history, supplements.
• Do a complete head-to-toe physical examination, including growth and development.
• Remember that sleep problems may sometimes be a symptom of a medical problem, e.g., airway abnormality or craniofacial abnormality causing OSA; GERD affecting sleep; seizures during sleep; secondary causes of headache affecting sleep, stress, depression.

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Failure to thrive
- Developmental delay
- Central cyanosis (intermittent or persistent)
- Symptoms suggestive of seizures
- A headache that wakes a patient up from sleep (suggestive of secondary headache)
- A headache that is present upon awakening from sleep or a nap (need to exclude raised intracranial pressure)
- Depression (patient, caregiver and/or family members)

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

An 18-month-old infant boy is seen at your clinic for routine well baby assessment and immunization. His mother incidentally mentions that she is very tired looking after the child. She describes herself as not having

had much sleep since the child was born. On further questioning, you learn that this is the first child, born at term with a good birth weight of 3.3 kg after an uneventful pregnancy. The baby has no significant medical history and has been growing well. However, the baby repeatedly (more than five or six times a night) wakes up throughout the night and cries very loudly, and can only be pacified by breastfeeding and/or carried and rocked in arms. The child has been totally breastfed since birth and only started weaning diet at 6 months old but continued on supplemental breastfeeding at least once or twice a day but multiple times throughout the night. The child is otherwise feeding well and has no problems of regurgitation or vomiting. Bedtime is not regular or consistent, and there is no bedtime routine; the baby is usually nursed to sleep.

To put the child to sleep, the child is sometimes carried and rocked in the arms and the same is also done to put him to sleep when he wakes up in the night. He is also described as a light sleeper who awakens every time he is put back to bed after being rocked to sleep. At other times, he is allowed to latch on to the mother's breast like a pacifier and suckle for comfort, but he will awaken with loud crying when he is unlatched from the breast.

As a result of the sleep issues, the child's father now sleeps on a mattress in the same room, whereas the child sleeps with his mother on the same bed.

On examination, the child is well. His growth parameters are well within normal for his age. There are no dysmorphic features noted. The chest, heart, and abdominal examinations are normal. The heart rate, respiratory rate, and temperature are normal. Neurological examination and developmental milestones are normal.

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?

o Poor sleep initiation and maintenance in an otherwise well child. There are recurrent nocturnal awakenings with inability to self-soothe to return himself to sleep.

- What is your diagnosis?

o Sleep-onset association disorder

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

o The baby repeatedly (more than five or six times a night) wakes up throughout the night and cries very loudly, and can only be pacified by breastfeeding and/or carried and rocked in arms.

o The child has to be carried and rocked in the arms to be put to sleep each time he wakes up and awakens every time he is put back to bed.

o He also latches on to the mother's breast like a pacifier most of the time and awakens and cries when he is unlatched from the breast.

- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

o None

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

o Sleep pattern irregularity; poor night-time sleep consolidation

- For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

o Frequent awakenings, unable to sleep through the night

o Inconsistent sleep routine

- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o These are the problems that occur in the first year of life and should have resolved by this age.

o Child not able to go back to sleep on his own; needs parental intervention.

- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

o Sleep-onset association disorder

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

o Persistent difficulty in self-soothing results in frequent and problematic night waking in the child and loss of sleep in the parent/caregiver; risk factor for stress in family and stress/depression in caregivers.

- What is your plan for this patient?

o No need for any investigation in this case. The history and clinical examinations are most essential in the assessment.

o Establish a consistent and regular bedtime routine.

o Train the child to self-soothe – parents should not respond immediately to the baby's sounds and allow the child to get back to sleep independently. Put the child back to bed drowsy but awake so as to encourage the development of self-soothing skills.

o Reassure parents.

o Review and monitor the child and family's progress.

# Sore Throat

Mas Suhaila Bte Isa

# DEFINITION OF SORE THROAT

A sore throat refers to pain felt in the throat.

# SYMPTOMS OF SORE THROAT

- Older children often report pain at the back of the throat, which may be worsened with swallowing (known as odynophagia).
- Babies and younger children are often unable to give the same information; rather, they present with irritability, drooling, refusal to feed, decreased feeding, or crying during feeding.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY AND COMMON DIAGNOSES

Causes: The causes of sore throat, listed from the most common to the least common, are

- Infection of structures around the throat; the etiology may be
  - Viral, which is often self-limiting
  - Bacterial, which often requires antibiotic treatment
- Post nasal drip, e.g., from allergic rhinitis
- Gastroesophageal reflux
- Chronic mouth breathing, e.g., obstructive sleep apnea (OSA)
- Foreign body, e.g., fish bone
- Injuries, e.g., trauma, ingestion of bleach, smoke inhalation
- Allergic reaction, e.g., anaphylaxis

# 1. Viral Infections

- Viral pharyngitis

- Is due to respiratory viruses such as influenza, parainfluenza, rhinovirus, coronavirus, respiratory syncytial virus, adenovirus.
- Common associated symptoms include rhinorrhea, cough, hoarseness, fever, lethargy, and reduced oral intake. There may be a positive sick contact with upper respiratory tract infection (URTI) symptoms. Physical examination shows nasal congestion with erythematous throat, tonsils may or may not be enlarged, and there are no exudates seen. Cervical lymph nodes may be swollen and tender.
- Conjunctivitis and enlarged tonsils with exudates can be found in adenovirus infection.
- Complications of viral pharyngitis include dehydration, otitis media, sinusitis, lower respiratory tract involvement, secondary bacterial infections.

540

• Infectious mononucleosis (IMS)

• Usually due to Epstein–Barr virus (EBV) or cytomegalovirus. Primary HIV infection can also present with sore throat and IMS-like syndrome.
• Enlarged erythematous tonsils with exudates, hepatosplenomegaly, and enlarged tender posterior cervical lymph nodes are seen in IMS.

• Herpangina and hand, foot, and mouth disease (HFMD)

• Due to coxsackievirus, echovirus, and herpes simplex virus.
• Vesicles or ulcers in the posterior pharynx are seen in herpangina and HFMD.
• Erythematous papules and vesicles over palms and soles are seen in HFMD.
• The pain of the sore throat can be quite severe.
• May be complicated by dehydration, encephalitis, and myocarditis.

# 2. Bacterial Infections

• Bacterial pharyngitis is most commonly due to Group A β-hemolytic streptococcus (Streptococcus pyogenes).
• Less common severe conditions that start with a sore throat but can progress rapidly to life-threatening emergencies include

• Peritonsillar abscess, also known as quinsy

• Epiglottitis

• Deep neck tissue abscess, e.g., retropharyngeal abscess, lateral pharyngeal abscess

• Corynebacteria diphtheria

• Uncommon bacterial causes include

• Group G and C β-hemolytic streptococcus
• Neisseria gonorrhea
• Arcanobacterium haemolyticum
• Mixed anaerobes in Vincent angina
• Mycoplasma pneumoniae or Chlamydia pneumoniae
• Francisella tularensis
• Yersinia enterocolitica or pestis

• Salient clinical features of the various bacterial infections

• Group A β-hemolytic streptococcus (GAS) (often termed “strep throat”)

■ Most common among children between 5 and 11 years (unusual in <3 years old).
■ Usually sudden onset, moderate to severe pain on swallowing, high fever, nonspecific symptoms like headache, nausea, vomiting, or abdominal pain. There can be accompanying scarlet fever rash. Clinical examination shows tonsillopharyngeal erythema and swelling, with whitish exudates, palatal petechiae, and swollen and tender anterior cervical lymph nodes.

■ GAS pharyngitis cannot be accurately diagnosed by history and physical examination alone. The use of a validated clinical decision

rule (e.g., modified Centor score, FeverPAIN score) with selective use of rapid antigen detection testing (when available) may improve diagnostic accuracy and aid the decision-making with regard to antibiotics.

# • Peritonsillar abscess

- Severe sore throat and pain on swallowing first with solids than with liquids.
- Clinical findings may include tonsillar exudate and cervical adenitis, fullness or bulging of the affected tonsil, and deviation of the uvula toward the contralateral tonsil.

# • Epiglottitis

- Severe sore throat of rapid onset with fever, stridor, muffled "hot potato" voice.
- Clinical examination shows toxic appearance, drooling, child prefers to sit upright and forward, tachycardia, tachypnea, with use of accessory muscles and retractions.

# • Deep neck tissue infections like retropharyngeal or lateral pharyngeal abscess

- Similar to epiglottitis but in addition may have a palpable tender and fluctuant swelling in the neck with fever and cervical adenitis.
- This child may have an abnormal neck posture, for example, torticollis and neck stiffness.

# • Group G and C β-hemolytic streptococci

- Similar in features to GAS pharyngitis.
- It can be associated with poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis, and reactive arthritis though association with acute rheumatic fever is less common.

# • Arcanobacterium haemolyticum infection

- Usually mild fever and sore throat. Resembles GAS with pharyngeal erythema, tonsillar exudate, cervical adenopathy, and scarlatiniform rash.
- It can also cause a membranous pharyngitis that can mimic diphtheria.

# • Pharyngeal diphtheria (Corynebacterium diphtheriae)

- Rare now unless unimmunized.
- Unilateral or bilateral tonsillar greyish pseudomembrane seen, which can extend to lower larynx and airways and cause severe obstruction.
- There can be accompanying soft tissue swelling, and cervical and submental lymphadenopathy giving rise to a "bull-neck" appearance.

# • Neisseria gonorrhea pharyngitis

- Seen in a sexually active person.
- Can cause exudative tonsillopharyngitis and cervical adenopathy.
- If isolated in pharynx of a prepubertal child, suspect sexual abuse.

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. Initial Impression

- Initial impression

- Assess appearance
- Assess work of breathing
- Assess circulation to skin (color)

- The initial impression helps determine the next best steps.

- If indicated, do primary assessment, secondary assessment (see Chapter 36 III-Looking Baby or Child).
- If not indicated, proceed with history and physical examination.

# 2. History

- Where is the sore throat? (Ask the patient to point to the area of pain.)

- Focality or laterality suggests foreign body (e.g., fish bone), abscess. Note that these conditions may cause pain that is central as well.

- Duration of symptoms

- Acute/short duration is usually due to infection, foreign body, injuries, or anaphylaxis.
- Chronic sore throat may point to postnasal drip, gastroesophageal reflux, or chronic mouth breathing as a cause.

- Severity of sore throat

- Severe sore throat may interrupt feeding with attendant dehydration.

- Onset – gradual or sudden?

- Sudden onset: foreign body aspiration, injuries, or anaphylaxis
- Rapid progression: infection, anaphylaxis

- Any specific event prior to sore throat, e.g., choking, ingestion of fish bone or foreign body, exposure to potential allergen?
- What are the associated symptoms?

- Fever almost always points to infection as a cause.
- Very ill-looking = bacterial infections, allergic reactions
- Altered conscious state = sepsis, shock, encephalitis
- Runny nose +/- cough suggests a viral etiology.
- Conjunctivitis = adenovirus
- Swollen eyes = IMS, allergic reaction
- Face pain = acute sinusitis, may be a complication of an upper respiratory tract infection
- Ear pain = otitis media, sometimes a complication of an upper respiratory tract infection
- Voice change

- Hoarse voice = usually seen in infections that affect the larynx or due to repeated coughing
- Hot potato voice = acute epiglottitis

- Cough

- Many of the causes of sore throat are associated with cough.

■ Cough may also represent involvement of the lower respiratory tract (infection, anaphylaxis, pulmonary edema, etc.)

○ Upper airway obstruction or breathing difficulty; e.g., noisy breathing or stridor, drooling, difficulty in breathing when lying supine, rapid breathing, cyanosis.

■ Bacterial infections like peritonsillar abscess, epiglottitis, bacterial trachitis, deep neck tissue abscess, diphtheria.

■ Upper airway obstruction can be rarely seen in some viral infections, e.g., IMS.

■ Lower respiratory tract infections can be associated with some upper respiratory tract infections (e.g., influenza, parainfluenza, respiratory syncytial virus); upper respiratory tract infections can predispose to secondary bacterial respiratory tract infections.

■ Some viral infections can cause myocarditis and symptoms of respiratory distress due to heart failure.

○ Drooling – can be seen when there is severe sore throat and the child does not want to swallow because swallowing induces pain; can be seen when there is evidence of upper airway obstruction or breathing difficulty.

○ Headache – many viral infections, GAS

○ Neck lumps, neck swelling, neck pain = viral infection (less likely to be tender/painful), bacterial infection (more likely to be tender/painful)

○ Painful neck movements or limited neck movement = presence of painful neck lump, pharyngeal abscess

○ Rash on body = Streptococcus, Arcanobacterium, allergic reaction, IMS postexposure to penicillin

○ Rash on palms/soles = HFMD

○ Vomiting, diarrhea, abdominal pain – many viral infections, GAS, allergic reaction

○ Joint aches, muscle aches = many viral infections

○ Trauma (physical, chemical, gaseous)

○ Chronic history of waking up with a sore throat (consider obstructive sleep apnea, mouth breathing when sleeping, postnasal drip, gastroesophageal reflux)

○ Symptoms suggestive of OSA (snoring, disturbed sleep, daytime somnolence)

○ Symptoms suggestive of gastroesophageal reflux (regurgitation, chest pain, acid brash, position-related symptoms)

○ Allergic rhinitis (itchy nose, runny nose, sneezing, triggers, other atopic symptoms)

• Any close contact with anyone sore throat? With fever? With URTI symptoms? With streptococcal infection? With hand, foot, and mouth disease?

• Does the child attend childcare? This is a risk factor for infectious etiology.

• Immunization status (specifically to diphtheria, Haemophilus influenzae type b, influenza)

- Assess hydration status (thirst, reduced urine output, reduced activity, weight loss)
- Medication history, especially antibiotic use, potential allergens
- Allergies
- Travel history
- Sexual history if appropriate

### 3. Physical Examination

- General inspection

- Is the child toxic looking? Unwell? Altered conscious state? Off color? Cyanosed?
- What is the posture of the child? Is the child leaning forward? Does the child resist lying down?
- Is the child drooling?
- Can the child speak?
- What is his voice quality?
- Listen for stridor and look for other signs of respiratory distress.

- If there are any concerns that the child is ill-looking, escalate and evaluate as stated in Chapter 36 Ill-Looking Baby or Child

- If stable, then proceed to examine further:

- Weight, body mass index = overweight is a risk factor for OSA
- Facial shape and structure = may be a risk factor for OSA
- Any sign of trauma to the head and neck area
- Facial tenderness over the frontal sinus or maxillary sinus area = may be indicative of sinusitis
- Hydration status (see Chapter 19)
- Eyes (look for conjunctivitis, periorbital swelling)
- Cervical lymphadenopathy (seen in infection, with painful cervical lymphadenopathy more suggestive of bacterial etiology)
- Other neck lumps, tenderness = more likely bacterial infection
- Limitation of neck movements (due to painful neck lump, meningeal irritation)
- Respiratory examination (look to see if there is any respiratory distress, upper air way obstruction, lower respiratory tract infection)
- Cardiovascular examination (abnormal in shock, diphtheria, myocarditis that is associated with some viral infections)
- Abdominal examination (hepatosplenomegaly in IMS)
- Neurological examination (encephalopathy, encephalitis)
- Look for rash (generalized or over palms and soles; nature of rash)
- Ear exam (acute otitis media is sometimes an association or complication of an upper respiratory tract infection; chronic otitis media)
- Nose and nasal discharge (seen in viral URTI, sinusitis)
- Leave throat examination to the last as it can be painful and uncomfortable.

- Look for pharyngeal papules or ulcers, tonsillar enlargement, pharynx or tonsillar erythema, presence of exudates, foreign body or thrush.

- Look for palatal petechiae.

4. Algorithm

Continued from previous page

![img-60.jpeg](img-60.jpeg)

# LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Significant upper air way obstruction, i.e., stridor, in respiratory distress, drooling, sitting leaning forward.

- Attend to the child quickly but do not agitate the child.
- Keep child in a position of comfort, which is often on the caregiver's lap.
- Do not examine the throat of a child whom you suspect to have epiglottitis as this can cause worsening of the airway obstruction.
- Escalate to senior doctor immediately with view to intubation.

- Significant respiratory distress
- Hemodynamic instability
- Ill-looking child
- Dehydrated child
- Altered conscious status

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

Alex, a 5-year-old boy, presents to the Children's Emergency with a 2-day history of sore throat. He is a healthy boy who was born at term with an unremarkable birth history. His immunizations are up to date. He is not on any medication and has no allergies. His developmental milestones have been normal. He attends fulltime day care. His 11-year-old sister had a cough and runny nose last week.

He did not finish his dinner last night and had complained of sore throat. This morning, he took only a few sips of milk and refused anything else after that. He has passed urine once this morning. He feels warm but his parents have not measured his temperature.

On examination, Alex is alert and not in respiratory distress. His blood pressure is 90/52 mmHg, heart rate (HR) 100/min, respiratory rate 30/min, and oxygen saturation 99% on room air. His temperature is 38.9°C. He has no signs of increased work of breathing and no stridor. His oral examination reveals slightly dry mucous membranes with no thrush. His pharynx was erythematous with no ulcers or palatal petechiae seen. Both his tonsils were enlarged and erythematous with whitish-yellow exudates. His lung examination was normal with good equal breath sounds bilaterally. There were multiple small (less than 1 cm), soft, mobile, and slightly tender anterior cervical lymph nodes palpable. There were no other enlarged lymph nodes and his abdominal exam was normal with no hepatosplenomegaly. Cardiovascular and neurological examination was unremarkable.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

• What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

• For each of these alternative diagnoses:

  ◦ List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

  ◦ List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

  ◦ If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

• What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

  ◦ Sore throat with fever and poor feeding

• What is your diagnosis?

  ◦ Pharyngitis likely secondary to Group A β-hemolytic streptococcus

  ◦ Possibly mild dehydration

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

  ◦ Erythematous pharynx with enlarged, inflamed tonsils with presence of exudates

  ◦ Tender, enlarged anterior cervical lymph nodes

  ◦ Lack of conjunctivitis or hepatosplenomegaly

  ◦ Decreased urine output, mucous dry, HR stable, BP stable, alert and conscious

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

  ◦ Positive contact with a family member who had recent URTI symptoms

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

  ◦ No information about the presence/absence of runny nose or nasal discharge

  ◦ Palatal petechiae, although this is nonspecific for GAS

  ◦ No information about the presence of rash that may suggest scarlet fever

• What are your alternative diagnoses if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?

  ◦ Viral pharyngitis

  ◦ Infectious mononucleosis (IMS) secondary to EBV infection

• For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

  ◦ Positive close contact with a family member with URTI may suggest a viral etiology.

  ◦ Tonsillar exudates can be present with IMS.

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

  ◦ Presence of acute onset of fever, tonsillar exudates, and tender adenopathy is more suggestive of GAS rather than viral etiology.

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

  ◦ In IMS, usually posterior cervical adenopathy, hepatomegaly, and splenomegaly are present.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

  - It is still most likely GAS pharyngitis

- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

  - Signs of mild dehydration present with dry mucous membranes

- What is your plan for this patient?

  - Perform a throat swab for rapid streptococcal antigen test (if test available) and culture.

  - Treat with amoxicillin or penicillin V for 10 days.

  - Provide symptom relief with analgesia, antipyretics as needed.

  - Encourage oral hydration and advise to return to Children's Emergency if there is worsening of sore throat or symptoms of dehydration.

  - Educate on mode of transmission and encourage patient and family to adopt infectious disease control measures.

  - Outpatient clinic review in 3 days when culture results are expected.

# Vomiting

Denise L.M. Goh • Elizabeth Y. Ang

# DEFINITION OF VOMITING

- Vomiting is also known as throwing up.
- It is an involuntary, vigorous ejection of the stomach's contents through the mouth and sometimes through the nose.

# SYMPTOMS OF VOMITING

- May be preceded by nausea (a feeling of wanting to vomit) and/or retching (involuntary spasmodic contractions of the abdominal and thoracic muscles against a closed glottis).
- Vomiting presents as an involuntary, forceful reappearance of previously ingested food/liquid through the mouth. It is differentiated from regurgitation, which is usually seen as an "effortless" or unforceful reappearance of previously ingested food/liquid.

# PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

- The brain has a vomiting center, which is situated in the medulla.
- Input from various parts of body to the vomiting center can trigger vomiting.
  - Cerebral cortex
  - Vestibular system
  - Chemoreceptor trigger zone
  - Other body organs, e.g., pharynx, gastrointestinal (GI) tract, other systemic illness

# COMMON DIAGNOSES

# 1. Cerebral Cortex Causes

- Causes

- Raised intracranial pressure (tumor, bleed, hydrocephalus)
- Head injury (concussion, contusion, cerebral edema, postconcussion, etc.)
- Infection (meningitis, encephalitis)
- Hypertensive emergency
- Migraine
- Eating disorder (e.g., bulimia)
- Anxiety/stress/fear
- Behavioral causes
  - Sensory food aversions/anticipatory gagging
  - Post-traumatic feeding disorder (following nasogastric tube feeding, or force feeding)

551

# • Manifestations

• Symptoms and signs of raised intracranial pressure can include headache, altered mental state, abnormal posturing, increased tone, hyperreflexia, hypertension, bradycardia, irregular breathing, abnormal pupil size, papilledema (Cushing triad = hypertension, bradycardia, and irregular breathing; it is an ominous sign of impending coning).
• Symptoms and signs of possible significant head injury include significant force/trauma, fall from a high height, presence of fractures, amnesia, loss of consciousness, altered mental state.
• Symptoms and signs of intracranial infection can include fever, neck pain, neck stiffness, altered mental state, ill-looking.
• Symptoms of hypertensive emergency: headache, blurred vision, epistaxis, nausea, vomiting, altered mental state, seizure, chest pain, dyspnea.
• Symptoms and signs of migraine can include aura, flashing lights, headache, relieved by resting or lying in the dark, or analgesia.
• See Chapter 27 for behavioral causes.

# 2. Vestibular System Causes

# • Causes

• Motion sickness
• Vestibular neuritis

# • Manifestations

• Symptoms and signs of motion sickness include nausea, vomiting, headache: symptoms come on with motion/travel and disappear when motion/travel ceases.
• Symptoms and signs of vestibular neuritis include vertigo, tinnitus, may be worsened by visual input, better when the eyes are closed, associated with nystagmus.

# 3. Chemoreceptor Trigger Zone Causes

# • Causes

• Uremia
• Hypoglycemia
• Hyperglycemia, diabetic ketoacidosis
• Hyperammonemia
• Inborn errors of metabolism (IEM), e.g., urea cycle defect, organic acidemia
• Sepsis/bacteremia
• Pregnancy
• Drugs; e.g., chemotherapy drugs, opioids
• Poisons; e.g., salicylate

# • Manifestations

• Symptoms and signs of uremia may include fluid retention, decreased urine output, lethargy, altered mental state.

- Symptoms and signs of chronic kidney disease will be present only if the kidney function has been deteriorating over a prolonged period of time.
- Symptoms and signs of hypoglycemia may include feeling hungry, feeling shaky, feeling sweaty, feeling anxious, feeling light headed, altered mental state, seizure.
- Symptoms and signs of hyperglycemia may include polyuria, feeling thirsty, dehydrated.
- Symptoms and signs of inborn errors of metabolism may include fluctuating mental status, relationship to eating/fasting, relationship to certain kinds of food, other signs and symptoms that are unique to the IEM.

### 4. Pharynx and Respiratory Tract Causes

- Causes

- Triggering of the gag reflex either accidentally or intentionally (e.g., eating disorder with bulimia)
- Post-tussive vomiting

### 5. Gastrointestinal Causes

- Causes

- Infection (e.g., viral gastroenteritis, bacterial gastroenteritis, food poisoning, hepatitis)
- Inflammatory (appendicitis, pancreatitis, hepatitis, inflammatory bowel disease, gut ischemia, cholecystitis, cholangitis)
- Surgical causes

- Luminal obstruction (foreign body, pyloric stenosis, volvulus, duodenal hematoma, adhesions, obstructed hernia, intussusception)
- Gut inflammation, gut perforation (appendicitis, bowel perforation, peritonitis)

- Peptic ulcer disease
- Food intolerance
- Allergy (anaphylaxis, food allergy)

- Manifestations

- Signs and symptoms of infection may include fever, diarrhea, abdominal pain, abdominal tenderness, relationship to food, contact history, lack of food hygiene.
- Signs and symptoms of inflammatory conditions may include fever, abdominal pain, abdominal tenderness, change in bowel habit.
- Signs and symptoms of surgical causes may include abdominal colic, bilious vomiting, change in bowel habit, abdominal distension.
- Signs and symptoms of gastroesophageal reflux may include heart burn, relationship to eating, relationship to volume of food, relationship to posture.
- Signs and symptoms of allergy may include relationship to eating, relationship to particular foods, may be associated with throat tightening, eye swelling, rashes.

# 6. Others

- Viral fever with vomiting

# CLINICAL APPROACH AND ALGORITHM

# 1. History Taking

- Is it really vomiting?

- Is it stomach contents that are coming up?
- Is it coming up forcefully and involuntarily or effortless?

- Onset, duration, frequency

- Is it acute or chronic? If it is chronic, are there any other symptoms in between the vomiting episodes, or is the child well in between episodes?

- Content of vomitus

- Any bilious vomiting? Surgical cause until proven otherwise.
- Any blood in the vomitus? May be due to swallowed blood, an upper GI bleed (see Chapter 6).

- Any triggers?

- Motion (motion sickness)
- Waking up from sleep (raised intracranial pressure)
- Food related (overfeeding, food intolerance, allergy, food poisoning, GI tract infection, inborn error of metabolism, gastroesophageal reflux, peptic ulcer disease)
- Sight of food (behavioral cause)
- Self-induced (eating disorder)
- After a head injury (raised intracranial pressure, concussion, contusion, cerebral edema, intracranial bleed)
- An event/situation which triggers anxiety, stress, or fear
- Medication (side-effect, adverse effect, overdose)
- Cough (post-tussive, aspiration)

- What are the associated symptoms?

- Do a systems review.
- Ask about newborn screening.
- Get a travel history, contact history, medication history, family history.
- Assess hydration.
- Assess growth.
- There are many possible symptoms. The following list shows the more common ones and their interpretations:

- Fever (viral illness with vomiting, viral GE bacterial GE, hepatitis, appendicitis, peritonitis, meningitis, encephalitis)

- Abdominal pain

- Colicky (GI cause, diabetic ketoacidosis, anxiety, stress, fever, acute porphyria)
- Constant (peritonitis, surgical causes, bowel ischemia)

- Abdominal tenderness (GI cause, acute porphyria)

1

- Is there any diarrhea? Ask about the characteristics of the diarrhea (GI infection, GI inflammation, some surgical causes, food intolerance, food allergy).
- Nausea (GI infection, GI inflammation, food intolerance, food allergy, motion sickness, vestibular neuritis, uremia, pregnancy, drugs, poisons, intestinal obstruction, migraine are the more common causes)
- Contact history (infection, food poisoning, other poisoning)
- Headache (raised intracranial pressure, head injury, intracranial infection, migraine, viral infections)
- Altered conscious state (hypotension, raised intracranial pressure, space occupying lesion, head injury, intracranial infection, intracranial bleed, uremia, hypoglycemia, diabetic ketoacidosis, severe dehydration, hyperammonemia, inborn error of metabolism, drugs, poisons)
- Neck stiffness, neck pain (meningitis, subarachnoid hemorrhage)
- Photophobia (meningitis, migraine)
- Neurological deficits (raised intracranial pressure, space occupying mass, hydrocephalus, intracranial bleed, head injury, intracranial infection)
- Facial swelling (anaphylaxis, allergic reaction, fluid overload due to chronic kidney disease, hypoalbuminemic state, etc.) (see Chapter 23 Edema)
- Pallor (bleeding, iron deficiency, uremia)
- Jaundice (liver failure, hepatitis, cholangitis, cholecystitis, some IEMs)
- Rash (food intolerance, food allergy, inflammatory bowel disease, drug, poison, viral infection)
- Gagging

- Related to food (behavioral, anxiety, stress, fear)

- Self-induced (bulimia)

- Failure to thrive (inadequate calories, GI cause, IEM, chronic kidney disease, renal failure, renal tubular acidosis, hyperglycemia)
- Developmental delay (may be related to the cause, e.g., IEMs, or may be the consequence of chronic vomiting causing malnutrition)
- Newborn screening results – was screening done for inborn errors of metabolism? Even if it was done, there is still a risk that this patient may have an IEM because not all conditions are screened for, and also because of possible false negative screening test.
- Previous tube feeding, previous feeding problem (behavioral cause)
- Tinnitus (viral infection, post-viral reaction, inner ear issues)
- Kidney disease (uremia)
- Polydipsia (hyperglycemia, renal disease)
- Polyphagia (hyperglycemia)
- Polyuria, nocturia (hyperglycemia, renal disease)
- Missed menstrual period (pregnant)
- Sexually active (risk for pregnancy)

○ Travel history, exposure to food with poor food hygiene, contact history (risk for infection, food poisoning)
○ Groin lump (hernia) – need to exclude obstructed or strangulated hernia

# 2. Physical Examination

• Do a head to toe examination focusing especially on the following:

○ Vital signs
○ Conscious state
○ Anterior fontanelle
○ Any pallor
○ Any jaundice
○ Hydration status
○ Neck flexion
○ Abdomen exam
○ Neurological exam (cranial nerves, long tract signs, cerebellar signs, etc.)
○ Heart
○ Lungs
○ Skin
○ Growth
○ Development
○ Mental health review

• There are many possible findings. The following list shows the more common ones and some of their interpretations:

○ Fever (viral illness with vomiting, viral GE bacterial GE, hepatitis, appendicitis, peritonitis, meningitis, encephalitis)
○ High heart rate (increased in dehydration, shock, fever, pain, anxious)
○ High respiration rate (increased in severe dehydration, shock, acidosis, respiratory distress)
○ High blood pressure (hypertensive emergency, pain, anxious, renal disease)
○ Low blood pressure (low in severe dehydration, shock)
○ Saturation (low in shock)
○ Capillary refill (prolonged in severe dehydration, shock)
○ Altered conscious state (hypotension, various causes of raised intracranial pressure, space occupying lesion, head injury, intracranial infection, intracranial bleed, uremia, hypoglycemia, diabetic ketoacidosis, severe dehydration, hyperammonemia, IEMs, drugs, poisons)
○ Weight loss (dehydration, inadequate caloric intake, diabetes mellitus, eating disorder, underlying chronic illness)
○ Failure to thrive (inadequate caloric intake, diabetes mellitus, eating disorder, underlying chronic illness)
○ Thirsty (sign of dehydration)
○ Pallor (iron deficiency, renal failure, chronic illness)
○ Sallow (renal failure)

- Jaundice (hepatitis, cholangitis, cholecystitis, some bacterial infections)
- Evidence of fluid overload (renal failure)
- Evidence of dehydration (see Chapter 19)
- Facial swelling (allergy, fluid overload)
- Neck stiffness, neck pain (meningitis, subarachnoid bleed)
- Abdomen

- Guarding and rebound tenderness suggest a surgical abdomen, peritonitis
- Tenderness only suggests inflammation and surgical abdomen.
- Enlarged liver: hepatitis, cholangitis, abscess
- Mass suggests surgical abdomen or a gravid uterus
- Hyperactive bowel sounds = increased motility (infection, luminal obstruction)
- Sluggish bowel sounds and absent bowel sounds = ileus (infection, peritonitis, drugs, poisons)

- Groin lump (obstructed hernia)

- Abnormal neurological signs (hypotension, various causes of raised intracranial pressure, space occupying lesion, head injury, intracranial infection, intracranial bleed, uremia, hypoglycemia, diabetic ketoacidosis, severe dehydration, hyperammonemia, IEM, drugs, poisons, vestibular neuritis)
- Signs of upper respiratory tract infection (post-tussive vomiting)
- Lungs (lower respiratory tract infection, aspiration)
- Skin rash (viral infection, bacterial infection, allergy)
- Injuries (head injuries, other injuries) (see Chapter 37)
- Development delay (IEM, space occupying lesion in the brain)

### 3. Algorithm

- The approach to vomiting is very complex as there are many differential diagnoses
- A very good history and a through physical examination is needed to get as much information as possible
- Based on the many pieces of information, one would then better be able to narrow down to the more appropriate differential diagnosis

LOOK OUT FOR RED FLAG SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS

- Blood in the vomitus or stools
- Bilious vomiting
- Peritonitis
- Moderate or severe dehydration
- Altered conscious state
- Raised intracranial pressure
- Neck stiffness
- Neurological deficit
- Failure to thrive

# CLINICAL CASE SCENARIO

A 2-year-old girl presented with a history of vomiting over the last month. The parents reported that it was a forceful involuntary expulsion of gastric juice or food that she had eaten. These episodes started a month ago. Initially, it would happen in the morning, soon after she woke up. Over time it became more frequent during the day. The vomitus was not bilious or bloody. There was no obvious relationship to a particular type of food or volume of food. There was no obvious sign or symptom of abdominal discomfort or pain. She had diarrhea for several days 2 weeks ago. Her stools were currently normal. Her parents reported that she had become more irritable and cranky over the last month. She would get unhappy easily and cry. She would also frequently hit her head when she was crying. Developmentally, she was making good progress and met all her milestones. There had not been any regression.

On examination, her weight, height, and head circumference were at the 25th percentile. Her temperature was 36.8°C, blood pressure was 122/80 mmHg, heart rate was 93/min, and respiratory rate was 23/min. She was alert but had a lot of stranger anxiety and did not like being touched. Her eye movements appeared full. There were no abnormal eye movements or eye positions. There was no pallor or jaundice. Her heart and lung examination was normal. The abdominal examination was normal. Her tone was increased in the lower limbs. There was hyperreflexia at the knee and ankle jerks. Her power was 5/5. She was well hydrated.

# SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

- What is the clinical problem?
- What is your diagnosis?

- List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.
- List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.
- If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

- What are your alternative diagnoses, if your initial diagnosis is incorrect?
- For each of these alternative diagnoses:

- List the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.
- List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.
- If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

- After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?
- Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?
- What is your plan for this patient?

# ANSWERS TO SELF-REFLECTION QUESTIONS

• What is the clinical problem?

o Vomiting

• What is your diagnosis?

o Raised intracranial pressure

• List the findings in this case that support this diagnosis.

o Vomiting after waking up from a sleep.

o Child hitting head may be suggestive of headache.

o Change in behavior may be suggestive of intracranial pathology.

o High blood pressure reading for her age.

o Increased tone and reflexes in the lower limbs.

• List the findings in this case that go against this diagnosis.

o The presence of diarrhea 2 weeks ago

• If this diagnosis is correct, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present or not provided in this case.

o Whether or not papilledema was present.

o No description of her posture. In severe raised intracranial pressure, there may be decerebrate posturing.

o Examination of her gait.

o Developmental assessment.

o Not enough information available to determine the cause of the raised intracranial pressure, e.g., is it a space-occupying lesion? Is it due to hydrocephalus?

• For each of these alternative diagnoses, list the findings in the case that support this alternative diagnosis.

o There is no good alternative diagnosis.

• List the findings in the case that go against this alternative diagnosis.

o None

• If this alternative diagnosis is true, list the findings that are expected to be present but are currently not present in this case.

o None

• After doing this reflection, what do you think is the MOST likely diagnosis?

o Raised intracranial pressure

• Is there any complication? Is there any red flag symptom?

o Raised intracranial pressure that is getting progressively worse

• What is your plan for this patient?

o Admit the patient immediately.

o Escalate to a senior doctor.

o Place the child on "nil by mouth" (NBM) with view to urgent head imaging.

o Obtain intravenous access.

o Get an urgent CT scan brain or urgent MRI brain.

o Child may need sedation/general anesthesia for the scan. To escalate to the sedation team or to an anesthetist.

# Pediatric Differential Diagnosis
## Top 50 Problems

Editors: Denise LM Goh • Elizabeth Y Ang

Using symptom-based and problem-based approaches, this indispensable text teaches medical students how to apply critical thinking and clinical reasoning to develop diagnostic competence. It is also suitable for any doctor who wishes to refresh his/her clinical approaches to pediatric patients.

With 50 carefully selected pediatric problems that are likely to be encountered in medical practice, each chapter is written in a user-friendly style and takes you through the fundamental thought process needed to arrive at the most likely diagnosis. This thought process includes:

- Understanding the correct definition of the clinical problem
- Recognizing the symptoms of the clinical problem
- Analyzing the pathophysiology behind the problem
- Creating a prioritized list of differential diagnosis
- Having a robust clinical approach/algorithm that enables one to arrive at the correct diagnosis
- Evaluating for red flag signs and symptoms
- Honing the thought process through the use of over 50 clinical case scenarios with structured self-reflection questions and answers

Foreword by Dr Naveen Thacker, President of Asia Pacific Pediatric Association
Peer reviewed by members of Asia Pacific Pediatric Association
